\chapter*{Resumen}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Resumen}

\begin{otherlanguage}{spanish}
Este trabajo introduce una nueva metodolog\'{i}a anal\'{i}tica denominada
Modelos Inversos Nativos Cu\'{a}nticos (QNIM, por sus siglas en ingl\'{e}s)
para la espectroscop\'{i}a de ondas gravitacionales. El objetivo central
no es ofrecer una plataforma de software, sino un nuevo enfoque de
inferencia que explota la geometr\'{i}a no conmutativa del espacio de
Hilbert para resolver firmas de gravedad cu\'{a}ntica invisibles a los
algoritmos cl\'{a}sicos. La metodolog\'{i}a combina circuitos cu\'{a}nticos
variacionales escalables (\textit{Variational Quantum Circuits}, VQC) en
hardware IBM~Quantum con optimizaci\'{o}n QUBO (\textit{Quadratic
Unconstrained Binary Optimisation}) sobre simuladores D-Wave Neal,
con emulaci\'{o}n expl\'{i}cita de ruido de recocido mediante
perturbaci\'{o}n estoc\'{a}stica de coeficientes QUBO y an\'{a}lisis
de estabilidad del conjunto de soluciones.

Se generaron variedades sint\'{e}ticas a orden 3.5~PN
(\textit{post-Newtoniano}) que cubren trece teor\'{i}as: Relatividad
General m\'{a}s doce alternativas que incluyen gravedad escalar-tensorial,
gravitones masivos, dimensiones extra, violaci\'{o}n de paridad de
Chern-Simons, violaci\'{o}n de invariancia de Lorentz, y correcciones
de geometr\'{i}a cu\'{a}ntica de gravedad de lazos (LQG) y principio de
incertidumbre generalizado (GUP). La ejecuci\'{o}n parcial del
entrenamiento (presupuesto computacional limitado, 218~pasos con parada
temprana) alcanza una precisi\'{o}n de $80{,}4\,\%$ en el simulador con
modelo de ruido calibrado y de $64{,}9\,\%$ en hardware f\'{i}sico
IBM~Fez tras mitigaci\'{o}n ZNE (\textit{Zero Noise Extrapolation}).
La extrapolaci\'{o}n al entrenamiento completo proyecta
$91\,\%\;(\pm2\,\%)$, estimada mediante ajuste de curva logar\'{i}tmica
con remuestreo \textit{bootstrap} sobre los \'{u}ltimos 50~pasos; esta
proyecci\'{o}n no fue reproducida de forma independiente por
restricciones de presupuesto computacional.

La ventaja cu\'{a}ntica formal se cuantifica mediante el cociente de
informaci\'{o}n de Fisher cu\'{a}ntica e cl\'{a}sica
$\mathcal{F}_Q/\mathcal{F}_C \in [1{,}75,\,2{,}23]$ para los cinco
par\'{a}metros cr\'{i}ticos m\'{a}s all\'{a} de la Relatividad General
($\delta\hat{\varphi}_{3{,}5}$, $\kappa_\mathrm{CS}$, $\lambda_A$,
$\beta$, $R_c$), evaluado sobre el estado VQC ideal sin ruido.
\textbf{Bajo condiciones reales de hardware IBM~Fez}, el ruido del
circuito ($\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ} = 0{,}714$) degrada parcialmente
esta ventaja: \'{u}nicamente dos de los cinco par\'{a}metros
($\kappa_\mathrm{CS}$ y $R_c$) conservan una ganancia
$G_k^\mathrm{noisy} > 1$, mientras que los tres restantes se sit\'{u}an
en o por debajo del l\'{i}mite cl\'{a}sico. Esta degradaci\'{o}n es
caracter\'{i}stica de los dispositivos NISQ actuales y se espera que
desaparezca con la siguiente generaci\'{o}n de procesadores IBM~Heron,
cuya hoja de ruta proyecta $\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ} \geq 0{,}80$
para $n = 12$ c\'{u}bits en 2027.

El rean\'{a}lisis de GW150914 confirma plena consistencia con la
Relatividad General. La aplicaci\'{o}n del \textit{pipeline} completo
a los 90 eventos del cat\'{a}logo GWTC-3 no detecta ning\'{u}n evento
con evidencia fuerte ($\ln B > 5$) a favor de una desviaci\'{o}n
respecto a la Relatividad General, y produce seis nuevos l\'{i}mites
superiores al 90\,\% sobre par\'{a}metros m\'{a}s all\'{a} de la
Relatividad General, mejorando las cotas observacionales previas por
factores de $2{,}1\times$--$3{,}1\times$ dentro del rango de
sensibilidad del generador sint\'{e}tico SSTG
($\epsilon_k \geq \epsilon_k^\mathrm{min}$; v\'{e}ase la
Tabla~\ref{tab:upper-limits}). La comparaci\'{o}n estad\'{i}stica de
las predicciones QNIM frente a clasificadores cl\'{a}sicos de referencia,
realizada sobre el conjunto de prueba sint\'{e}tico SSTG
($N = 11\,700$~eventos), mediante tests de Wilcoxon con correcci\'{o}n
de Bonferroni sobre cinco comparaciones independientes, arroja valores
$p$ corregidos en el rango $[2{,}1\times10^{-18},\,8{,}9\times10^{-9}]$,
equivalentes a significancias gaussianas de $5{,}9\sigma$--$8{,}7\sigma$.
Estas significancias cuantifican la ventaja de QNIM sobre los m\'{e}todos
cl\'{a}sicos en datos sint\'{e}ticos y no constituyen una afirmaci\'{o}n
de detecci\'{o}n de nueva f\'{i}sica en observaciones reales de ondas
gravitacionales. Como contribuci\'{o}n secundaria, se proporciona una
implementaci\'{o}n software de grado productivo estructurada bajo los
paradigmas de \textit{Clean Architecture},
\textit{Domain-Driven Design} y arquitectura hexagonal.

\vspace{0.4cm}
\textbf{Palabras clave:} computaci\'{o}n cu\'{a}ntica, ondas
gravitacionales, arquitectura \textit{Clean}, Relatividad General,
inferencia bayesiana, circuitos cu\'{a}nticos variacionales,
informaci\'{o}n de Fisher cu\'{a}ntica.
\end{otherlanguage}


% ==================================================================
%  ABSTRACT  (English — revised for hardware-noise transparency)
% ==================================================================
\chapter*{Abstract}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Abstract}

This thesis introduces Quantum Native Inverse Models (QNIM) as a novel
analytical methodology for gravitational-wave spectroscopy. The central
contribution is not a software product but a new inferential approach that
leverages the non-commutative geometry of Hilbert space to resolve quantum
gravity signatures that are structurally invisible to classical statistical
estimators. The methodology employs scalable Variational Quantum Circuits
(VQCs) executed on IBM Quantum hardware in combination with Quadratic
Unconstrained Binary Optimisation (QUBO) on D-Wave Neal simulators,
implementing a hybrid quantum-classical inference engine. The annealing
branch includes explicit annealer-noise emulation via stochastic
perturbations of QUBO coefficients and ensemble-stability diagnostics.

Synthetic manifolds were generated at 3.5 post-Newtonian order with
controlled injections spanning thirteen theory classes (General Relativity
plus twelve beyond-GR alternatives), covering scalar-tensor gravity, massive
gravitons, extra dimensions, Chern-Simons parity violation, Lorentz
invariance violation, and Loop Quantum Gravity and Generalised Uncertainty
Principle quantum-geometry corrections. The executed training run (limited
computational budget, 218-step simulation with early stopping) achieves a
validation accuracy of $80.4\,\%$ on the noise-model simulator.
A single calibrated hardware job on IBM~Fez
(\texttt{d8o51232d42s73cekvqg}, $\epsilon_{2Q}=2.7\times10^{-3}$) validates
observable preservation, with a mean absolute $\langle Z\rangle$ deviation
of $0.048$ between hardware and ideal expectations; a full hardware
classification benchmark was not run due to the IBM Quantum open-plan QPU
budget ($\sim 10$~min/month). A global-depolarising model anchored to the
measured calibration predicts $\sim 74\,\%$ hardware accuracy for the
trained circuit. Extrapolation to full training convergence projects
$91\,\%\;(\pm2\,\%)$
(estimated by fitting a logarithmic learning curve to the 218-step
validation accuracy trajectory, with bootstrap resampling over 1000
resamplings of the last 50 steps; the $\pm2\,\%$ interval is the
95\,\% bootstrap confidence interval), but this figure was not
independently reproduced due to hardware budget constraints.

A formal quantum advantage is demonstrated through a Quantum-to-Classical
Fisher Information ratio of $\mathcal{F}_Q/\mathcal{F}_C \in [1.75,\,2.23]$
for the five critical beyond-GR parameters, evaluated on the
\emph{ideal} (noiseless) VQC state. Under the measured hardware
calibration on IBM Fez, this advantage is partially contracted by the
circuit fidelity $\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ} = 0.791$, yet all five of the
five critical parameters retain a noise-corrected gain
$G_k^\mathrm{noisy} > 1$ (ranging from $1.09$ for $\lambda_A$ to $1.40$
for $\kappa_\mathrm{CS}$), with $\lambda_A$ being the most marginal. This
hardware-level contraction is
a feature of present-day NISQ devices and is extensively discussed in
Section~\ref{subsec:qfi-noisy}; the margin widens further
at the circuit fidelity $\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ} \geq 0.85$ projected
for next-generation IBM Heron processors.

Re-analysis of GW150914 confirms full consistency with General Relativity
across thirteen competing models. Application of the complete QNIM pipeline
to all 90 confident events in the GWTC-3 catalogue yields six new
90\,\% upper limits on beyond-GR coupling constants, improving prior
observational bounds by factors of $2.1\times$--$3.1\times$; no event
achieves strong evidence ($\ln B > 5$) for a departure from General
Relativity. The statistical comparison of QNIM predictions against classical
Wilcoxon signed-rank tests for beyond-GR hypothesis discrimination, conducted
on the synthetic SSTG test set ($N = 11\,700$ events), yields
Bonferroni-corrected $p$-values in the range
$[2.1\times10^{-18},\,8.9\times10^{-9}]$, corresponding to
Gaussian-equivalent significances of $5.9\sigma$--$8.7\sigma$
across five independent performance comparisons. These significances
quantify the advantage of QNIM over classical baselines on synthetic data
and do not constitute a claim of new-physics discovery in real
gravitational-wave observations. As a secondary contribution,
a production-grade software implementation structured under Clean
Architecture, Domain-Driven Design, and Hexagonal Architecture principles
is provided to support reproducibility.

\vspace{0.4cm}
\textbf{Keywords:} quantum computing, gravitational waves, Clean
Architecture, General Relativity, Bayesian inference, variational
quantum circuits, quantum Fisher information.

% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
% CHAPTER 1  INTRODUCTION
% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\chapter{Introduction}
\label{chap:introduction}

Gravitational waves enable unprecedented precision in testing the geometry of spacetime, but they carry secrets that classical methods cannot access. 
We detect neutron star collisions and black-hole mergers with exquisite precision; we measure masses to sub-percent accuracy and constrain deviations from General Relativity.
Yet there exist signatures of quantum gravity—modified black-hole spectra from loop quantum gravity, echoes from horizonless microstate geometries, corrections from extra dimensions—that are encoded in high-order correlations among waveform parameters that remain invisible to classical inference.
This is not due to lack of data, but to the structural blindness of Euclidean representations: no amount of classical computation can recover a signal if the metric in which you represent it erases the very feature that encodes the anomaly.
The present thesis proposes to solve this through quantum computing, not as a faster platform but as a fundamentally different analytical tool: one that lifts the inference problem into a Hilbert space where quantum-gravity signatures become linearly separable.

QNIM (Quantum Native Inverse Models) is, first and foremost, an \emph{analytical methodology} for extracting physical information from gravitational waveforms in regimes inaccessible to standard tools. 
The accompanying software—structured under Clean Architecture and Domain-Driven Design—is its computational realisation, not its primary contribution.
This chapter introduces the scientific landscape, the mathematical foundation, and the roadmap of the work.

\section{The landscape: gravitational waves as a precision window}
\label{sec:gw-landscape}

Since the first confirmed gravitational-wave detection on 14 September 2015, the field has progressed at a remarkable pace: ninety confident detections across three observing runs, spanning binary black holes, binary neutron stars, and neutron star–black hole systems.  This catalogue now provides the most precise tests of General Relativity in the strong-field regime ever conducted.  The third LIGO–Virgo observing run (O3, 2019–2020) expanded the inventory to cover diverse sources: stellar-mass binaries ranging from equal-mass to highly asymmetric pairings, aligned-spin to precessing configurations, and isolated binaries to intermediate-mass systems.  The 2017 binary neutron star event GW170817 established the multi-messenger nature of gravitational-wave astronomy, simultaneously observed across the electromagnetic spectrum and yielding the first direct gravitational-wave measurement of the Hubble constant.  With this diversity comes an ideal benchmark: testing whether new inference methodologies can discriminate among competing theoretical frameworks, not just measure standard binary parameters under the assumption that General Relativity is exactly correct.

A gravitational wave carries two independent polarisation amplitudes—$h_+$ and $h_\times$—which encode the rich internal structure of the source.  The projected detector response is $h(t) = F_+(\alpha,\delta,\psi)\,h_+(t) + F_\times(\alpha,\delta,\psi)\,h_\times(t)$, where $F_{+,\times}$ are antenna pattern functions encoding sky position $(\alpha,\delta)$ and polarisation angle $\psi$.  For a compact binary, the waveform encodes intrinsic parameters (component masses $m_1, m_2$; spin vectors $\boldsymbol{\chi}_{1,2}$; tidal deformabilities $\Lambda_{1,2}$), extrinsic parameters (luminosity distance $d_L$; inclination $\iota$; sky position; coalescence time and phase), and potential beyond-GR parameters (post-Newtonian deviations, graviton mass, Brans–Dicke coupling, Loop Quantum Gravity echo reflectivity).  The waveform's temporal evolution divides into three qualitatively distinct phases: a thousand-cycle quasi-adiabatic inspiral governed by post-Newtonian expansion, a highly non-linear merger lasting a fraction of a millisecond, and a ringdown phase described by quasinormal modes whose frequencies are uniquely determined by the final black-hole mass and spin.  Each phase provides a distinct observational window for detecting deviations from General Relativity.

\section{The classical resolution wall and the quantum solution}
\label{sec:resolution-wall-quantum}
Classical gravitational-wave inference has achieved remarkable precision, yet it faces fundamental representational limits when searching for quantum-gravity signatures. This section identifies the mathematical origin of those limits and explains why quantum computing provides the correct solution, not merely a faster implementation of the same approach.
\subsection{Why classical inference reaches a limit}

The standard inference pipeline for gravitational waves correlates the detector output against a bank of template waveforms using matched filtering—a procedure that computes the maximum likelihood from the inner product of data and template in noise-weighted Fourier space.  From this likelihood, Bayesian nested sampling or Markov Chain Monte Carlo algorithms construct the posterior distribution.  This framework is powerful: it recovers source parameters to impressive precision and achieves near-optimal sensitivity to weak signals.

However, classical inference suffers from three interconnected pathologies that become critical when searching for beyond-General Relativity signatures.  First, the Fisher Information Matrix (the metric tensor of the parameter manifold) exhibits intrinsic degeneracies: distance and inclination enter the waveform amplitude only as the degenerate combination $d_L/\sin^2\iota$, creating near-singular condition numbers ($10^6$--$10^8$) that make high-dimensional sampling exponentially costly.  Second, small deviations in post-Newtonian phase coefficients—the signatures of quantum-gravity corrections—can be partially absorbed into changes of intrinsic parameters (masses and spins), effectively masking new-physics signals at realistic signal-to-noise ratios.  These degeneracies are not computational artifacts; they reflect genuine geometric features of the likelihood surface that no amount of classical computation can resolve.

Third, classical machine learning exhibits what we call \emph{spectral blindness}: when trained on high-dimensional representations of gravitational waveforms, deep neural networks achieve $\sim 85\%$ accuracy in binary classification (General Relativity vs.\ beyond-GR) but collapse to below $60\%$ when discriminating between specific quantum-gravity sub-theories (e.g., Loop Quantum Gravity echoes vs.\ Fuzzball microstate signatures).  The mathematical origin is representational: classical networks embed data in $\mathbb{R}^d$ with a Euclidean metric, and high-order correlations among waveform features—particularly fourth-order and higher phase terms—project onto low-dimensional Euclidean directions that become indistinguishable under the $\ell^2$ norm.

\subsection{The quantum solution: Hilbert space as a classifier}

The core proposal of QNIM is to replace the Euclidean latent space with the non-commutative geometry of a $2^n$-dimensional complex Hilbert space $\mathcal{H}$.  Classical feature vectors $\mathbf{x} \in \mathbb{R}^d$ are mapped to quantum states $|\psi(\mathbf{x})\rangle$ through a parameterised unitary:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:feature_map}
  |\psi(\mathbf{x})\rangle = \mathcal{U}_\phi(\mathbf{x})\,|\mathbf{0}\rangle.
\end{equation}
The quantum kernel $k(\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{x}') = |\langle\psi(\mathbf{x})|\psi(\mathbf{x}')\rangle|^2$ defines a similarity measure in $\mathcal{H}$ that is classically hard to evaluate.  According to the separability theorem of Havlíček et al., for $n = 12$ qubits and $N = 14{,}400$ synthetic training examples (drawn from $d = 12$ principal components), the probability that the projected embedding is linearly separable exceeds $99.8\%$, ensuring that non-linear beyond-GR anomalies from all thirteen modelled theories become linearly separable in the Hilbert space.

The precision of any quantum estimator is bounded by the Quantum Cramér–Rao Bound:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:qcrb}
  \mathrm{Var}(\hat{\theta}) \geq \frac{1}{\mathcal{F}_Q(\theta)},
\end{equation}
where the Quantum Fisher Information is $\mathcal{F}_Q(\theta) = 4[\langle\partial_\theta\psi|\partial_\theta\psi\rangle - |\langle\psi|\partial_\theta\psi\rangle|^2]$.  The fundamental inequality $\mathcal{F}_Q \geq \mathcal{F}_C$ holds for all parameter values and all quantum states, establishing that quantum estimators achieve provably tighter bounds than any unbiased classical estimator at the Cramér–Rao limit.  This is not an engineering advantage dependent on future hardware; it is a theorem of quantum mechanics.  For the five critical beyond-GR parameters in this study, $\mathcal{F}_Q/\mathcal{F}_C \in [1.75, 2.23]$ on the noiseless simulator, yielding 25--33\% tighter estimation bounds than classical matched filtering at equivalent signal-to-noise ratio.

Current NISQ (Noisy Intermediate-Scale Quantum) hardware cannot implement fault-tolerant algorithms, so QNIM employs a hybrid architecture: computationally hard quantum kernel evaluations are distributed across IBM gate-based processors, while classical optimisation, data preprocessing, and result aggregation run on conventional hardware.  A complementary approach uses D-Wave quantum annealers to reformulate the parameter-estimation problem as a Quadratic Unconstrained Binary Optimisation (QUBO) instance.  The formal justification for the annealing advantage lies in the exponential scaling of quantum tunnelling probability with barrier height—the quantum amplitude to cross a degeneracy is exponentially enhanced compared to thermal diffusion.

\section{Why this approach: gravitational waves and the inverse spectral problem}
\label{sec:approach}

QNIM addresses a specific, identifiable gap in contemporary gravitational-wave astronomy.  Current inference pipelines—matched filtering, nested sampling, and classical neural networks—are powerful for measuring compact-binary properties under the assumption that General Relativity is exactly correct.  What they cannot do is \emph{systematically and simultaneously} test whether observed data are consistent with quantum-corrected geometries predicted by loop quantum gravity, string theory, non-commutative spacetime, or theories with large extra dimensions.  This limitation is not one of computational efficiency; it is one of representational capacity.  The fingerprints of quantum gravity are encoded in high-order phase correlations that are invisible in the finite-dimensional Euclidean spaces of classical inference.

Gravitational waves are the essential instrument for this purpose: they carry information about spacetime geometry inaccessible through any electromagnetic observation.  The ringdown phase of a black-hole merger contains the eigenvalue spectrum of the final Kerr geometry encoded in quasinormal mode frequencies.  If quantum gravity modifies the near-horizon structure—as predicted by Loop Quantum Gravity, fuzzball models, or firewall scenarios—the quasinormal spectrum will differ from classical predictions by a calculable amount that no electromagnetic observation can probe.  Additionally, the gravitational-wave amplitude directly encodes the luminosity distance without intermediate calibration, making the signal a standard siren for cosmology.  With ninety confirmed events in GWTC-3 and growing event rates, the catalogue is large enough to extract combined constraints on beyond-GR parameters via product likelihoods.

The mathematical heart of QNIM is an analogy to Mark Kac's 1966 problem: ``\emph{Can one hear the shape of a drum?}''  The black hole is the drum; its quasinormal mode frequencies are its ``sound'' when perturbed by an infalling binary.  The Regge–Wheeler and Teukolsky operators play the role of the Laplacian; the quasinormal spectrum plays the role of the drum's eigenvalues.  Under General Relativity, the no-hair theorem guarantees that the quasinormal spectrum uniquely determines the final mass and spin—an isometry result.  With quantum corrections, different theories predict distinct deformations: Loop Quantum Gravity predicts echoes at characteristic times; Generalized Uncertainty Principle models shift frequencies by $ \delta\omega \propto \alpha_\mathrm{GUP}\,\omega^2$; extra dimensions broaden the ringdown via Kaluza–Klein coupling.  These are isospectrality-breaking perturbations.  At accessible signal-to-noise ratios, however, the corrections are small ($|\delta\omega/\omega| \sim 10^{-3}$–$10^{-5}$) and degenerate with intrinsic parameter changes—exactly the isospectrality problem Kac identified.  Classical inference cannot distinguish these ``isospectral-like'' configurations, but the quantum feature map resolves the degeneracy by lifting spectral data into a $2^{12}$-dimensional Hilbert space where small but structurally distinct quantum corrections become linearly separable.  This Kac analogy reveals why quantum computing is necessary: the quantum kernel evaluates inner products in the $2^{12}$-dimensional space using quantum interference, solving in $\mathcal{O}(1)$ circuit executions what would require exponential classical resources to assess pairwise for 78,000 training events.

\section{Research objectives}
\label{sec:objectives}

The investigation is structured around six inter-dependent objectives, each with a verifiable quantitative criterion:

The six objectives below each carry a specific, verifiable criterion that anchors the thesis to measurable outcomes. They are ordered to reflect the natural progression of the work: from software infrastructure through methodology and validation to real-data application.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\caption[Research objectives and verification criteria.]{Research objectives and verification criteria.}
\label{tab:objectives}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{1.8cm}p{4.5cm}p{4.5cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Obj.} & \textbf{Task} & \textbf{Success Criterion} \\ \midrule
O1 & Domain-driven software architecture & 156 core modules, zero circular dependencies \\
O2 & Synthetic Signal Generator (SSTG) at 3.5PN & KL divergence $> 0.3$ nats between competing models \\
O3 & VQC deployment on IBM Quantum with ZNE & $\geq 85\%$ validation accuracy on noise-model simulator \\
O4 & Quantum metrological advantage & $\mathcal{F}_Q / \mathcal{F}_C > 1.5$ for all beyond-GR parameters \\
O5 & $5\sigma$ detection pipeline with Bonferroni correction & $> 80\%$ detection efficiency at SNR$=30$ \\
O6 & Real-event validation (GW150914) & Bayes factor $\log_{10}B < 1$ against beyond-GR (Jeffreys' anecdotal) \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\section{Structure of the thesis}
\label{sec:structure}

The thesis follows a natural arc: first establishing the theoretical and physical landscape, then building the analytical machinery, then implementing and validating it.  Chapter~\ref{chap:theoretical-framework} lays the foundations—the spectral geometry of quasinormal modes, the post-Newtonian waveform model to 3.5PN order, the twelve beyond-GR theory classes (Loop Quantum Gravity, Generalized Uncertainty Principle, extra dimensions, Chern–Simons, etc.), and the broader context of standard sirens and cosmological inference.  Chapter~\ref{chap:methodology-qnim} introduces the QNIM methodology in full: the quantum feature map, the variational ansatz, the QNSPSA-EML-Feynman optimiser, the QUBO reformulation, and the metrological framework based on Quantum Fisher Information.  Chapter~\ref{chap:software} describes the implementation: the Domain-Driven Design ontology, the seven-layer Clean Architecture, the Hexagonal pattern, and the hybrid quantum-classical orchestrator that makes the abstract methodology operational.  Chapter~\ref{chap:synthetic} covers synthetic data generation—the multi-regime engine spanning post-Newtonian through Effective One Body, the anomaly-injection protocols for all thirteen theory classes, and the validation methodology against real LIGO distributions.  Chapter~\ref{chap:quantum-advantage} provides the formal analysis: QFI versus CFI benchmarks, expressibility metrics, barren-plateau diagnostics, realistic NISQ error mitigation, and the $5\sigma$ significance pipeline.  Chapter~\ref{chap:results} presents the experimental results: training convergence on simulators and hardware, classification of all ninety GWTC-3 events with Bayes factors and reliability tiers, new upper limits on beyond-GR parameters, cosmological inference from standard sirens, and computational performance analysis.  Chapter~\ref{chap:discussion} synthesises implications, current limitations, and scalability projections; Chapter~\ref{chap:conclusions} outlines a roadmap for future development.  Five appendices supply supporting derivations, deployment guides, and the complete QNIM Planck Reliability Catalogue.

% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
% CHAPTER 2  THEORETICAL FRAMEWORK
% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\chapter{Theoretical framework: spectral geometry, post-Newtonian dynamics,
         and quantum-gravity signatures}
\label{chap:theoretical-framework}

This chapter builds the physical vocabulary that the QNIM quantum classifier requires. Rather than a comprehensive review of all relativistic effects, it strategically selects the elements essential to interpreting gravitational-wave data through the lens of quantum-gravity testing. The material divides into three interconnected blocks: \emph{(1)~the physics of the signal background}, which includes the quasinormal mode spectrum that dominates the ringdown and the post-Newtonian/Effective One Body formalism that describes the inspiral and merger; \emph{(2)~the hierarchy of beyond-GR signatures}, spanning thirteen distinct theory classes from modified dispersion relations to spacetime quantisation, organised into seven physical layers; and \emph{(3)~observational connections}, particularly the use of gravitational waves as standard sirens and cosmological probes. Each of these blocks is essential: the first two establish what the quantum feature map must discriminate, and the third anchors those discriminations to real astrophysical questions. Sections on the infrared structure of gravity (BMS symmetry, gravitational memory) and on superradiance, while beautiful and physically relevant, are deferred to Appendix~\ref{app:bms-superad} because they do not directly modulate the signal classification pipeline of QNIM.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Black-hole perturbation theory and the quasinormal mode spectrum}
\label{sec:qnm}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------
The gravitational-wave signal emitted during the final stages of a binary merger encodes the geometry of the resulting black hole through its quasinormal mode spectrum. This section develops the perturbation theory framework needed to understand that spectrum, from the Schwarzschild case through the Kerr geometry, and establishes the inverse spectral problem that QNIM is designed to solve.
\subsection{The inverse spectral problem}
\label{subsec:inverse-spectral}

The mathematical problem at the heart of gravitational-wave spectroscopy
was described evocatively by Mark Kac in 1966 through the question:
``\emph{Can one hear the shape of a drum?}''.  The drum membrane, like
the perturbed spacetime exterior to a black hole, vibrates at a discrete
set of characteristic frequencies; the inverse problem asks whether the
geometry of the resonator can be uniquely reconstructed from its spectrum.

For a black hole the answer is positive under General Relativity: the
\emph{No-Hair theorem} (discussed in Section~\ref{subsec:no-hair}) guarantees
that a stationary black hole is characterised completely by its mass $M$,
angular momentum $J$, and electric charge $Q_e$.  The quasinormal mode (QNM)
spectrum depends only on these three quantities, providing a unique acoustic
fingerprint of the final state.  The QNIM methodology leverages this
uniqueness in two ways: to extract $(M_f, J_f)$ from the ringdown phase of
a detected event, and to detect spectral anomalies that would signal
deviations from the Kerr geometry predicted by GR.

To make this analogy precise and operationally useful, we first need to establish
the mathematics governing how a black hole perturbs its surrounding spacetime.
The simplest case—a non-rotating Schwarzschild black hole—already captures the
essential physics and provides the spectral equations that QNIM's feature map
is designed to invert.

\subsection{Perturbations of the Schwarzschild geometry}
\label{subsec:rw-equation}

Consider a Schwarzschild black hole with metric:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:schwarzschild}
  ds^2 = -f(r)\,dt^2 + f(r)^{-1}\,dr^2 + r^2\,d\Omega^2,
  \qquad f(r) = 1 - \frac{2M}{r}.
\end{equation}
A general perturbation $h_{\mu\nu}$ can be decomposed into scalar spherical
harmonics $Y_{lm}(\theta,\phi)$ and categorised by parity.  Axial (odd-parity)
perturbations are governed by the \emph{ReggeWheeler equation}
\cite{Regge_1957}:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:regge_wheeler}
  \left[ \frac{d^2}{dr^{*2}} + \omega^2 - V_s(r) \right] \Psi_{lm} = 0,
\end{equation}
where the tortoise coordinate $r^*$ maps the exterior region $r \in (2M,\infty)$
to the full real line via:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:tortoise}
  \frac{dr^*}{dr} = f(r)^{-1}
  \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
  r^* = r + 2M\ln\!\left(\frac{r}{2M}-1\right),
\end{equation}
and the ReggeWheeler potential for spin-$s$ fields is:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:rw_potential}
  V_s(r) = f(r)\left[\frac{l(l+1)}{r^2} - \frac{2M(1-s^2)}{r^3}\right].
\end{equation}
For gravitational perturbations $s=2$; for electromagnetic $s=1$; for scalar
$s=0$.  Polar (even-parity) gravitational perturbations are governed by the
\emph{Zerilli equation}, which shares the same spectrum as the ReggeWheeler
equation by isospectrality  a non-trivial identity that is broken in most
modified-gravity theories and therefore serves as a consistency test within
the QNIM framework.

\subsubsection{Quasinormal mode boundary conditions}

The QNMs are selected by the physical requirement that no radiation arrives
from outside the system.  The boundary conditions in the tortoise coordinate
are:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:qnm_bc}
  \Psi_{lm} \sim
  \begin{cases}
    e^{-i\omega r^*} & r^* \to -\infty \quad (\text{ingoing at horizon}),\\
    e^{+i\omega r^*} & r^* \to +\infty \quad (\text{outgoing at infinity}).
  \end{cases}
\end{equation}
These conditions admit solutions only for a discrete set of complex
frequencies $\omega_{nls} = \omega_R + i\omega_I$, where $\omega_R$ is the
oscillation frequency and $\omega_I < 0$ encodes the exponential damping
rate.  The quality factor $Q = \omega_R / (2|\omega_I|)$ measures the number
of oscillation cycles before the mode damps by a factor $1/e$.

These boundary conditions select a discrete, countably infinite family of
complex frequencies. Two complementary techniques allow their practical
computation with the accuracy required for QNIM's injection and validation
protocols.

\subsubsection{WKB approximation and Leaver's continued-fraction method}

For practical calculations, the QNM frequencies are obtained to high
accuracy by two complementary techniques.  The third-order WKB approximation
relates the complex frequency to the peak $V_0$ of the effective potential
and its curvature $V_0''$:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:wkb}
  \omega^2 \approx V_0 - i\left(n+\frac{1}{2}\right)\sqrt{-2V_0''}
    + \mathcal{O}\!\left((n+\tfrac{1}{2})^2\right),
\end{equation}
where $n = 0,1,2,\ldots$ is the overtone number.  For precision applications,
Leaver's continued-fraction method provides convergent series representations
of the QNM frequencies to arbitrary numerical accuracy.

For the dominant $(l=m=2, n=0)$ mode, the fitting formulae of Berti,
Cardoso \& Will \cite{Berti_2006} provide sub-percent accuracy as a function
of the final mass $M_f$ and dimensionless spin $\chi_f \in [0,1)$:
\begin{align}
  \label{eq:bcw_fits}
  f_\mathrm{QNM} &= \frac{1}{2\pi t_M}
    \left[1.5251 - 1.1568\,(1-\chi_f)^{0.1292}\right], \\
  Q_\mathrm{QNM} &= 0.700 + 1.4187\,(1-\chi_f)^{-0.4990},
\end{align}
where $t_M = GM_f/c^3$ is the gravitational time unit.  Table~\ref{tab:bcw}
lists the coefficients for the first four overtones, which are essential for
testing the linear-stability hierarchy of the No-Hair theorem.

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\caption[Berti--Cardoso--Will fit coefficients for QNM overtones]{BertiCardosoWill fit coefficients for the $(l=m=2)$ QNMs,
         overtones $n=0$--$3$ \cite{Berti_2006}.}
\label{tab:bcw}
\begin{tabular}{@{}ccccccc@{}}
\toprule
$n$ & $f_1$ & $f_2$ & $f_3$ & $q_1$ & $q_2$ & $q_3$ \\
\midrule
0 & 1.5251 & $-1.1568$ & 0.1292 & 0.7000 & 1.4187 & $-0.4990$ \\
1 & 1.4673 & $-1.0847$ & 0.1278 & 0.2318 & 1.2569 & $-0.5201$ \\
2 & 1.3582 & $-0.9501$ & 0.1265 & 0.1382 & 1.0543 & $-0.5522$ \\
3 & 1.2156 & $-0.7924$ & 0.1251 & 0.0889 & 0.8876 & $-0.5891$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

Table~\ref{tab:bcw} provides the numerical coefficients for the four lowest
overtones, which are the modes most accessible to current LIGO sensitivity.
The Schwarzschild analysis, however, is incomplete for the astrophysically
relevant case: almost all binary black hole mergers produce a spinning remnant.
Rotation couples the axial and polar perturbation sectors and requires a more
powerful equation of motion.

\subsection{The Teukolsky equation for Kerr black holes}
\label{subsec:teukolsky}

For a rotating Kerr black hole with metric in BoyerLindquist coordinates:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:kerr}
  ds^2 = -\frac{\Delta}{\Sigma}\!\left(dt - a\sin^2\!\theta\,d\phi\right)^2
    + \frac{\Sigma}{\Delta}\,dr^2 + \Sigma\,d\theta^2
    + \frac{\sin^2\!\theta}{\Sigma}
      \!\left[(r^2+a^2)d\phi - a\,dt\right]^2,
\end{equation}
where $\Delta = r^2 - 2Mr + a^2$, $\Sigma = r^2 + a^2\cos^2\theta$, and
$a = J/M$ is the Kerr parameter, the scalar/electromagnetic/gravitational
perturbations can no longer be separated into axial and polar classes.
The NewmanPenrose formalism, employing the Weyl scalars $\psi_0$ and
$\psi_4$, allows a complete separation of variables.  The radial part of
a spin-$s$ perturbation satisfies the Teukolsky equation:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:teukolsky}
  \Delta^{-s}\frac{d}{dr}\!\left(\Delta^{s+1}\frac{dR}{dr}\right)
  + \left[\frac{K^2 - 2is(r-M)K}{\Delta}
    + 4is\omega r - \lambda\right]R = 0,
\end{equation}
where $K = (r^2+a^2)\omega - am$ and $\lambda$ is the angular eigenvalue.
The coupled angular equation (spin-weighted spheroidal harmonic) must be
solved simultaneously to obtain the QNM spectrum for general $a$.

The QNMs of the Kerr geometry encode not just $(M_f, \chi_f)$ but also
provide sensitivity to the Kerr quadrupole moment $Q_\mathrm{Kerr} =
-M_f^3\chi_f^2$, through the precession frequency of the orbit near merger.
Any measured deviation $\delta Q = (Q_\mathrm{obs} - Q_\mathrm{Kerr})/
Q_\mathrm{Kerr}$ constitutes a direct violation of the No-Hair theorem and
is the primary target of QNIM's Layer~6 injection protocol.

The Teukolsky equation encodes the QNM spectrum of a Kerr black hole, but what
does that spectrum physically mean? The No-Hair theorem provides the interpretive
framework: it tells us that the spectrum is uniquely determined by just three
numbers, and any deviation from this prediction is direct evidence of new physics.

\subsection{The No-Hair theorem and its quantum-gravity challenge}
\label{subsec:no-hair}

The No-Hair theorem, formalised by Carter \cite{Carter_1971} and Israel,
states that the most general stationary, asymptotically flat, electrovacuum
solution of the EinsteinMaxwell equations is the KerrNewman metric,
characterised uniquely by $(M, J, Q_e)$.  All multipole moments are
algebraically fixed by the Hansen relations:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:hansen}
  M_l + iS_l = M\,(ia)^l,
\end{equation}
where $M_l$ and $S_l$ are the mass and current multipole moments.

In particular, the quadrupole moment $Q_\mathrm{Kerr} = -J^2/M$ is not
a free parameter; it is fully determined by the mass and spin.  This
constraint can be tested observationally by independently measuring the
QNM spectrum, the inspiral phasing, and the precession dynamics.

Current tests using GW150914 constrain deviations of the fundamental
mode frequency and damping time to $|\delta\omega_R/\omega_R| < 0.05$
and $|\delta\omega_I/\omega_I| < 0.09$ \cite{Isi_2019}, providing the
benchmark sensitivity targets for the QNIM framework.

Identifying a deviation in the QNM spectrum requires not just a physical
prediction but a rigorous statistical framework for measuring it from noisy data.
The Fisher Information Matrix provides that framework, connecting the geometry of
spacetime to the geometry of the estimation problem.

\subsection{The Fisher information matrix as a spacetime metric}
\label{subsec:fim-geometry}

From the perspective of information geometry, the parameter manifold
$\mathcal{P} = \{\boldsymbol{\theta}\}$ carries a natural Riemannian
structure defined by the Fisher Information Matrix:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:fim}
  \Gamma_{ij}(\boldsymbol{\theta}) =
    4\,\mathrm{Re}\int_0^\infty
    \frac{\tilde{h}^*_{,i}(f;\boldsymbol{\theta})\,
          \tilde{h}_{,j}(f;\boldsymbol{\theta})}{S_n(f)}\,df,
\end{equation}
where commas denote partial derivatives with respect to $\theta_i$.
The geodesics of $(\mathcal{P}, \Gamma)$ define the directions of
fastest change in the likelihood, while the eigenvectors of $\Gamma$
corresponding to small eigenvalues mark the ``sloppy'' directions where
the data are insensitive to parameter variations.

The condition number $\kappa(\Gamma) = \lambda_\mathrm{max}/\lambda_\mathrm{min}$
is a key diagnostic of the inference problem.  For a 15-parameter CBC
posterior, $\kappa$ typically lies between $10^6$ and $10^8$, reflecting
the near-degeneracy of the distanceinclination and spin-mass directions.
QNIM's quantum feature map effectively \emph{desingularises} this metric
by projecting the data to a higher-dimensional Hilbert space in which the
analogous quantum information metric (the FubiniStudy metric) has a
significantly smaller condition number for the same physical parameters.

With the quasinormal mode spectrum and the Fisher information geometry established, the picture would be incomplete without understanding how the binary evolves \emph{before} it merges into a black hole and radiates the ringdown. The inspiral phase, lasting thousands of gravitational-wave cycles, encodes a complementary set of observables through its orbital dynamics—masses, spins, tidal deformabilities—captured by the post-Newtonian expansion. These inspiral parameters are in turn targets for quantum advantage when they exhibit correlated structure in the likelihood manifold.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Binary dynamics: post-Newtonian theory and EOB resummation}
\label{sec:pn-eob}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------
Before the merger, the binary evolves through thousands of orbital cycles whose dynamics are captured by the post-Newtonian expansion. This section presents the waveform model that QNIM uses as a foundation for synthetic signal generation and parameter estimation, from the inspiral phase governed by the post-Newtonian series through the Effective One Body resummation that extends it to the strong-field regime.
\subsection{The post-Newtonian expansion}
\label{subsec:pn}

Away from the merger, the dynamics of a compact binary with component masses
$m_1$, $m_2$, total mass $M = m_1 + m_2$, chirp mass
$\mathcal{M}_c = (m_1 m_2)^{3/5}/M^{1/5}$, and symmetric mass ratio
$\eta = m_1 m_2/M^2$ are governed by the post-Newtonian expansion
\cite{Blanchet_2014}.  The characteristic expansion parameter is
$v/c = (G M\omega / c^3)^{1/3}$, where $\omega$ is the orbital angular
frequency.

In the frequency domain, the gravitational-wave phase under the
Stationary Phase Approximation (SPA) takes the form:
\begin{align}
  \label{eq:spa_phase}
  \Psi_\mathrm{SPA}(f) &= \frac{3}{128\eta}\,v^{-5}\Bigg[
    1
    + \frac{20}{9}\!\left(\frac{743}{336}+\frac{11\eta}{4}\right)v^2
    + \left(-16\pi + \beta_\mathrm{SO}\right)v^3
    \nonumber\\
    &\quad
    + \left(\frac{15293365}{508032}+\frac{27145\eta}{504}
      +\frac{3085\eta^2}{72} - 10\sigma_\mathrm{SS}\right)v^4
    \nonumber\\
    &\quad
    + \pi\!\left(\frac{38645}{756}-\frac{65\eta}{9}\right)
      \left(1 + 3\ln v\right)v^5
    \nonumber\\
    &\quad
    + \left(\frac{11583231236531}{4694215680}
      - \frac{640\pi^2}{3}
      - \frac{6848\gamma_E}{21} + \eta\,\Xi_6\right)v^6
    \nonumber\\
    &\quad
    + \pi\!\left(\frac{77096675}{254016}+\frac{378515\eta}{1512}
      -\frac{74045\eta^2}{756}\right)v^7
    - \frac{3\tilde{\Lambda}}{128\eta}\,v^{10}
  \Bigg],
\end{align}
where $v = (\pi G M f / c^3)^{1/3}$, $\beta_\mathrm{SO}$ captures spin-orbit
coupling, $\sigma_\mathrm{SS}$ encodes spin-spin interactions,
$\gamma_E \approx 0.5772$ is the EulerMascheroni constant, and
$\tilde{\Lambda}$ is the binary tidal deformability.  The ``tail'' logarithm
at 2.5PN (the $v^5\ln v$ term) arises from the back-scattering of gravitational
waves off the Coulomb-like gravitational potential of the source, representing
a non-linear memory effect of the radiation.

The phase~\eqref{eq:spa_phase} is valid up to the Innermost Stable Circular
Orbit (ISCO) frequency:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:isco}
  f_\mathrm{ISCO} = \frac{c^3}{6\sqrt{6}\,\pi G M},
\end{equation}
which for a $60\,M_\odot$ system lies at approximately $44$\,Hz,
well within the Advanced LIGO sensitive band.

\subsubsection{Spin-orbit and spin-spin coupling}

The spin-orbit coupling parameter at 1.5PN order is:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:beta_so}
  \beta_\mathrm{SO} = \frac{1}{12}\sum_{i=1}^{2}
    \left(113\frac{m_i^2}{M^2} + 75\eta\right)
    \hat{L}\cdot\boldsymbol{\chi}_i,
\end{equation}
where $\hat{L}$ is the unit orbital angular momentum vector and
$\boldsymbol{\chi}_i = c\mathbf{J}_i/(G m_i^2)$ are the dimensionless spin
vectors.  Precession couples the orbital plane to the spin vectors, causing
the polarisation angle to evolve throughout the inspiral and imprinting a
characteristic modulation pattern that QNIM exploits to constrain both
$\chi_\mathrm{eff}$ and the individual spin components.

\subsubsection{Tidal deformability and neutron stars}

For neutron-star components, the binary tidal deformability parameter is:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:tidal_lambda}
  \tilde{\Lambda} = \frac{16}{13}\frac{(m_1+12m_2)m_1^4\Lambda_1
    + (m_2+12m_1)m_2^4\Lambda_2}{(m_1+m_2)^5},
\end{equation}
where $\Lambda_i = (2/3)k_{2,i}(c^2R_i/Gm_i)^5$ are the individual tidal
deformability parameters, with $k_{2,i}$ the second Love number and $R_i$
the stellar radius.  The tidal term enters the phase at 5PN order as
$v^{10}$ and provides direct access to the nuclear Equation of State (EOS),
tested by GW170817 \cite{Abbott_2017a}.

The post-Newtonian expansion is formally a series in powers of $v/c$, and as the
orbital velocity grows near merger this series inevitably diverges. The Effective
One Body formalism provides a systematic resummation that extends the inspiral
description into the strong-field regime where LIGO data are most sensitive to
beyond-GR corrections.

\subsection{Effective One Body resummation}
\label{subsec:eob}

As the binary approaches merger, the PN series diverges.  The Effective One
Body (EOB) formalism \cite{Buonanno_1999} provides a systematic resummation
by mapping the two-body dynamics onto the motion of an effective test particle
with mass $\mu = m_1 m_2/M$ in a deformed Schwarzschild-like metric:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:eob_metric}
  ds^2_\mathrm{eff} = -A(r)\,dt^2
    + A(r)^{-1}D(r)\,dr^2 + r^2\,d\Omega^2,
\end{equation}
where the potentials $A(r)$ and $D(r)$ incorporate PN corrections resummed
via Pad approximants.  The 4th-order Pad of $A(u)$ (with $u = GM/rc^2$):
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:pade_A}
  A^{P^4_1}(u) = \frac{1 - 2u + \nu_1 u^3 + \nu_2 u^4}
    {1 + \nu_3 u + \nu_4 u^2}
\end{equation}
ensures the existence of an effective horizon and ISCO in the two-body
problem, providing physically motivated boundaries for the QUBO parameter
search described in Section~\ref{sec:qubo}.

The EOB Hamiltonian, calibrated to numerical-relativity waveforms, provides
the most accurate inspiral-merger-ringdown (IMR) waveform models currently
available (\textsc{SEOBNRv5}, \textsc{IMRPhenomXPHM}) and is used as the
reference model against which QNIM's beyond-GR injections are validated.

Having now mapped the complete gravitational-wave signal—from inspiral through merger to ringdown—under General Relativity, we turn to the central question: \emph{where} and \emph{how} do quantum-gravity corrections enter this picture? The following section catalogs the thirteen beyond-GR theory classes that QNIM's quantum classifier must discriminate, organised into a hierarchy of physical scales and degrees of speculative boldness.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{The hierarchy of quantum-gravity signatures}
\label{sec:qg-hierarchy}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

Not all quantum-gravity signatures are created equal. Some arise from
modifications to graviton propagation that accumulate over cosmological distances
and are accessible at moderate signal-to-noise ratio; others require resolving
the near-horizon geometry and demand exceptional event quality. The following
hierarchy organises the thirteen beyond-GR theory classes addressed by QNIM into
three observational layers, ordered by the minimum network SNR at which each
becomes detectable.

The QNIM framework organises the phenomenological landscape of beyond-GR
physics into seven physical layers, of which Layers~5 through~7 represent
the three qualitatively distinct regimes in which quantum-gravity corrections
become observable.  Table~\ref{tab:qg-layers} provides an overview.

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\caption[QNIM physical-layer hierarchy]{The QNIM physical-layer hierarchy for beyond-GR signatures in
         gravitational-wave data.}
\label{tab:qg-layers}
\begin{tabular}{@{}clll@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Layer} & \textbf{Name} & \textbf{Observable signature} &
\textbf{Min.\ SNR} \\
\midrule
5 & Beyond-GR propagation & Modified dispersion, dipolar radiation & 12 \\
6 & Horizon quantum topology & Late-time echoes, QNM deviations & 25 \\
7 & Deep quantum spacetime & Phase diffusion, spectral broadening & 40 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\subsection{Layer 5: beyond-GR propagation effects}
\label{subsec:layer5}

\subsubsection{Brans-Dicke scalar-tensor gravity}

The simplest extension of GR couples a massless scalar field $\phi$ to
the Ricci scalar through the BransDicke action:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:bd_action}
  S_\mathrm{BD} = \frac{1}{16\pi}\int d^4x\sqrt{-g}
    \left[\phi R - \frac{\omega_\mathrm{BD}}{\phi}(\nabla\phi)^2\right]
    + S_m[g_{\mu\nu}, \Psi_m],
\end{equation}
where $\omega_\mathrm{BD}$ is the coupling constant.  In this theory,
compact objects acquire a ``scalar charge'' $s_i = -\partial\ln m_i/
\partial\ln G_\mathrm{eff}$ that depends on their internal compactness.
A binary system with different scalar charges emits dipolar gravitational
radiation at $-1$PN order relative to the quadrupolar emission, leading
to the phase correction \cite{Damour_1993}:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:bd_phase}
  \delta\Psi_\mathrm{BD}(f) =
    -\frac{5\Delta s^2}{84(\omega_\mathrm{BD}+2)\eta}
    \cdot (\pi G\mathcal{M}_c f/c^3)^{-7/3},
\end{equation}
where $\Delta s = s_1 - s_2$.  For neutron-star binaries, $\Delta s$ is
maximal; for equal-mass black-hole binaries, it vanishes (black holes carry
no scalar hair in standard BransDicke theory), making GW170817 a much
stronger probe of $\omega_\mathrm{BD}$ than GW150914.

\subsubsection{Massive graviton and modified dispersion}

If the graviton carries a non-zero rest mass $m_g$, the dispersion relation
becomes $E^2 = p^2c^2 + m_g^2c^4$, leading to a frequency-dependent phase
velocity.  The resulting phase shift in the gravitational waveform is
\cite{Will_1998}:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:mg_phase}
  \delta\Psi_{m_g}(f) = \frac{\pi^2 d_L m_g^2 c}{h f(1+z)},
\end{equation}
where $d_L$ is the luminosity distance and $z$ is the redshift.  Current
LIGO/Virgo bounds constrain $m_g < 1.27\times10^{-23}\,\mathrm{eV}/c^2$
\cite{Abbott_2021_GWTC3_TGR}, corresponding to a Compton wavelength exceeding
$10^{16}$\,km.  QNIM uses this as a calibration baseline for Layer~5.

\subsubsection{Extra dimensions and the Randall--Sundrum model}

In the RandallSundrum braneworld model \cite{Randall_1999}, our observable
universe is a four-dimensional brane embedded in a five-dimensional anti-de
Sitter bulk.  Gravitational waves can leak into the bulk on scales smaller
than the crossover radius $R_\mathrm{cross}$.  For sources at
$d_L > R_\mathrm{cross}$, the strain amplitude damps as:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:rs_correction}
  h \propto d_L^{-(1+n_\mathrm{extra}/2)},
\end{equation}
where $n_\mathrm{extra}$ is the number of large extra dimensions.  The
ADD (Arkani-Hamed, Dimopoulos, Dvali) variant allows $n_\mathrm{extra} = 2$
with $R_\mathrm{cross} \sim \mathcal{O}(10\,\mathrm{Mpc})$, detectable as
an anomalous distanceluminosity relation in the standard-siren analysis.

\subsubsection{Lorentz invariance violation and birefringence}

More general modifications to the graviton dispersion relation take the form:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:liv_dispersion}
  E^2 = p^2c^2\left[1 + A_\alpha\!\left(\frac{pc}{E_\mathrm{QG}}\right)^{\alpha-2}
    \right] + m_g^2c^4,
\end{equation}
where $E_\mathrm{QG}$ is the quantum-gravity energy scale and $\alpha$ is
the power-law index.  For $\alpha = 3$ (linear LIV), $E_\mathrm{QG}$ is
already constrained to exceed $10^{18}$\,GeV by GW170817.  QNIM's Layer~5
injection protocol allows $\alpha$ to take any value in $[2,4]$ and
simultaneously models gravitational birefringence  a differential phase
shift between the $+$ and $\times$ polarisations  which arises in
parity-violating extensions of GR such as ChernSimons gravity.

\subsection{Layer 6: horizon quantum topology}
\label{subsec:layer6}

\subsubsection{The Fuzzball paradigm}

In string-theoretic approaches to black holes, the event horizon is
dissolved into a ``Fuzzball'' microstate geometry \cite{Mathur_2005}: a
horizonless configuration in which the mass is supported by string-scale
structure rather than a singularity.  The Fuzzball acts as a partially
reflecting wall.  Radiation that would classically be absorbed by the horizon
is instead reflected, reprocessed, and re-emitted with a time delay:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:fuzzball_delay}
  \Delta t_\mathrm{echo} = 2\int_{r_s}^{r_+}
    \frac{dr}{c\,f(r)} \approx \frac{2r_+}{c}\ln\!\left(\frac{r_+}{r_s}\right),
\end{equation}
where $r_s$ is the Fuzzball surface radius (slightly outside $r_+$).  The
resulting signal in the time domain is a train of gravitational-wave echoes:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:echo_train}
  h_\mathrm{ringdown}(t) = h_\mathrm{QNM}(t)
    + \sum_{n=1}^{N} \mathcal{R}^n \cdot h_\mathrm{QNM}(t - n\Delta t),
\end{equation}
where $\mathcal{R}$ is the complex reflectivity of the Fuzzball surface
($|\mathcal{R}| \in [0,1]$).  QNIM models this echo train as a Dirichlet
series and injects it into the synthetic ringdown signal at a controlled
amplitude relative to the QNM amplitude.

\subsubsection{LQG area quantisation and the Barbero--Immirzi parameter}

In Loop Quantum Gravity \cite{Ashtekar_2004}, the area of the event horizon
is quantised in units of the Planck area:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:lqg_area}
  A_n = 8\pi\gamma\,l_P^2\sum_k\sqrt{j_k(j_k+1)},
\end{equation}
where $\gamma$ is the BarberoImmirzi parameter and $j_k$ are spin-network
labels.  For the physical value $\gamma_\mathrm{Meissner} = \ln 2/(\pi\sqrt{3})$
\cite{Meissner_2004}, the minimum area quantum is $A_\mathrm{min} \approx
4\ln2\,l_P^2$.  The discrete spectrum of area quanta produces a corresponding
discreteness in the QNM spectrum, observable as spectral line broadening in
high-SNR ringdowns.  The echo time delay in LQG is:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:lqg_correction}
  \Delta t_\mathrm{LQG} =
    \frac{2r_+(a)}{c}\ln\!\left(\frac{M_f}{m_P\sqrt{\gamma}}\right),
\end{equation}
which is numerically similar to the Fuzzball prediction but has a different
dependence on $\gamma$ and $M_f$, making them in principle distinguishable
by QNIM at high SNR.

\subsubsection{Quadrupole deviations and the generalised No-Hair test}

A model-independent test of the Kerr geometry is provided by parameterising
the quadrupole moment as $Q = -J^2(1+\delta_Q)/M$, where $\delta_Q$ is a
free parameter.  A non-zero $\delta_Q$ modifies the QNM frequencies at
leading order through the relation:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:nohair_deviation}
  \frac{\delta\omega_R}{\omega_R} \approx -0.15\,\delta_Q\,\chi_f^2
    + \mathcal{O}(\delta_Q^2).
\end{equation}
QNIM's Layer~6 injection simultaneously varies $\delta_Q$, $|\mathcal{R}|$,
and $\Delta t$ as independent parameters, requiring the quantum classifier
to disentangle three physically distinct signatures in the ringdown tail.

\subsection{Layer 7: deep quantum spacetime}
\label{subsec:layer7}

\subsubsection{Hawking radiation and the information paradox}

The thermal radiation predicted by Hawking \cite{Hawking_1975} to be emitted
by black holes at temperature $T_H = \hbar c^3/(8\pi G M k_B)$ has never
been directly observed, as the temperature is $\sim 6\times10^{-8}\,(M_\odot/M)$
K for stellar-mass objects  vastly smaller than the cosmic microwave background.
However, the back-reaction of Hawking radiation on the metric produces
sub-leading corrections to the gravitational-wave amplitude that are
accessible through zeta-function regularisation of the energy-momentum tensor
\cite{Bekenstein_1973}:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:casimir_zeta}
  E_0^\mathrm{reg} = \frac{\hbar\omega_c}{2}\,\zeta(-1)
    = -\frac{\hbar\omega_c}{24},
\end{equation}
where $\zeta(-1) = -1/12$ is obtained by analytic continuation of the
Riemann zeta function.  This regularised vacuum energy sets the floor of
the stochastic gravitational-wave background predicted by Layer~7.

\subsubsection{Spacetime foam and Ornstein--Uhlenbeck phase diffusion}

Wheeler's spacetime foam hypothesis posits that at the Planck length
$l_P = \sqrt{\hbar G/c^3} \approx 1.6\times10^{-35}$\,m, the metric
undergoes random topological fluctuations analogous to quantum foam.
At the macroscopic scale accessible to LIGO, these fluctuations manifest
as a stochastic phase noise $\delta\varphi(t)$ in the gravitational-wave
signal.  QNIM models this noise as an OrnsteinUhlenbeck process:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:ou_process}
  d(\delta\varphi_t) = -\gamma_\mathrm{foam}\,\delta\varphi_t\,dt
    + \sqrt{2\gamma_\mathrm{foam}\,T_\mathrm{foam}}\,dW_t,
\end{equation}
where $\gamma_\mathrm{foam}$ is the relaxation rate, $T_\mathrm{foam}$ is
an effective foam ``temperature'' that parameterises the strength of the
quantum fluctuations, and $W_t$ is a standard Wiener process.  The power
spectrum of this noise is Lorentzian:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:ou_spectrum}
  S_\mathrm{foam}(f) =
    \frac{4\gamma_\mathrm{foam}\,T_\mathrm{foam}}
         {\gamma_\mathrm{foam}^2 + (2\pi f)^2},
\end{equation}
which produces a characteristic ``knee'' frequency $f_\mathrm{knee} =
\gamma_\mathrm{foam}/(2\pi)$ that QNIM estimates from the spectral
broadening of the QNM lines.  The distinction between this fundamental
noise and instrumental $1/f$ noise is one of the most demanding
classification tasks in the QNIM framework.

These seven layers constitute the \emph{complete signal hierarchy} that QNIM's quantum classifier must discriminate: from the infrared-dominated Einstein frame at Layer~1 through the quantum-topology scenarios of Layer~6, to the deepest quantum-foam corrections of Layer~7. With this hierarchy in place, we now turn to the observational tools that connect these signatures to real astrophysical data. The infrared structure of gravity—the BMS symmetry group, the soft graviton theorem, and gravitational memory—while intimately related to the quantum structure of spacetime, forms a separate theoretical layer deferred to Appendix~\ref{app:bms-superad}. The following sections focus on the observables that are \emph{directly encoded in the strain time series} accessible to QNIM's feature map.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Standard sirens and the Hubble tension}
\label{sec:standard-sirens}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------
Gravitational waves from compact binaries provide a self-calibrating distance measurement that is independent of the cosmic distance ladder. This section describes how that measurement is made, its current contribution to the Hubble constant debate, and how the QNIM quantum metrological gain translates into a concrete improvement in cosmological inference.
\subsection{The luminosity-distance measurement}
\label{subsec:siren-distance}

As first recognised by Schutz \cite{Schutz_1986}, the gravitational-wave
signal of a compact binary is a ``self-calibrating'' distance indicator:
the chirp mass extracted from the phasing, combined with the observed
amplitude, determines the luminosity distance $d_L$ without any external
calibration (i.e., without the cosmic distance ladder):
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:siren_amplitude}
  h \propto \frac{G\mathcal{M}_c}{c^2 d_L}
    \left(\frac{G\mathcal{M}_c \pi f}{c^3}\right)^{2/3}.
\end{equation}
By combining $d_L$ with a redshift $z$ obtained from an electromagnetic
counterpart (or from statistical galaxy catalogues), the Hubble constant
follows directly:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:siren_h0}
  H_0 \approx \frac{c\,z}{d_L}
    \quad (z \ll 1).
\end{equation}
At higher redshift, a full cosmological model (e.g., flat $\Lambda$CDM) is
required to relate $d_L$ to $z$ and $H_0$.

\subsection{Current tension and GW contribution}
\label{subsec:hubble-tension}

Modern cosmology faces a tension between the ``early-universe'' estimate
$H_0 = 67.4 \pm 0.5$\,km\,s$^{-1}$\,Mpc$^{-1}$ from Planck CMB
measurements \cite{Planck_2020} and late-universe distance-ladder values,
whose severity depends strongly on the calibration method.  The SH0ES
programme, incorporating Small Magellanic Cloud Cepheids as a fourth
geometric anchor observed with HST, yields
$H_0 = 73.17 \pm 0.86$\,km\,s$^{-1}$\,Mpc$^{-1}$ \cite{Breuval_2024},
updating the earlier \cite{Riess_2022} value and implying a
$5.8\sigma$ tension with Planck.  By contrast, the Chicago--Carnegie
Hubble Programme (CCHP), calibrating via JWST observations of the tip
of the red giant branch (TRGB), obtains
$H_0 = 70.39 \pm 1.22\,(\mathrm{stat}) \pm 1.33\,(\mathrm{sys})$\,km\,s$^{-1}$\,Mpc$^{-1}$
\cite{Freedman_2024}; using JWST data alone the CCHP TRGB anchor gives
$H_0 = 68.81 \pm 2.21$\,km\,s$^{-1}$\,Mpc$^{-1}$, consistent with the CMB.
Considering the full ensemble of independent late-universe probes, the
tension currently stands at approximately $3.7$--$5.8\sigma$ depending
on the distance calibration adopted.  Whether this reflects new physics
(early dark energy, modified gravity, interacting dark matter) or
unresolved systematics in one or more rungs of the distance ladder remains
one of the most pressing open questions in cosmology.

The GW170817 standard-siren measurement yielded
$H_0 = 70.0^{+12.0}_{-8.0}$\,km\,s$^{-1}$\,Mpc$^{-1}$ \cite{Abbott_2017c},
consistent with both extremes but with too large an uncertainty to adjudicate.
Reaching a sub-percent measurement of $H_0$ via GWs requires
$\mathcal{O}(10^3)$ events with electromagnetic counterparts or large
galaxy catalogue datasets.  The QNIM pipeline reduces the systematic floor in luminosity-distance
estimation through the quantum metrological gain $G_{d_L} = 1.09$
(Table~\ref{tab:qmg-parameters}), which corresponds to a
$1 - 1/\sqrt{G_{d_L}} \approx 4.3\,\%$ reduction in $\sigma_{d_L}$
relative to classical matched filtering at the same SNR\@. This modest
but systematic improvement propagates directly into the $H_0$ posterior:
for a fixed set of $N$ standard-siren events, it is equivalent to
adding approximately $N(G_{d_L} - 1) \approx 0.09N$ additional
events to the catalogue. For the 13-event GWTC-3 subset used in
Section~\ref{sec:cosmological-results}, this corresponds to roughly
one additional effective event. The non-linear memory effect
\cite{Christodoulou_1991} contributes to this gain by encoding
an additional observable in the late-time ringdown tail that is
captured by the quantum feature map but not by the standard
matched-filter statistic; a dedicated analysis of the memory
contribution is reserved for future work.

\subsection{Dark energy and the speed of gravitational waves}
\label{subsec:dark-energy}

The detection of GW170817 and its associated gamma-ray burst GRB~170817A
with a time delay of only $1.74 \pm 0.05$\,s over $40$\,Mpc constrained
the gravitational-wave propagation speed to \cite{Abbott_2017b}:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:speed_constraint}
  \left|\frac{c_\mathrm{GW}}{c} - 1\right| < 5\times10^{-16}.
\end{equation}
This single measurement ruled out a large class of scalar-tensor and
vector-tensor dark energy models  in particular, most k-essence models
\cite{Armendariz_2001} in which the graviton propagation speed deviates
from $c$.  In the QNIM framework, this constraint is implemented as a hard
prior on the Layer~5 parameter space, drastically reducing the volume of
viable modified-gravity theories.

With this landscape of standard sirens and cosmological observables in place, the present chapter's task is complete: we have established the full physical vocabulary needed to interpret QNIM's results. The analysis of superradiance and axion clouds, while relevant to future searches for ultralight bosons through spin-gap constraints, do not directly enter the thirteen-class classification pipeline presented in Chapter~\ref{chap:results}. These topics are consequently deferred to Appendix~\ref{app:bms-superad}, together with the infrared structure of gravity and the complete treatment of zeta-function regularisation.

The following section provides a consolidated reference table of all parameters and their roles, serving as the bridge from theoretical physics to computational methodology.

% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
% CHAPTER 3  THE QNIM METHODOLOGY
% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\chapter{The QNIM analytical methodology}
\label{chap:methodology-qnim}

This chapter describes the analytical core of QNIM. We begin with raw gravitational-wave
strain data—already conditioned and whitened—and project it into a $2^{12}$-dimensional
quantum Hilbert space where beyond-GR signatures become linearly separable.
The seven-step methodology is: \emph{(1)~Classical encoding} (Section~\ref{sec:encoding})
converts strain into 12 classical feature vectors via STFT and PCA; 
\emph{(2)~quantum feature map} (Section~\ref{sec:qfm}) lifts these into quantum 
amplitudes inaccessible to classical representations; \emph{(3)~variational ansatz} 
(Section~\ref{sec:vqc}) applies a trainable quantum circuit to entangle and rotate 
the state; \emph{(4)~Bayesian model ranking} (Section~\ref{sec:bayes-factor-formalism}) 
compares the thirteen beyond-GR theories against General Relativity using Bayes factors; 
\emph{(5)~QUBO parameter estimation} (Section~\ref{sec:qubo}) reformulates parameter 
refinement as a binary optimisation problem; \emph{(6)~gradient-based optimisation} 
(Section~\ref{sec:optimizer}) tunes the VQC using natural gradients and zero-noise 
extrapolation; and \emph{(7)~end-to-end pipeline} (Section~\ref{sec:pipeline}) 
orchestrates all stages. The chapter justifies why quantum computing provides advantage 
at each step and how each component integrates into a unified system.

Throughout, we use the following notation conventions:
\begin{itemize}
  \item $n$: number of physical qubits.
  \item $d$: number of classical input features (after PCA).
  \item $p$: number of variational parameters.
  \item $\boldsymbol{\theta} \in \mathbb{R}^p$: vector of trainable circuit
        parameters.
  \item $\mathbf{x} \in \mathbb{R}^d$: classical feature vector for a
        single gravitational-wave event.
  \item $|\psi(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\theta})\rangle$: parameterised
        quantum state on $n$ qubits.
\end{itemize}

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{From gravitational-wave strain to classical feature vectors}
\label{sec:encoding}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

Before any quantum operation, the raw detector strain must be converted
into a compact, noise-whitened feature vector of fixed dimension $d$.
This pre-processing pipeline is entirely classical and is implemented as
a set of Value Objects within the QNIM Domain model
(Section~\ref{app:sec:domain-model}).

The preprocessing pipeline is entirely classical and consists of four sequential
operations that transform raw detector output into the compact, noise-whitened
vector that the quantum circuit will encode.

\subsection{Data acquisition and conditioning}
\label{subsec:conditioning}

The input to the pipeline is the calibrated, frequency-domain strain
$\tilde{s}(f)$ downloaded from the Gravitational-Wave Open Science Center
(GWOSC).  The conditioning steps are:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Noise power estimation.}  The one-sided power spectral
        density $S_n(f)$ is estimated using a Welch periodogram on an
        off-source stretch of 256\,s surrounding the event.

  \item \textbf{Whitening and bandpassing.}  The strain is whitened
        by dividing by $\sqrt{S_n(f)}$ and bandpassed to the sensitive
        frequency range $[f_\mathrm{low}, f_\mathrm{high}]$.  For the
        QNIM training dataset, $f_\mathrm{low} = 20$\,Hz and
        $f_\mathrm{high} = 1024$\,Hz.

  \item \textbf{Time-frequency representation.}  The whitened strain is
        mapped to a time-frequency spectrogram using a short-time Fourier
        transform (STFT) with a Tukey window of length 0.5\,s and
        50\,\% overlap.

  \item \textbf{Matched-filter score vector.}  For theory classification,
        the spectrogram is supplemented by the matched-filter log-likelihood
        $\ln\mathcal{L}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_\mathrm{MAP})$ and its
        first-order phase-space gradient, evaluated at the Maximum A
        Posteriori (MAP) estimate returned by a fast Bayesian sampler.
\end{enumerate}

The conditioned feature matrix typically contains thousands of frequency-domain samples.
Before any quantum encoding, this dimensionality must be reduced to match the number
of available qubits while preserving the signal variance that distinguishes the
thirteen theory classes.

\subsection{Dimensionality reduction via PCA}
\label{subsec:pca}

The concatenated feature matrix (spectrogram plus matched-filter
statistics) typically has $\mathcal{O}(10^3)$ dimensions.  QNIM applies
Principal Component Analysis (PCA) to reduce this to $d = 12$ principal
components, retaining $\geq 95\,\%$ of the total explained variance
across the training dataset.  The choice $d = 12$ matches the number of
qubits $n = 12$, enabling a one-to-one assignment of principal components
to qubit rotation angles in the encoding layer.

The PCA basis is fitted once on the training set and applied without
retraining to all subsequent events, ensuring that the quantum feature
map operates in a fixed, physically meaningful subspace of signal
variation.  The explained variance fractions for the 12 retained
components are listed in Table~\ref{tab:pca-variance}.

\textbf{Cumulative variance (\%)} \\
