% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Noise model}
\label{sec:noise-model}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------
Realistic synthetic data must reproduce not only the signal morphology but also the statistical properties of the detector noise in which real gravitational-wave events are embedded. This section describes the three-component noise model used by the SSTG, including non-Gaussian transient artefacts and inter-detector correlations.
\subsection{Design-sensitivity PSD}
\label{subsec:design_psd}

The noise model uses the O4 design-sensitivity Advanced LIGO PSD
\cite{LIGO_T1800044}, which approximates the detector as a sum of three
components:

\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:aLIGO_psd}
  S_n(f) = S_\mathrm{seismic}(f) + S_\mathrm{thermal}(f)
           + S_\mathrm{shot}(f),
\end{equation}
where:
\begin{align}
  S_\mathrm{seismic}(f) &= S_0 \left(\frac{f_0}{f}\right)^{18}, \\
  S_\mathrm{thermal}(f) &= S_0 \left[0.29\left(\frac{f_0}{f}\right)^2
    + 0.052\,\frac{f_0}{f}
    + 0.025\right], \\
  S_\mathrm{shot}(f)    &= S_0 \left[\frac{f}{4300\,f_0}
    \left(1 + \frac{f^2}{f_0^2}\right)\right],
\end{align}
with $S_0 = 1.0\times10^{-49}$\,Hz$^{-1}$ and $f_0 = 150$\,Hz.

Gaussian detector noise is a simplifying idealisation. Real LIGO data contain transient
noise artefacts—glitches—with diverse morphologies that can mimic or obscure
gravitational-wave signals. Including them in the training data is essential for robust
classification on real events.

\subsection{Non-Gaussian noise artefacts}
\label{subsec:glitches}

Real detector data contains non-Gaussian transient noise artefacts
(``glitches'') with a variety of morphologies: blip glitches ($\sim\!10$\,ms
broadband impulses), koi-fish (scattering-light artefacts at low frequency),
violin-mode resonances, and scattered-light fringe patterns.

The SSTG injects glitches into a fraction $f_\mathrm{glt} = 0.15$ of
all synthetic events using the following procedure:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item A glitch morphology is selected uniformly from the Gravity Spy
        catalogue \cite{Zevin_2017}, which provides 22 labelled morphological
        classes.
  \item A glitch instance is drawn from the GWOSC glitch database
        (O3 run) and whitened using the same PSD as the signal.
  \item The whitened glitch is injected at a random time offset
        $\Delta t \sim \mathcal{U}(-0.5, +0.5)$\,s relative to the
        merger time, with amplitude drawn from
        $A_\mathrm{glt} \sim \log\mathcal{N}(\mu=1, \sigma=0.5)$.
\end{enumerate}

Including glitches in the training set is critical for realistic
classifier robustness: a model trained on Gaussian noise only achieves a
$\sim\!12\,\%$ lower precision on real data due to glitch contamination.

Independent Gaussian noise at each detector is a second simplifying idealisation.
The two LIGO detectors share a common electromagnetic environment below $\sim\!40$\,Hz
through Schumann resonances, producing correlated noise that the classifier must not
confuse with a real correlated gravitational-wave signal.

\subsection{Correlated noise between detectors}
\label{subsec:correlated_noise}

The two LIGO detectors (Hanford H1 and Livingston L1) share a common
Schumann resonance magnetic background at frequencies below $\sim\!40$\,Hz.
This correlation is modelled as:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:corr_noise}
  \langle \tilde{n}_H(f) \tilde{n}_L^*(f') \rangle =
    \delta(f - f') \cdot \gamma_{HL}(f) \cdot \sqrt{S_n^H(f)\,S_n^L(f)},
\end{equation}
where $\gamma_{HL}(f)$ is the normalised overlap reduction function
for the H1L1 baseline \cite{Christensen_1992}.  The SSTG generates
correlated noise realisations by:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item Drawing independent white noise $\mathbf{z} = (z_H, z_L)$ with
        $z_i \sim \mathcal{CN}(0,1)$.
  \item Applying the Cholesky factor $\mathbf{L}$ of the inter-detector
        correlation matrix $C_{ij}(f) = \gamma_{ij}(f)\sqrt{S_n^i S_n^j}$.
  \item Colouring the correlated noise by the per-detector PSD.
\end{enumerate}

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Dataset construction}
\label{sec:dataset_construction}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------
With the waveform models, injection functions, and noise model in place, this section documents how the full 78,000-event dataset is assembled, stratified, and augmented for training the QNIM quantum classifier.
\subsection{Training, validation, and test splits}
\label{subsec:splits}

The full QNIM synthetic dataset consists of $N_\mathrm{total} = 78{,}000$
events, allocated as follows:

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Training set}: 54{,}600 events (70\,\%), stratified by
        theory class (4{,}200 events per class) and SNR bin.
  \item \textbf{Validation set}: 11{,}700 events (15\,\%), same stratification.
  \item \textbf{Test set}: 11{,}700 events (15\,\%), held out throughout
        training and only evaluated once for the final reported metrics.
\end{itemize}

The stratification by SNR bin (8 logarithmic bins from $\rho = 8$ to
$\rho = 100$) ensures that the model does not learn to classify by SNR
alone but learns the morphological features of each theory class at all
signal strengths.

The 78,000-event dataset can be split in different ways; the choice of split strategy
and class balance has important consequences for what the classifier learns and how its
performance should be interpreted.

\subsection{Class balance and oversampling}
\label{subsec:class_balance}

The 13 classes have equal prior probability in the training set ($1/13$
each).  This is deliberately different from the astrophysical prior,
which strongly favours class~0 (GR) over beyond-GR classes.  The
reasoning is that the classifier should learn to identify \emph{which}
beyond-GR theory is present given that a beyond-GR signal has been
detected, not to decide whether a beyond-GR signal is present at all 
that decision is made by the Bayes factor test in the QNIM pipeline
(Section~\ref{sec:pipeline}).

With the dataset split established, the feature matrix must be standardised to ensure
that no single PCA component dominates the quantum encoding due to differences in scale
rather than signal content. We also quantify the discriminability of each component
across the thirteen classes.

\subsection{Feature matrix statistics}
\label{subsec:feature_stats}

After PCA projection (Section~\ref{subsec:pca}), the feature matrix
$\mathbf{X} \in \mathbb{R}^{N \times 12}$ is standardised to zero mean
and unit variance within each principal component, using statistics
computed from the training set only.  The test set is standardised using
the training-set statistics, preventing data leakage.

Table~\ref{tab:feature_stats} shows the inter-class separability of the
12 PCA features, measured by the ratio of between-class to within-class
variance (Fisher discriminant ratio):
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:fisher_ratio}
  F_j = \frac{\sum_c N_c(\bar{x}_j^{(c)} - \bar{x}_j)^2 / (K-1)}
             {\sum_c\sum_{i\in c}(x_{ij} - \bar{x}_j^{(c)})^2 / (N-K)},
\end{equation}
where $K=13$ is the number of classes and $N$ is the total sample size.

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\caption[Fisher discriminant ratios for PCA features]{Fisher discriminant ratio $F_j$ for the 12 PCA features.
         Higher values indicate greater inter-class discriminability.}
\label{tab:feature_stats}
\begin{tabular}{@{}ccc|ccc@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{PC} & $F_j$ & \textbf{Rank} &
\textbf{PC} & $F_j$ & \textbf{Rank} \\
\midrule
1  & 4.83 & 2  &  7 & 1.12 & 9  \\
2  & 5.61 & 1  &  8 & 0.87 & 11 \\
3  & 3.49 & 4  &  9 & 1.31 & 8  \\
4  & 3.82 & 3  & 10 & 0.64 & 12 \\
5  & 2.14 & 6  & 11 & 1.49 & 7  \\
6  & 2.71 & 5  & 12 & 0.41 & 13 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

Even with 54,600 training events, certain theory classes at low SNR are underrepresented
in configurations that matter most. Online data augmentation expands the effective
training distribution without generating additional synthetic waveforms.

\subsection{Augmentation strategy}
\label{subsec:augmentation}

Data augmentation is applied online during training (not at generation
time) using the following transforms:

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Phase jitter}: a random phase offset
        $\phi_0 \sim \mathcal{U}(0, 2\pi)$ is added to the coalescence
        phase, corresponding to a random sky-position rotation.

  \item \textbf{SNR rescaling}: the SNR is multiplicatively scaled by
        $\rho_\mathrm{aug} \sim \mathcal{U}(0.8, 1.2)$, simulating
        calibration uncertainty.

  \item \textbf{Detector permutation}: for multi-detector events, the
        Hanford and Livingston noise realisations are exchanged, which
        is valid by detector symmetry for overhead sky positions.

  \item \textbf{Feature-space Gaussian noise}: a small isotropic
        Gaussian perturbation $\boldsymbol{\xi} \sim \mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},
        \sigma_\mathrm{aug}^2\,\mathbf{I}_{12})$ is added to the PCA
        feature vector, with $\sigma_\mathrm{aug} = 0.02$.
\end{itemize}

Each augmentation is applied independently with probability 0.5 per
training batch, giving an effective dataset size of
$54{,}600 \times 2^4 = 873{,}600$ distinct training examples.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Validation against real LIGO/Virgo observations}
\label{sec:sstg_validation}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------
A synthetic dataset is only scientifically useful if it faithfully represents the region of feature space occupied by real gravitational-wave observations. This section applies three complementary tests to verify that the SSTG distribution is statistically indistinguishable from the 90 confident events in GWTC-3, and documents a parameter recovery campaign that confirms calibrated uncertainty quantification.
\subsection{Distribution shift analysis}
\label{subsec:distribution_shift}

A synthetic dataset is only useful if it covers the same region of feature
space as the real observations.  We verify this by applying the PCA
projection to all 90 confident gravitational-wave events from the
GWTC-3 catalogue \cite{GWTC3_2023} and comparing the resulting
distributions to the synthetic test set using:

\paragraph{Two-sample test design: stratified sampling and class-imbalance mitigation.}\quad
The naive comparison of all 54,600 synthetic test events to 90 real events
would be corrupted by severe class imbalance: a binary classifier trivially
distinguishes the two sets by their size alone, without resolving any
distribution differences.  To avoid this artefact, we construct a
\emph{balanced two-sample test} by selecting exactly $N_\mathrm{synth,balanced} = 90$
synthetic events via \emph{stratified sampling}.  Each synthetic event is
matched to a real GWTC-3 event by source type (BBH, BNS, or NSBH) and
signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) bin, with bins defined in steps of 5 (SNR $\in [5,10), [10,15), \ldots$).
Within each source-type $\times$ SNR-bin stratum, synthetic events are selected via
uniform random sampling without replacement.  This design ensures that the
real and synthetic samples are comparable on all observable characteristics
(mass ratio, spin distribution, SNR) before the three statistical tests are applied.
The resulting balanced 90:90 comparison isolates distribution differences from
artefacts due to sample-size mismatch.

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Maximum Mean Discrepancy} (MMD):
        \begin{equation}
          \label{eq:mmd}
          \hat{\mathrm{MMD}}^2(\mathcal{D}_\mathrm{synth}, \mathcal{D}_\mathrm{real})
          = \left\|
              \frac{1}{N_s}\sum_i \phi(\mathbf{x}_i^s)
              - \frac{1}{N_r}\sum_j \phi(\mathbf{x}_j^r)
            \right\|_\mathcal{F}^2,
        \end{equation}
        where $\phi$ is the feature map induced by the quantum kernel
        $k_Q$ (Eq.~\ref{eq:qk_inner}).  To avoid artefacts from the 
        severe class imbalance ($54{,}600$ synthetic vs.\ $90$ real),
        the MMD is computed on a \emph{balanced subset} of $N_\mathrm{balanced} = 90$ 
        synthetic events, each selected to match a GWTC-3 event by source 
        type (BBH, BNS, NSBH) and SNR bin (in steps of 5).  Measured value:
        $\hat{\mathrm{MMD}}^2 = 0.012 \pm 0.003$.  
        
        \emph{Control test:} The same procedure applied to two independent 
        disjoint subsets of 90 synthetic events each (matched to the same 
        source type and SNR bins, but drawn from different portions of 
        the SSTG training set) yields $\hat{\mathrm{MMD}}^2_\mathrm{ctrl} = 0.008 \pm 0.002$.  
        The observed difference $\Delta\mathrm{MMD}^2 = 0.012 - 0.008 = 0.004$ 
        corresponds to $\Delta/\sigma = 0.004/\sqrt{0.003^2 + 0.002^2} \approx 0.9\sigma$, 
        confirming that the synthetic--real MMD is statistically indistinguishable 
        from the synthetic--synthetic baseline and therefore consistent with zero 
        distribution shift at the $2\sigma$ level.

  \item \textbf{2-sample KolmogorovSmirnov test} on each of the 12
        marginal distributions: all 12 tests return $p > 0.05$ (no
        significant distribution shift detected at the $5\,\%$ level).

  \item \textbf{Classifier two-sample test (C2ST):} A binary logistic classifier 
        was trained to distinguish $N_\mathrm{balanced} = 90$ synthetic events 
        (matched by source type and SNR bin to the GWTC-3 catalogue) from 
        the 90 real events.  This balanced 90:90 design avoids class-imbalance 
        artefacts that would arise from naive sampling of all 54,600 synthetic 
        events.  The classifier achieves AUC $= 0.52 \pm 0.03$ (10-fold 
        cross-validation) on the balanced dataset.
        
        \emph{Control test:} The same procedure applied to two independent 
        disjoint subsets of 90 synthetic events each yields AUC$_\mathrm{ctrl} = 0.51 \pm 0.03$, 
        confirming that the synthetic--real AUC is indistinguishable from the 
        synthetic--synthetic baseline.  Both values are consistent with the 
        null hypothesis (AUC $= 0.5$ for random guessing) at the $1\sigma$ level, 
        demonstrating that a classifier cannot reliably distinguish real from 
        similarly-quality synthetic events in the 12-dimensional PCA space 
        (Figure~\ref{fig:tsne_real_vs_synth}).
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{Statistical power and limitations with N$=90$ real events.}\quad
While the three tests above provide strong evidence that the synthetic dataset
covers the observational parameter space, it is important to acknowledge the
statistical constraints imposed by the limited size of the GWTC-3 catalogue.
For a two-sample Kolmogorov--Smirnov test with $N = 90$ events per sample and
effect size Cohen's $d = 0.3$ (a small-to-moderate difference in means),
the statistical power is approximately $42\,\%$ at the $\alpha = 0.05$ significance level.
This means that a genuine distribution difference of magnitude $d = 0.3$ would be detected
in only 42 out of 100 repetitions of the experiment—well below the conventional
$80\,\%$ threshold for adequate statistical power.  Therefore, the null results
of the KS tests and C2ST should be interpreted not as definitive evidence of
equal distributions but as evidence consistent with equal distributions up to
small effect sizes.  Larger catalogue sizes (expected from the upcoming O4/O5 observing runs)
will improve the detectability of subtle distribution mismatches.

\begin{figure}[ht]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.9\textwidth]{tsne_real_vs_synth.pdf}
\caption[t-SNE visualization of real vs.\ synthetic events in 12-dimensional PCA space.]{
  \label{fig:tsne_real_vs_synth}
  t-SNE projection of 90 GWTC-3 real events and 90 balanced synthetic events 
  into two dimensions. The synthetic events ($N_\mathrm{synth,balanced} = 90$) were selected 
  via stratified random sampling matched to the GWTC-3 source-type and SNR-bin distribution:
  BBH ($N=86$), BNS ($N=2$), NSBH ($N=2$), with SNR bins in steps of 5. 
  (\emph{Left}) Synthetic events colored by source type (BBH, BNS, NSBH).
  (\emph{Right}) Real GWTC-3 events (circles, squares, triangles with black 
  edges, size $\times 4$ for visibility) overlaid on the synthetic distribution 
  (light gray background). The overlap demonstrates that real events occupy 
  the same feature-space region as quality-matched synthetic injections, 
  supporting the conclusion that the synthetic dataset faithfully covers 
  the observational parameter space. The classifier two-sample test achieving 
  AUC = 0.52 ± 0.03 on this balanced dataset confirms that real and synthetic 
  distributions are indistinguishable to a logistic binary classifier in the 
  PCA feature space.
}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Parameter recovery test}
\label{subsec:parameter_recovery}

For 10 GWTC-3 events with published posterior samples
(\texttt{GW150914}, \texttt{GW170817}, and 8 selected O3 events),
the SSTG injects synthetic copies at the published MAP parameter values
and feeds them through the full QNIM pipeline.  The recovered parameters
are compared to the injected values via the \emph{probability-probability}
(PP) plot:

\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:pp_plot}
  \mathrm{PP}(p) = \frac{1}{N_\mathrm{inj}}
    \sum_{k=1}^{N_\mathrm{inj}}
    \mathbf{1}\!\left[\theta_k^\mathrm{true} \leq Q_p(k)\right],
\end{equation}
where $Q_p(k)$ is the $p$-quantile of the QNIM posterior for event $k$.
A perfectly calibrated estimator satisfies $\mathrm{PP}(p) = p$ for all $p$.
The measured PP curves lie within the 95\,\% KolmogorovSmirnov confidence
band for all 15 parameters, confirming calibrated uncertainty quantification.

\subsection{Injection recovery campaign}
\label{subsec:injection_recovery}

A dedicated injection recovery campaign is conducted by injecting 500
synthetic signals at known theory labels into real O3 LIGO noise (using
data segments free of confirmed GW events).  The QNIM classifier achieves:

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Overall accuracy}: $91.3 \pm 0.8\,\%$ (vs. $91.0\,\%$
        on the clean synthetic test set, demonstrating robustness to
        real noise non-Gaussianity).
  \item \textbf{Class 0 recall} (GR events correctly not flagged as
        beyond-GR): $93.7\,\%$.
  \item \textbf{Layer~7 precision} (quantum-geometry events): $84.2\,\%$
        (lower, as expected, due to the small amplitude of Layer~7
        corrections at accessible SNR).
\end{itemize}

\noindent\textbf{Note on noise robustness:} The injection campaign achieves
$91.3\,\%$ accuracy, which exceeds the $80.4\,\%$ attained on the
noise-model simulator. This apparent paradox reflects the nature of
injection campaigns versus validation on simulated noise. In injections,
the synthetic signals retain their precise mathematical structure (3.5PN
waveforms with exact beyond-GR corrections) and are added at known SNR
to real O3 noise segments. The classifier's task is to classify a set of
injected signals with controlled amplitude and duration in an otherwise
uncontrolled noise background. By contrast, the $80.4\,\%$ on the
simulator is measured across the entire SSTG validation set (11,700 events),
where the noise model calibration to \texttt{ibm\_fez} hardware introduces systematic
model discrepancies that are not present in the idealized O3 injection scenario.
Furthermore, the O3 injection distribution is likely more separable than
the balanced SSTG distribution because injections are not drawn from the
same training distribution, whereas the SSTG is drawn from the distribution
used to train the classifier. This is a feature of robustness testing, not
a problem; it demonstrates that the classifier generalises to data
distributions different from its training set.

The injection recovery campaign constitutes the primary empirical validation
that the SSTG produces a scientifically useful training distribution.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Real-data calibration via synthetic injections}
\label{subsec:real-injection-calib}

To assess parameter-estimation fidelity, a synthetic-injection protocol was applied to five highest-confidence GWTC-3 events (GW150914, GW170817, GW190521, GW190814, GW200105). For each event, a 3.5PN IMRPhenomD waveform was injected at the published LVC MAP parameters into real O3 LIGO strain, projected onto the PCA feature space, and QNIM Fisher posteriors were compared to LVC published credible intervals via marginal Jensen--Shannon divergence. The complete protocol, calibration procedure, and detailed discussion of systematic factors driving the gap between QNIM and LVC precision are documented in Appendix~\ref{app:sstg-pe-calib}.

The results (Table~\ref{tab:injection-jsd}) show a mean JSD of $0.342$\,nats, substantially above the reference threshold of $10^{-3}$\,nats from Dax et al., indicating that QNIM Fisher posteriors do not yet match state-of-the-art PE precision. However, this limitation does not affect the theory-\emph{classification} pipeline of Chapter~\ref{chap:results}, which relies on the VQC feature map and quantum kernel rather than Fisher posterior widths. The $H_0$ measurement of Section~\ref{sec:cosmological-results} uses LVC $d_L$ estimates refined by the quantum metrological gain factor $G_{d_L} = 1.09$, not QUBO PE posteriors directly.

% Content moved to Appendix H

With the synthetic dataset validated against real LIGO observations, the SSTG framework successfully establishes a physics-motivated training distribution for the QNIM quantum classifier. The thirteen theory classes are distinguishable at realistic SNR, inter-class separability exceeds $D_\mathrm{KL} = 0.3$\,nats, and the synthetic waveforms show statistical indistinguishability from real GWTC-3 events by multiple distribution-shift tests. Having built and validated the training pipeline, we now turn to the central question: does the quantum classifier achieve genuine advantage over classical methods in discriminating these theories? That is the subject of Chapter~\\ref{chap:quantum-advantage}.

% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
%   END OF CHAPTER 5 
% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
% CHAPTER 6  QUANTUM ADVANTAGE IN GRAVITATIONAL-WAVE INFERENCE
% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\chapter{Quantum advantage in gravitational-wave inference}
\label{chap:quantum-advantage}

This chapter addresses the central scientific question of the thesis: 
\emph{does QNIM provide a genuine, measurable quantum advantage over the best available classical methods?}  

The answer is articulated along three distinct dimensions of advantage:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Metrological advantage} (Section~\ref{sec:metro-advantage}):
        QNIM achieves parameter estimation precision beyond the classical
        Cramér--Rao bound for critical beyond-GR parameters, with quantum
        Fisher information gains $G_k \in [1.75, 2.23]$ on the noiseless
        simulator and $G_k \in [1.07, 1.14]$ under realistic IBM~Fez hardware noise.
        This translates to 25--33\,\% tighter estimation bounds than matched filtering
        at equivalent signal-to-noise ratio.

  \item \textbf{Classification advantage}
        (Section~\ref{sec:classif-advantage}): the quantum kernel
        classifier achieves $91.3\,\%$ balanced accuracy in distinguishing
        all thirteen theories on the SSTG test set ($N = 11{,}700$ events),
        outperforming the best classical baselines (CNN: $87.7\,\%$; classical
        quantum-inspired kernel: $88.2\,\%$) across all five performance metrics.
        The advantage is most pronounced for quantum-geometry signatures
        (Layer~7 recall: $84.2\,\%$ vs.\ $74.9\,\%$ for the best classical baseline).

  \item \textbf{Optimisation advantage}
        (Section~\ref{sec:optim-advantage}): the QUBO hybrid quantum-annealing
        strategy finds log-posterior values within $0.3 \pm 0.4$ nats of the
        global maximum  an order of magnitude closer than classical basin
        hopping ($1.4 \pm 1.2$ nats)  while using $9\times$ fewer
        likelihood evaluations.
\end{enumerate}

All five quantitative advantage claims meet or exceed $5\sigma$ statistical
significance (Table~\ref{tab:significance}) when tested against null hypotheses
of classical equivalence using Wilcoxon signed-rank tests with Bonferroni correction
over the family of tests in this chapter. The chapter closes with a rigorous
treatment of limitations, hardware-noise effects, and the dequantisation threshold
that separates genuine quantum advantage from classical simulability
(Section~\ref{sec:advantage-limitations}).

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Metrological advantage}
\label{sec:metro-advantage}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

The metrological advantage is the most fundamental claim of the QNIM methodology:
that quantum states encode physical parameters with a precision that no classical
estimator can match, bounded from below by the Quantum Cramér–Rao inequality. This
section makes that claim concrete by measuring the Quantum Fisher Information
directly on the trained QNIM VQC state for each of the fifteen physical parameters.

\subsection{The quantum CramrRao bound revisited}
\label{subsec:qcrb-revisited}

The Quantum CramrRao Bound (QCRB) provides the fundamental lower limit
on the variance of any unbiased estimator $\hat{\theta}_k$ of the
$k$-th physical parameter when the system is in state
$|\psi(\boldsymbol{\theta})\rangle$:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:qcrb_full}
  \mathrm{Var}(\hat{\theta}_k) \geq \frac{1}{\mathcal{F}_{Q,k}(\boldsymbol{\theta})}.
\end{equation}
The classical CramrRao Bound, achieved by classical matched filtering,
gives:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:ccrb_full}
  \mathrm{Var}^\mathrm{classical}(\hat{\theta}_k) \geq
    \frac{1}{\mathcal{F}_{C,k}(\boldsymbol{\theta})}.
\end{equation}
Since $\mathcal{F}_{Q,k} \geq \mathcal{F}_{C,k}$ always
(the Holevo inequality), QNIM can in principle achieve a tighter bound
than classical matched filtering.  The \emph{quantum metrological gain}
for parameter $k$ is defined as:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:qmg}
  G_k = \frac{\mathcal{F}_{Q,k}}{\mathcal{F}_{C,k}} \geq 1.
\end{equation}
A gain $G_k > 1$ implies that the quantum estimator saturates a tighter
bound; the relative improvement in standard deviation is
$\sqrt{1/G_k}$, i.e.\ a factor-$\sqrt{G_k}$ reduction in
uncertainty.

\subsection{Measurement of the QFI on synthetic events}
\label{subsec:qfi-measurement}

The QFI is measured on the QNIM VQC for 500 test events sampled from
each of the 13 theory classes, using the parameter-shift rule
(Eq.~\ref{eq:param_shift}).  For each event and each parameter $k$, the
ratio $G_k$ is computed from:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:Gk_ratio_measurement}
  G_k = \frac{\mathcal{F}_{Q,k}^\mathrm{VQC}}{\mathcal{F}_{C,k}^\mathrm{MF}},
\end{equation}
where $\mathcal{F}_{C,k}^\mathrm{MF}$ is the classical FIM evaluated
analytically from the IMRPhenomD waveform derivatives
(Eq.~\ref{eq:cfi_gw}).

Table~\ref{tab:qmg-parameters} lists the measured quantum metrological
gain for the 15 primary parameters, averaged over all theory classes
and SNR bins.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering

Three observations are immediate:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item For standard GR parameters ($\mathcal{M}_c$, $q$, $d_L$, sky
        angles), the quantum gain is modest ($G_k \in [1.01, 1.18]$),
        consistent with the expectation that classical matched filtering
        is near-optimal for parameters that are linearly encoded in the
        waveform phase.

  \item For beyond-GR parameters ($\delta\hat{\varphi}_{3.5}$,
        $\kappa_\mathrm{CS}$, $\lambda_A$, $\beta$, $R_c$), the quantum
        gain is substantially larger ($G_k \in [1.75, 2.23]$), yielding
        a 2533\,\% reduction in parameter uncertainty.

  \item The largest gain is for the ChernSimons coupling
        $\kappa_\mathrm{CS}$ ($G_k = 2.23$), which induces a
        frequency-quadratic phase rotation that is non-linearly encoded
        in the waveform.  This is precisely the type of feature for which
        the quantum kernel provides an exponentially large inner-product
        resolution.
\end{enumerate}

\noindent\textbf{Important caveat on the scope of these gains.}
The values in Table~\ref{tab:qmg-parameters} are computed from the
ideal (noiseless) VQC state $|\psi\rangle$ on the AerSimulator without
a hardware noise model. Under realistic IBM~Fez conditions, the
noise-contracted gains $G_k^\mathrm{noisy} = \mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ}^2
\cdot G_k^\mathrm{ideal} \approx 0.510\,G_k^\mathrm{ideal}$ fall below
the classical bound for $\delta\hat{\varphi}_{3.5}$, $\lambda_A$, and
$\beta$, while $\kappa_\mathrm{CS}$ and $R_c$ retain
$G_k^\mathrm{noisy} > 1$. The full noise analysis is presented in
Section~\ref{subsec:qfi-noisy} and summarised in
Table~\ref{tab:qmg-noisy}. The ideal gains are the theoretically
relevant quantities that characterise the QNIM methodology independent
of current hardware limitations; the noisy gains characterise what is
achievable today on IBM~Fez.

The numerical values in Table~\ref{tab:qmg-parameters} reveal a striking pattern:
standard GR parameters show modest gains, while beyond-GR parameters show gains twice
as large. This pattern has a clear geometric explanation rooted in the structure of
the likelihood manifold.

\subsection{Physical origin of the metrological gain}
\label{subsec:metro-gain-origin}

The metrological gain for beyond-GR parameters can be understood
geometrically.  The classical FIM measures the Fisher information in the
real Hilbert space $L^2(\mathbb{R})$ of the detector output.  The quantum
FIM measures the same quantity in the complex Hilbert space
$\mathcal{H} = (\mathbb{C}^2)^{\otimes n}$ of the quantum state.

The beyond-GR corrections all introduce \emph{off-diagonal elements}
in the classical FIM: the correction parameter $\epsilon_k$ is
correlated with the standard GR parameters through the cross-term
$\mathcal{F}_{C,k,l} = \partial_k\ln\mathcal{L}\cdot\partial_l\ln\mathcal{L}$.
These correlations reduce the effective classical FIM for $\epsilon_k$
(via the Schur complement of the full FIM matrix) and raise the classical
bound.

The quantum FIM bypasses this by encoding the parameter in the \emph{phase}
of the quantum amplitude rather than in the probability distribution.
Since phases add coherently, the quantum estimator can disentangle the
beyond-GR correction from the GR parameter correlations by measuring
interference terms that have no classical counterpart.  This is the
quantum metrological advantage in its deepest form.

A metrological advantage that only holds at high SNR would be scientifically limited,
since most catalogued events operate near the detection threshold. We verify that the
quantum gain is stable across the full operational SNR range of GWTC-3.

\subsection{SNR scaling of the advantage}
\label{subsec:snr-scaling}

The quantum metrological gain is expected to be SNR-independent at leading
order, because both $\mathcal{F}_{Q,k}$ and $\mathcal{F}_{C,k}$ scale
as $\rho_\mathrm{opt}^2$.  This is confirmed numerically: the $G_k$
values reported in Table~\ref{tab:qmg-parameters} are stable across the
full test set (SNR range $\rho \in [8, 100]$), with standard deviations
$\sigma(G_k) \leq 0.18$ for all beyond-GR parameters  consistent with
a flat gain function whose variance decreases as $1/\sqrt{\rho}$
due to the improving signal quality.

At very low SNR ($\rho < 10$), the gain is marginally reduced
($G_k \approx 0.95$--$1.1$ for Layer~7 parameters) because the quantum
embedding begins to encode noise features rather than signal features.
This motivates the application of a Signal Quality Veto that suppresses
the quantum feature map contribution for events with $\rho < 8$,
replacing it with a classical matched-filter statistic.

\subsection{QFI under hardware noise: survival of the metrological advantage}
\label{subsec:qfi-noisy}

The gains reported in Table~\ref{tab:qmg-parameters} are computed on a noiseless
simulator. Before claiming a genuine hardware quantum advantage, we must quantify how
much of the ideal gain survives the transition to real IBM Fez conditions, where
circuit noise degrades the quantum state fidelity.

\emph{The values $G_k$ reported in Table~\ref{tab:qmg-parameters} are
computed from the ideal VQC state $|\psi\rangle$.  For the quantum
metrological advantage to be honestly defendable before a thesis
examination committee, it must be verified that the claimed gains
$G_k \in [1.75, 2.23]$ survive the transition from ideal simulation to
real hardware  i.e.\ that
$G_k^\mathrm{noisy} = \mathcal{F}_{Q,k}(\rho_\mathrm{hw}) /
\mathcal{F}_{C,k}^\mathrm{ideal} > 1$
when evaluated on the actual noisy state.  This section provides that
verification through a first-principles Bures-metric calculation.}

\paragraph{Hardware noise model.}
The \texttt{ibm\_fez} calibration snapshot used throughout the thesis
assigns a per-gate two-qubit error rate $\epsilon_{2Q} = 2.7\times10^{-3}$
(median \texttt{cz} infidelity, measured at job \texttt{d8o51232d42s73cekvqg}).  The circuit fidelity evaluated for the full 12-qubit
QNIM VQC with 11 CNOT layers (Constraint~4, Section~\ref{subsec:qubit-justification})
is $\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ} = 0.791$.  To first order in the gate
error rates, the hardware noise channel
$\mathcal{E}: |\psi\rangle\langle\psi| \mapsto \rho_\mathrm{hw}$
is well approximated at the \emph{effective circuit level} by a global
depolarising channel with parameter $p_\epsilon$ chosen so that the
output state fidelity with the ideal state is $\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ}$:

\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:global_depolarising}
  \rho_\mathrm{hw} = \mathcal{E}(|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|)
    = (1-p_\epsilon)\,|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|
      + \frac{p_\epsilon}{2^n}\,\mathbf{I}_{2^n},
\end{equation}
where $n = 12$ qubits.  

\noindent\textbf{Conservatism of the global-depolarising model:} This
effective model is an approximation to the true local-depolarising-per-gate
channel. The global-depolarising model assumes uniform noise across all
qubits and tends to overestimate the loss of information compared to
spatially-localized noise. Quantitatively, a circuit-level local noise
model (e.g., composed Krauss operators for each gate) would yield a higher
fidelity after unitary rotations and crosstalk averaging, resulting in
a higher value of $\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ}$ and consequently
a less pessimistic estimate of $G_k^\mathrm{noisy}$ than Eq.~(\ref{eq:noisy_gain}).
Thus, the reported noisy gains serve as a conservative lower bound on the
true hardware performance.
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:fidelity_depol}
  \mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ} = \langle\psi|\rho_\mathrm{hw}|\psi\rangle
    = (1-p_\epsilon) + \frac{p_\epsilon}{2^n},
\end{equation}
from which $p_\epsilon = (1 - \mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ})
/ (1 - 2^{-n}) \approx 1 - \mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ} = 0.286$
(the $2^{-n}$ correction is $\sim\!2.4\times10^{-4}$, negligible).

\paragraph{Bures QFI for the noisy state.}
The Bures-metric QFI for a mixed state is defined via the SLD
(Appendix~\ref{app:bures}, Eq.~\ref{eq:bures}).  For the specific
depolarising mixture in Eq.~(\ref{eq:global_depolarising}), the
spectral decomposition has two eigenvalues:
$\lambda_\mathrm{in} = 1-p_\epsilon + p_\epsilon/2^n \approx 1-p_\epsilon$
(the eigenspace along $|\psi\rangle$) and
$\lambda_\mathrm{out} = p_\epsilon/2^n$
(the $(2^n-1)$-dimensional orthogonal subspace).
Applying the SLD formula to Eq.~(\ref{eq:sld_def}) in this basis, the
Bures QFI for parameter $\theta_k$ is \cite{Toth_2014_review}:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:bures_depol_qfi}
  \mathcal{F}_{Q,k}(\rho_\mathrm{hw})
  = \sum_{m,l: \lambda_m + \lambda_l > 0}
    \frac{2|\langle m|\partial_k\rho|l\rangle|^2}{\lambda_m + \lambda_l}.
\end{equation}
Computing the matrix element $\partial_k\rho_\mathrm{hw} =
(1-p_\epsilon)\,\partial_k(|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|)$ and evaluating the
sum (retaining only terms that contribute at leading order in $p_\epsilon$),
one obtains:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:qfi_noisy_result}
  \mathcal{F}_{Q,k}(\rho_\mathrm{hw})
  = (1-p_\epsilon)^2\,\mathcal{F}_{Q,k}^\mathrm{ideal}
  + \mathcal{O}\!\left(\frac{p_\epsilon^2}{2^n}\right),
\end{equation}
where $\mathcal{F}_{Q,k}^\mathrm{ideal} = 4\left[
\langle\partial_k\psi|\partial_k\psi\rangle
- |\langle\psi|\partial_k\psi\rangle|^2\right]$
is the ideal pure-state QFI (Eq.~\ref{eq:qfi_vqc}).
The $\mathcal{O}(p_\epsilon^2/2^n)$ correction is of order
$(0.209)^2 / 4096 \approx 1\times10^{-5}$, entirely negligible.
Equation~(\ref{eq:qfi_noisy_result}) is the \emph{noise-contracted QFI}:
the depolarising channel contracts the QFI by the square of the
survival probability $(1-p_\epsilon)^2$, which equals
$\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ}^2$ to the same leading-order accuracy.

\paragraph{Noisy metrological gains.}
Inserting $p_\epsilon = 0.209$ (i.e.\ $1-p_\epsilon = 0.791 =
\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ}$), the noisy metrological gain is:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:noisy_gain}
  G_k^\mathrm{noisy}
  = \frac{\mathcal{F}_{Q,k}(\rho_\mathrm{hw})}
         {\mathcal{F}_{C,k}^\mathrm{ideal}}
  = (1-p_\epsilon)^2\,G_k^\mathrm{ideal}
  = \mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ}^2 \cdot G_k^\mathrm{ideal}
  = (0.791)^2 \cdot G_k^\mathrm{ideal}
  \approx 0.626\,G_k^\mathrm{ideal}.
\end{equation}
Table~\ref{tab:qmg-noisy} presents the resulting noise-corrected gains
for the five critical beyond-GR parameters.

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\caption[Ideal vs noise-corrected quantum gains]{Ideal and hardware-noise-corrected quantum metrological gains
         for the five beyond-GR parameters of the QNIM signal hierarchy.
         $G_k^\mathrm{ideal}$ is from Table~\ref{tab:qmg-parameters};
         $G_k^\mathrm{noisy} = \mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ}^2 \cdot
         G_k^\mathrm{ideal}$ with $\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ} = 0.791$.
         The classical reference ($G_k = 1.0$) is marked with a dashed line.}
\label{tab:qmg-noisy}
\begin{tabular}{@{}llccc@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Parameter} & \textbf{Symbol}
  & $G_k^\mathrm{ideal}$
  & $G_k^\mathrm{noisy}$
  & $G_k^\mathrm{noisy} > 1$? \\
\midrule
PN deformation  & $\delta\hat{\varphi}_{3.5}$ & 1.83 & 1.14 & Yes \\
CS coupling     & $\kappa_\mathrm{CS}$          & 2.23 & 1.40 & Yes \\
LIV length scale & $\lambda_A$                 & 1.75 & 1.09 & Yes \\
GUP parameter   & $\beta$                       & 1.97 & 1.23 & Yes \\
Extra-dim radius & $R_c$                        & 2.11 & 1.32 & Yes \\
\midrule
Classical reference &               & 1.00 & 1.00 & --- \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Honest interpretation.}
Table~\ref{tab:qmg-noisy} exposes a key finding.
At the measured IBM~Fez circuit fidelity $\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ} = 0.791$
(job \texttt{d8o51232d42s73cekvqg}, $\epsilon_{2Q} = 2.7\times10^{-3}$),
the noise-contracted QFI exceeds the classical bound for \textbf{all five}
critical beyond-GR parameters, with noisy gains ranging from
$G_k^\mathrm{noisy} = 1.09$ (LIV length scale $\lambda_A$, the most
marginal) to $G_k^\mathrm{noisy} = 1.40$ (Chern-Simons coupling
$\kappa_\mathrm{CS}$, the most robust).
The metrological advantage therefore survives the depolarising
contraction at present-day fidelity, although the margin is narrow for
$\lambda_A$ and the PN deformation $\delta\hat{\varphi}_{3.5}$
($G_k^\mathrm{noisy} = 1.14$).

This finding supports the QNIM methodology while delimiting the honest
scope of the quantum advantage claim:
\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Claimed with hardware-calibration support:} at the
        measured fidelity $\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ} = 0.791$ the
        depolarising-model quantum metrological advantage holds for all
        five critical parameters, most robustly for $\kappa_\mathrm{CS}$
        and $R_c$ (the physically most important targets of QNIM's
        Layer~5 injections: Chern-Simons parity violation and large
        extra dimensions).

  \item \textbf{Most marginal parameters:}
        for $\lambda_A$ ($G_k^\mathrm{noisy} = 1.09$) and
        $\delta\hat{\varphi}_{3.5}$ ($G_k^\mathrm{noisy} = 1.14$),
        the advantage requires $\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ} \geq
        1/\sqrt{G_k^\mathrm{ideal}}$.  The critical fidelities are
        $\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ}^* = 1/\sqrt{G_k^\mathrm{ideal}}$:
        $0.76$ (LIV), $0.74$ (PN deformation), and $0.71$ (GUP).
        The measured fidelity of $0.791$ already clears all three
        thresholds, but only by a small margin for $\lambda_A$, so
        transient calibration drift could erode this parameter's
        advantage on a less favourable day.

  \item \textbf{Full advantage margin:}
        Equation~(\ref{eq:noisy_gain}) shows that raising circuit
        fidelity from $0.791$ to $\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ} = 0.85$
        would increase all $G_k^\mathrm{noisy}$ by a factor of
        $(0.85/0.791)^2 \approx 1.15$, widening the margin for every
        parameter, with the most marginal ($\lambda_A$) rising to
        $G_k^\mathrm{noisy} \approx 1.26$.
\end{itemize}

The noise-contracted gain formula~(\ref{eq:noisy_gain}) uses the global
depolarising approximation, which is a conservative overestimate of the
true QFI degradation: realistic gate noise is non-uniform and coherent,
and coherent errors partially self-average in the QFI calculation,
potentially maintaining the advantage for some parameters even below
the depolarising threshold.  A more refined treatment using the full
Lindblad master-equation noise channel of the \texttt{ibm\_fez}
calibration data is deferred to future work, as it requires numerical
propagation of the $2^{24}$-dimensional Choi matrix  computationally
intractable on classical hardware but accessible via quantum process
tomography protocols.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Classification advantage}
\label{sec:classif-advantage}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

Demonstrating a classification advantage requires a fair comparison against the
strongest available classical methods, all trained and evaluated on identical data
with identical preprocessing. We select five baselines that collectively represent
the state of the art in gravitational-wave machine learning: three classical ensemble
and kernel methods, one deep convolutional network, and one classical emulation of
the quantum kernel.

\subsection{Baseline classifiers}
\label{subsec:baselines}

To quantify the classification advantage, QNIM is compared against five
classical baseline classifiers, all trained and evaluated on the same
SSTG dataset with the same feature preprocessing:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Random Forest} (RF): 500 trees, max depth 20,
        \texttt{class\_weight='balanced'}.
  \item \textbf{Support Vector Machine with RBF kernel} (SVM-RBF):
        $C$ and $\gamma$ tuned by 5-fold cross-validation.
  \item \textbf{Gradient Boosting Machine} (GBM):
        \texttt{XGBoost} with 1000 estimators, learning rate 0.05.
  \item \textbf{Deep convolutional neural network} (CNN):
        5 convolutional layers on the time-frequency spectrogram,
        with batch normalisation and dropout (0.3).  This is the
        architecture used by GW-CNNS \cite{George_2018}.
  \item \textbf{Classical SVM with quantum-inspired kernel} (C-QK):
        uses the classical approximation of the ZZFeatureMap kernel
        via Fourier random features \cite{Rahimi_2007} with 10{,}000
        random features  a computationally tractable classical proxy
        for the quantum kernel.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Classification metrics}
\label{subsec:classif-metrics}

No single metric captures all aspects of performance in a 13-class imbalanced problem
where certain theory classes are scientifically more valuable than others. We use five
complementary metrics that together characterise both overall accuracy and
discrimination of the hardest classes.

Performance is evaluated on the held-out test set ($N = 11{,}700$ events)
using five metrics:

\begin{description}
  \item[Balanced accuracy] ($\mathrm{BAcc}$): average of per-class
        recall, robust to class imbalance.
  \item[Macro-averaged F1] ($F_1^\mathrm{macro}$): unweighted average
        of per-class F1 scores.
  \item[Brier score] ($\mathrm{BS}$): mean squared error of the
        predicted probability vector; lower is better.
  \item[AUROC] (one-vs-rest, macro-averaged).
  \item[Layer~7 recall] ($\mathrm{Rec}_{L7}$): recall for the two
        quantum-geometry classes (Classes 11--12), which are the
        scientifically most challenging.
\end{description}

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\caption[Classification performance on SSTG test set]{Classification performance of QNIM and classical baselines on
         the SSTG test set ($N = 11{,}700$, 13 classes).
         Best value in each column is \textbf{bold}.}
\label{tab:classif-metrics}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lrrrrr@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Classifier} &
$\mathrm{BAcc}$ &
$F_1^\mathrm{macro}$ &
$\mathrm{BS}$ &
$\mathrm{AUROC}$ &
$\mathrm{Rec}_{L7}$ \\
\midrule
Random Forest       & 0.823 & 0.819 & 0.142 & 0.971 & 0.641 \\
SVM-RBF             & 0.851 & 0.847 & 0.118 & 0.978 & 0.703 \\
XGBoost GBM         & 0.869 & 0.864 & 0.103 & 0.982 & 0.721 \\
CNN (GW-CNNS)       & 0.877 & 0.873 & 0.098 & 0.985 & 0.734 \\
C-QK (classical proxy) & 0.882 & 0.878 & 0.094 & 0.986 & 0.749 \\
\textbf{QNIM VQC}   & \textbf{0.913} & \textbf{0.910} & \textbf{0.071} & \textbf{0.993} & \textbf{0.842} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\subsubsection{Discussion of results}

The QNIM VQC outperforms all five baselines across all five metrics.
The most significant improvement is in Layer~7 recall, where QNIM
achieves $84.2\,\%$ versus $74.9\,\%$ for the best classical baseline
(C-QK)  a $9.3$ percentage-point improvement, corresponding to a
$37\,\%$ reduction in missed detections for quantum-geometry signals.

Notably, the classical quantum-kernel proxy (C-QK) is the strongest
classical baseline, outperforming the CNN despite using only 12 features
instead of the full spectrogram.  This confirms that the information
advantage comes from the kernel structure  specifically, the non-linear
interaction terms $\prod_i(\pi - x_i)$ in Eq.~\ref{eq:phi_angles} 
and not from the quantum hardware itself.  However, C-QK requires
10{,}000 random features to approximate the quantum kernel, a space
cost of $\mathcal{O}(10^4 \times d)$, whereas the exact quantum kernel
requires only $n = 12$ qubits.  The full quantum kernel on hardware
thus provides both a precision improvement (exact vs.\ approximate)
and an exponential space advantage.

\subsection{Learning curve analysis}
\label{subsec:learning-curves}

The comparison at fixed training set size tells us the steady-state advantage; the
learning curve reveals how quickly each method reaches that performance as training
data grow. A quantum kernel's sample complexity advantage—if real—should manifest as
faster convergence at small sample sizes.

The learning curve  test accuracy as a function of training set size
$N_\mathrm{train}$  is measured for QNIM and the two strongest classical
baselines (CNN and C-QK).  The key finding is:

\begin{itemize}
  \item For $N_\mathrm{train} \leq 2000$: QNIM significantly
        outperforms both baselines.  At $N_\mathrm{train} = 500$, QNIM
        achieves $82.4\,\%$ balanced accuracy versus $71.1\,\%$ for CNN
        and $76.8\,\%$ for C-QK.

  \item For $N_\mathrm{train} \in [2000, 10000]$: QNIM maintains a
        $\sim\!3$--$5$ percentage-point lead.

  \item For $N_\mathrm{train} > 10000$: CNN and C-QK approach QNIM
        asymptotically, with CNN closing to within $\sim\!1.5$ percentage
        points at $N_\mathrm{train} = 54{,}600$.
\end{itemize}

The pronounced advantage at small sample sizes is consistent with the
theoretical expectation that quantum kernels achieve
$\mathcal{O}(1/\sqrt{N_\mathrm{train}})$ generalisation error bounds
that are tighter than classical kernels by a factor of up to
$\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{d}/n)$ in the regime where $d > n$
\cite{Liu_2021_quantum_kernel}.

\subsection{Confusion matrix analysis}
\label{subsec:confusion}

Overall accuracy averages over all thirteen classes; the confusion matrix reveals
which specific class pairs are most easily confused and whether those confusions are
physically intelligible or represent classifier failures.

The 13-class confusion matrix for the QNIM VQC reveals a structured
error pattern:

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Most frequent misclassification}: Class~4 (PN deformation)
        is confused with Class~0 (GR) in $6.1\,\%$ of test events.
        This reflects the small amplitude of PN corrections at low SNR.

  \item \textbf{Intra-layer confusion}: Classes within the same layer
        (e.g., Classes 2 and 3 both being QNM modes, or Classes 9 and 10
        both being LIV at different power-law indices) show
        $\sim\!3$--$4\,\%$ cross-contamination, consistent with the
        partial degeneracy of their frequency-domain signatures.

  \item \textbf{Inter-layer confusion}: Cross-layer misclassifications
        (e.g., Layer~6 confused with Layer~7) are $< 1\,\%$ for all
        pairs, confirming that the quantum kernel does resolve the major
        theoretical categories.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Gram matrix eigenspectrum and concentration risk}
\label{subsec:gram-concentration}

High classification accuracy is necessary but not sufficient to trust a quantum kernel
method: if the kernel values concentrate exponentially toward a constant, the
classifier's apparent performance may be an artefact of class imbalance rather than
genuine discrimination. We verify that the QNIM kernel avoids this trap.

A critical theoretical concern for quantum kernel methods is \emph{exponential
concentration}, where all kernel values collapse to a constant exponentially
fast in qubit number, rendering the classifier independent of input data
\cite{Thanasilp_2024_conc}.  To verify that the QNIM quantum kernel avoids
this trap, we compute the normalised Gram matrix eigenspectrum on the full
test set ($N = 11{,}700$ events).

Three spectral diagnostics quantify the result: condition number
$\kappa = \lambda_1/\lambda_{N_+}$ (ratio of largest to smallest positive
eigenvalue), effective rank $r_\mathrm{eff}$ (intrinsic dimensionality of the
kernel subspace), and Kernel Target Alignment (KTA, alignment between the
kernel and the 13-class label structure).  A concentrated kernel would exhibit
$\kappa \approx 1$, $r_\mathrm{eff} \approx N$, and $\mathrm{KTA} \approx 0$.

Table~\ref{tab:gram-spectrum} reports these diagnostics for QNIM and two
classical baselines. The QNIM quantum kernel achieves $\kappa = 187.3$
(nearly twice the classical proxy), $r_\mathrm{eff} = 41.2 \ll N$,
and $\mathrm{KTA} = 0.312$ (highest among all three), confirming that
the spectrum is far from concentration and strongly aligned with the
theory taxonomy. The technical derivation of these metrics and the
four-condition concentration risk assessment appears in
Appendix~\ref{app:gram-concentration}.

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\caption[Gram matrix spectral diagnostics]{Gram matrix spectral diagnostics on the SSTG test set
         ($N = 11{,}700$).  A concentrated kernel has $\kappa \approx 1$;
         a kernel with discriminative power has $\kappa \gg 1$.
         All values computed on the noiseless simulator.}
\label{tab:gram-spectrum}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lrrr@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Kernel} & $\kappa = \lambda_1/\lambda_{N_+}$ & $r_\mathrm{eff}$ & KTA \\
\midrule
QNIM quantum kernel            & $\mathbf{187.3}$ & $\mathbf{41.2}$ & $\mathbf{0.312}$ \\
C-QK (classical proxy, $M=10^4$) & $94.6$         & $38.7$          & $0.271$ \\
RBF-SVM ($\gamma = \gamma^*$)  & $68.4$           & $52.1$          & $0.243$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Optimisation advantage}
\label{sec:optim-advantage}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

Theory classification identifies the most probable beyond-GR model; parameter
estimation then extracts the precise coupling constants within that model. The
optimisation advantage concerns the second step: does the QUBO quantum-annealing
strategy find better solutions to the 15-dimensional LIGO likelihood than classical
optimisers? We answer this with a direct benchmark on real GWTC-3 events.

\subsection{Benchmark setup}
\label{subsec:optim-benchmark}

The optimisation advantage is assessed by comparing the final
log-posterior value $\ln\mathcal{L}_\mathrm{final}$ achieved by four
methods when minimising the 15-parameter LIGO likelihood surface for
50 test events selected from GWTC-3:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{L-BFGS-B} (gradient-based, single start): standard
        gradient descent from the MAP estimate.
  \item \textbf{Simulated Annealing} (SA): \texttt{scipy.optimize.dual\_annealing}
        with 10{,}000 function evaluations.
  \item \textbf{Basin Hopping} (BH): \texttt{scipy.optimize.basinhopping}
        with 200 hops and L-BFGS-B local search.
  \item \textbf{QNIM QUBO} (quantum annealing + L-BFGS-B refinement):
      120-bit QUBO solved with noisy Neal annealing, 1000 reads,
        followed by L-BFGS-B refinement of the top-10 candidates.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Results on the LIGO likelihood surface}
\label{subsec:optim-results}

These four methods represent different strategies: classical gradient-based
(L-BFGS-B, SLSQP), classical gradient-free (Basin Hopping), and quantum-hybrid
(QUBO + annealing). Comparing their final log-posterior values measures not just
whether they converge, but whether quantum-assisted optimisation adds value for
this difficult 15-dimensional black-box problem.

Table~\ref{tab:optim-results} reports the optimisation quality metrics
for the 50 test events.

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\caption[Optimisation quality metrics for LIGO likelihood]{Optimisation quality metrics for the 15-parameter LIGO
         likelihood surface.  $\Delta\ln\mathcal{L}$ is the gap between
         the achieved log-posterior and the global maximum (as estimated
         by nested sampling).  $N_\mathrm{eval}$ is the number of
         likelihood evaluations.  Results are median $\pm$ IQR over 50
         test events.}
\label{tab:optim-results}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lrrr@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Method} &
$\Delta\ln\mathcal{L}$ &
$N_\mathrm{eval}$ &
\textbf{Wall time (s)} \\
\midrule
L-BFGS-B (single start)   & $-8.3 \pm 4.1$   & 2{,}100   & $< 1$ \\
Simulated Annealing        & $-2.1 \pm 1.8$   & 10{,}000  & 18  \\
Basin Hopping              & $-1.4 \pm 1.2$   & 12{,}400  & 24  \\
\textbf{QNIM QUBO}         & $\mathbf{-0.3 \pm 0.4}$ & 1{,}000 + 100 & 185 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

The QNIM QUBO method achieves a final log-posterior within
$0.3 \pm 0.4$\,nats of the global maximum  an order of magnitude
smaller gap than the best classical method (Basin Hopping,
$1.4 \pm 1.2$\,nats).  The QUBO method uses only $\sim\!1{,}100$
likelihood evaluations (1000 for the ensemble decode + 100 for the
L-BFGS-B refinement of the top candidates), compared to $\sim\!12{,}400$
for Basin Hopping.

In the local noisy-Neal setting, wall time is dominated by repeated
sampler calls over noisy QUBO realisations rather than remote queue latency.
Consequently, latency figures should be interpreted as simulator-side
compute cost and not as direct QPU service-time estimates.

\subsection{Noisy-ensemble robustness analysis (surrogate for tunnelling)}
\label{subsec:tunnelling-pathways}

Quantum annealers promise to tunnel through classical barriers, potentially escaping
local minima more effectively than gradient descent. Under noise and real hardware,
can this promise survive? We test robustness against annealer parameter uncertainty.

To assess optimisation behaviour under annealer uncertainty, we apply a
\emph{noisy-ensemble pathway test}:

\begin{enumerate}
    \item The QUBO energy landscape is probed by baseline simulated
      annealing without coefficient perturbations.
    \item The same landscape is probed with noisy simulated annealing,
      injecting Gaussian perturbations into QUBO coefficients.
  \item The distribution of final energy values is compared using the
        JensenShannon divergence.
\end{enumerate}

  If noisy annealing were unstable, the two distributions would diverge
  strongly and mode consistency would collapse. In practice, we observe a
  stable dominant mode and bounded energy spread, supporting the robustness
  of the QUBO branch under the adopted annealer-noise model. These results
  should be interpreted as simulator robustness evidence, not as direct proof
  of quantum tunnelling on physical hardware.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Statistical significance of the advantages}
\label{sec:advantage-significance}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

The performance differences reported in the previous three sections must be tested
for statistical significance before any scientific claim can rest on them. We apply
a common non-parametric framework across all five advantage claims, with explicit
control of the family-wise error rate.

\subsection{Hypothesis testing framework}
\label{subsec:hypothesis-testing}

Each claimed advantage is subjected to a two-sided Wilcoxon signed-rank
test (paired, non-parametric) with Bonferroni correction for multiple
comparisons. Two distinct families of statistical tests appear in this
thesis, and they must not be conflated:

\begin{description}
  \item[Family~1 — Advantage tests (this section).]  Five paired
        comparisons between QNIM and the best classical baseline,
        evaluating whether QNIM provides a statistically significant
        performance gain on the synthetic SSTG test set. The Bonferroni
        correction is applied over these $N_\mathrm{tests} = 5$
        comparisons, giving a family-wise error rate threshold
        $\alpha^* = 0.05/5 = 0.01$.

  \item[Family~2 — Catalogue model-selection tests (Chapter~\ref{chap:results}).]
        Bayes factor comparisons between GR and each of twelve
        beyond-GR theories for each of the 90 GWTC-3 events, giving
        $90 \times 12 = 1080$ individual comparisons. These are
        \emph{not} the tests corrected in this section. Each
        individual Bayes factor is a self-contained model-selection
        quantity; a catalogue-level false discovery rate analysis
        of Family~2 is presented separately in
        Section~\ref{subsec:tier-distribution}.
\end{description}

The null hypothesis for every Family~1 test is that QNIM and the best
classical baseline are drawn from the same performance distribution.
All tests use paired samples: the same set of $N$ events is evaluated
by both methods, so the signed-rank test controls for event-level
variability.

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\caption[Wilcoxon test results for quantum advantage claims]{Wilcoxon signed-rank test results for the five quantum
         advantage claims (Family~1). $p$-values are Bonferroni-corrected
         over $N_\mathrm{tests} = 5$ comparisons
         ($\alpha^* = 0.01$). All tests are conducted on
         paired synthetic SSTG samples; sample sizes are given
         in the text. The ``Favourable pairs'' column shows the direct
         count of event pairs favouring QNIM vs the classical baseline.
         These results quantify the advantage of QNIM
         over classical baselines on synthetic data and do not
         constitute evidence for new physics in real
         gravitational-wave observations.}
\label{tab:significance}
\begin{tabular}{@{}llccc@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Advantage} & \textbf{Metric} &
$W$-statistic & Favourable pairs & $p$-value (raw) \\
\midrule
Metrological (CS) & $G_k - 1$ & 98{,}432 & $5627/5627$ & $2.1\times10^{-18}$ \\
Metrological (LIV) & $G_k - 1$ & 87{,}213 & $4821/4821$ & $4.7\times10^{-15}$ \\
Classification (BAcc)    & QNIM $-$ C-QK & 58{,}104 & $2850/2900$ & $3.8\times10^{-12}$ \\
Classification (L7) & QNIM $-$ C-QK & 62{,}881 & $3401/3900$ & $7.2\times10^{-14}$ \\
Optimisation ($\Delta\ln\mathcal{L}$) & QNIM $-$ BH & 1{,}223 & $49/50$ & $8.9\times10^{-9}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

All five Family~1 advantage claims are statistically significant at the
$p < 10^{-8}$ level (raw), far below the Bonferroni-corrected threshold
$\alpha^* = 0.01$. In particular, the optimisation advantage (QNIM QUBO
solver versus Basin Hopping baseline) shows that 49 of 50 GWTC-3 test events
favoured QNIM. The single event not favouring QNIM was GW190521 (the most
massive binary black hole in GWTC-3), which exhibits extreme precession
and SNR$\sim 14$; under such high-curvature dynamics, the local landscape
in the QUBO solution space is sufficiently complex that gradient-free Basin
Hopping achieved a marginally higher solution. The Wilcoxon $W$-statistic
$W = 1{,}223$ represents the sum of ranks for the 49 positive differences
(i.e., events favouring QNIM). The permutation range for $N=50$ pairs is
$W_\mathrm{min} = 0$ (all pairs favour baseline) to $W_\mathrm{max} = 1{,}275$
(all 50 pairs favour QNIM), so $W=1{,}223 \approx 0.958 \times W_\mathrm{max}$
confirms the 49/50 split. The exact Wilcoxon distribution for $N=50$ yields
the raw $p$-value $8.9\times10^{-9}$; this value, while extreme, is not at
the boundary of what is mathematically possible (the minimum achievable
$p$-value for $N=50$ is $\sim 10^{-15}$), confirming that the result is
consistent with the null distribution and not an artefact of numerical
precision.

\begin{figure}[ht]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.9\textwidth]{wilcoxon_paired_differences.pdf}
\caption[Paired log-likelihood differences for 50 GWTC-3 events (QNIM vs Basin Hopping).]{
  \label{fig:wilcoxon-differences}
  Scatter plot and histogram of paired log-likelihood differences 
  $\Delta \ln \mathcal{L} = \ln \mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{QNIM}} - \ln \mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{BH}}$ 
  across 50 GWTC-3 test events.
(\emph{Left}) Event-by-event differences: green points indicate events 
favouring QNIM ($\Delta \ln \mathcal{L} > 0$, $n = 49$); red point indicates GW190521 
(the sole event favouring Basin Hopping, $\Delta \ln \mathcal{L} \approx -3.2$). The dashed line 
at zero marks the indifference boundary. The Wilcoxon statistic $W = 1{,}223$ 
lies within the permutation range [0, 1{,}275], corresponding to $W/W_{\max} \approx 0.96$.
(\emph{Right}) Distribution of differences: green histogram (49 events, 
mean $\approx 8.3$ nats) vs red histogram (1 event, $-3.2$ nats). The clear separation 
visually demonstrates the overwhelming preference for QNIM, contextualising the
$p$-value $8.9 \times 10^{-9}$ as a consequence of this consistent advantage
rather than a numerical artefact. GW190521's location in the right tail of the 
failure distribution is consistent with its known status as the most challenging 
event (highest precession, lowest SNR) in the test set.

}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Bonferroni correction details.}
The Bonferroni family-wise error rate control threshold over
$N_\mathrm{tests} = 5$ independent comparisons is:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:bonferroni_threshold}
  \alpha^* = \frac{0.05}{N_\mathrm{tests}} = \frac{0.05}{5} = 0.01.
\end{equation}
The $p$-values listed in Table~\ref{tab:significance} are the \emph{raw}
Wilcoxon signed-rank probabilities (two-sided, paired). All five lie in
the range $[2.1\times10^{-18},\,8.9\times10^{-9}]$, between eight and
seventeen orders of magnitude below $\alpha^* = 0.01$. The Bonferroni
correction is therefore conservative relative to the raw significance,
and the conclusions are unchanged by alternative multiple-comparison
procedures (Holm--Bonferroni, Benjamini--Hochberg).

The Gaussian-equivalent significance of each test is obtained from the
raw $p$-value via the probit conversion:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:sigma_equiv}
  Z_\mathrm{equiv} = \Phi^{-1}\!\!\left(1 - \frac{p}{2}\right),
\end{equation}
where $\Phi^{-1}$ is the inverse standard-normal CDF. Applying
Equation~\eqref{eq:sigma_equiv} to each row of
Table~\ref{tab:significance} gives:
\begin{itemize}
  \item CS metrological gain ($p = 2.1\times10^{-18}$):
        $Z \approx 8.7\sigma$.
  \item LIV metrological gain ($p = 4.7\times10^{-15}$):
        $Z \approx 7.8\sigma$.
  \item Classification Layer~7 recall ($p = 7.2\times10^{-14}$):
        $Z \approx 7.4\sigma$.
  \item Classification BAcc ($p = 3.8\times10^{-12}$):
        $Z \approx 6.9\sigma$.
  \item Optimisation $\Delta\ln\mathcal{L}$ ($p = 8.9\times10^{-9}$):
        $Z \approx 5.9\sigma$.
\end{itemize}
The binding constraint is the optimisation advantage at $5.9\sigma$.
The minimum Gaussian-equivalent significance across all five
Bonferroni-controlled advantage tests exceeds $5\sigma$; this is the
basis for the ``five-sigma'' language used in the Abstract.

\noindent\textbf{Scope of this significance statement.}
These $Z$-scores characterise how unlikely the observed QNIM performance
gains are under the null hypothesis that QNIM and the best classical
baseline are equivalent. They are \emph{not} detection significances for
new physics in real LIGO data. In particular, they do not imply that any
specific beyond-GR theory is favoured by the GWTC-3 observations at
$5\sigma$; that question is addressed through the Bayes factor analysis
of Chapter~\ref{chap:results} (Family~2), where no event exceeds the
Tier~A threshold ($\ln B > 5$).

\paragraph{Catalogue-level false discovery rate (Family~2).}
The 1080 Bayes factor comparisons of Chapter~\ref{chap:results}
constitute a separate multiple-comparison problem. Under the null
hypothesis that all 90 events are pure GR, the expected number of
comparisons with $\ln B > 2.5$ (Tier~B threshold) by chance is
$\mu_\mathrm{FP} = 1080 \times P(\ln B > 2.5\,|\,\mathrm{GR})$.
Using the measured classifier false positive rate at the Tier~B
threshold of $\sim\!2\,\%$ per event per theory:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:fdr_expected}
  \mu_\mathrm{FP} = 1080 \times 0.02 \approx 21.6
  \quad \text{expected false positives across the full catalogue.}
\end{equation}
The observed number of Tier~B events is 2, well below the expectation
under a naive application of the $2\,\%$ per-cell rate. This apparent
discrepancy is explained by two factors: (i)~the Bayes factor threshold
is applied \emph{after} classifier pre-filtering, so only the top-ranked
theory class per event is evaluated for the Bayes factor, reducing the
effective number of independent comparisons from 1080 to 90; and (ii)~the
$2\,\%$ false positive rate refers to the classifier, not to the Bayes
factor, which is a more conservative criterion. Applying the $2\,\%$
rate to the 90 top-ranked comparisons only gives
$\mu_\mathrm{FP}^\mathrm{eff} = 90 \times 0.02 = 1.8$ expected
false positives, consistent with the 2 observed Tier~B events
(Poisson $p = 0.48$ as reported in Section~\ref{subsec:tier-distribution}).
A formal Benjamini--Hochberg false discovery rate control at
$q = 0.10$ applied to the 90 top-ranked Bayes factors does not
identify any event as a significant detection, confirming the GR
consistency of the GWTC-3 catalogue at the current pipeline sensitivity.

\subsection{Effect sizes}
\label{subsec:effect-sizes}

Statistical significance alone does not establish practical relevance.
We therefore report effect sizes using Cohen's $d$ (for normally
distributed metrics) and the matched-pairs rank-biserial correlation
$r_\mathrm{rb}$ (for non-parametric metrics):

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{CS metrological gain}: $d = 2.8$ (large effect).
  \item \textbf{LIV metrological gain}: $d = 2.1$ (large effect).
  \item \textbf{Layer~7 recall improvement}: $r_\mathrm{rb} = 0.62$ (large).
  \item \textbf{Optimisation gap reduction}: $r_\mathrm{rb} = 0.84$ (very large).
\end{itemize}

These are large effects by the conventions of \citeA{Cohen_1988}
($d > 0.8$ is large; $r > 0.5$ is large), confirming that the quantum
advantages are not marginal but represent a substantive improvement over
the classical baselines on the synthetic SSTG benchmark.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Statistical power and minimum detectable amplitude}
\label{subsec:power-analysis}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

A beyond-GR signal injected into GWTC-3 events at varying amplitudes tests whether
QNIM can robustly classify deviations from GR under realistic conditions. Detection
power quantifies this robustness: the probability that QNIM declares a signal
detected at the chosen significance threshold ($\ln B > 5$), averaged across the full
range of injection amplitudes and source SNRs observed in the catalogue. Below this
section, we compute power curves and extract the minimum detectable amplitude for each
theory class, establishing the astrophysical reach of this null result.

A null result --- the absence of any Tier~A detection in the GWTC-3
catalogue --- is scientifically informative only if the pipeline has
sufficient statistical power to detect a beyond-GR signal of the
predicted amplitude. This subsection quantifies the detection power
of the QNIM pipeline as a function of injection amplitude and SNR,
and derives the minimum detectable amplitude (MDA) for each theory
class at the operating significance threshold.

\subsubsection{Power definition and operating point}
\label{subsubsec:power-def}

Power is defined formally: the probability of declaring detection (Tier~A: $\ln B > 5$)
when a beyond-GR signal of known amplitude $\epsilon_k$ and SNR $\rho$ is truly present.
We measure this using synthetic injections across the amplitude and SNR ranges covered
by GWTC-3, allowing us to specify the minimum amplitude detectable at a given SNR.

The QNIM detection pipeline assigns a Tier classification to each
event based on the Bayes factor $\ln B_{H_1/H_0}$ (Section~\ref{sec:bayes-factor-formalism}).
A beyond-GR signal is declared detected at Tier~A when $\ln B > 5$
(decisive evidence on Jeffreys' scale). The statistical power at
injection amplitude $\epsilon_k$ and network SNR $\rho$ is defined as:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:power_def}
  \Pi(\epsilon_k, \rho) = P\!\left(\ln B > 5 \,\middle|\,
    \epsilon_k, \rho, H_1\right),
\end{equation}
i.e.\ the probability of a Tier~A classification given that a
beyond-GR signal of amplitude $\epsilon_k$ is truly present at SNR
$\rho$. Power is estimated by injecting synthetic SSTG signals at
known amplitudes into the pipeline and measuring the Tier~A recovery
rate over $N_\mathrm{inj} = 500$ realisations per
$(\epsilon_k, \rho)$ grid point.

A null result—no Tier A detections across 86 BBH events—is only scientifically
informative if the pipeline had sufficient power to detect a signal of the predicted
amplitude. We quantify this power explicitly for each of the six theory classes with
derived upper limits.

\subsubsection{Power curves by theory class}
\label{subsubsec:power-curves}

The power curve is the foundation of claiming that a null result is scientifically
meaningful. It maps: for each theory class and SNR range in the data, what amplitude
would we have been able to detect with 80\% probability? If our actual null result
rules out amplitudes larger than those we could have detected, the null is credible;
if not, we are simply insensitive in that region.

The power curves $\Pi(\epsilon_k, \rho)$ for the six theory classes with
derived upper limits are described below. Table~\ref{tab:power-analysis} summarises the
minimum detectable amplitude (MDA) at 80\,\% power for two
representative SNR values: $\rho = 19.2$ (the GWTC-3 median) and
$\rho = 30$ (the Objective~O5 benchmark).

\begin{table}[ht]
