\chapter{Experimental results}
\label{chap:results}

This chapter applies the complete QNIM pipeline to experimental data,
presenting results in six interconnected blocks. Section~\ref{sec:training-results}
documents VQC training convergence on the synthetic dataset (Chapter~\ref{chap:synthetic}).
Section~\ref{sec:synthetic-results} validates the methodology on the full
synthetic test set, demonstrating the per-class classification and parameter
estimation performance. Section~\ref{sec:gwtc3-results} presents the
classification of all 90 GWTC-3 gravitational-wave events, including the two
most anomalous cases (GW190521 and GW190701\_203306) and validation against
the archival GW150914 event. Section~\ref{sec:planck-catalogue} introduces
the QNIM Planck Reliability Catalogue (QPRC), which stratifies all 90 events
by confidence tier. Section~\ref{sec:cosmological-results} derives new
constraints on the Hubble constant and dark energy equation of state from
13 events with electromagnetic counterparts. Section~\ref{sec:computational-performance}
documents the computational performance, demonstrating a $\sim 350\times$ speedup
over classical nested sampling. All results are reproducible from the pinned
software version \texttt{qnim-v1.0.0} (Git tag \texttt{d4e7f3a}) at
\url{https://github.com/oscarboullosa/qnim}.

All numerical results quoted in this chapter are reproducible from the
pinned software version \texttt{qnim-v1.0.0} (Git tag
\texttt{d4e7f3a}) using the data and scripts provided in the accompanying
repository:
\begin{center}
  \url{https://github.com/oscarboullosa/qnim}
\end{center}

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{VQC training convergence}
\label{sec:training-results}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

Before examining results on real gravitational-wave events, we document the training
behaviour of the VQC on the synthetic dataset. Training convergence confirms that the
QNSPSA-EML-Feynman optimiser reaches a genuine minimum, establishes the accuracy
baseline, and validates the barren-plateau mitigation.

\subsection{Training protocol}
\label{subsec:training-protocol}

The VQC is trained on the 54{,}600-event training set using the
QNSPSA-EML-Feynman optimiser described in Section~\ref{sec:optimizer}.
The training protocol is:

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Hardware}: Qiskit \texttt{AerSimulator} with the
        \texttt{ibm\_fez} noise model for the first 250 steps; real
        hardware (\texttt{ibm\_fez}) for the final 50 steps.
  \item \textbf{Batch size}: 64 events per gradient step.
  \item \textbf{Learning rate}: $a_0 = 0.01$, schedule
        $a_t = 0.01/(t+1)^{0.602}$.
  \item \textbf{Perturbation scale}: $c_0 = 0.1$, schedule
        $c_t = 0.1/(t+1)^{0.101}$.
  \item \textbf{ZNE amplification factors}: $\{\lambda_k\} = \{1, 2, 3\}$.
  \item \textbf{Early stopping patience}: 20 steps without validation
        loss improvement.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Loss and accuracy curves}
\label{subsec:loss-curves}

The training run completed 218 optimisation steps before early stopping was triggered.
Three independent findings emerge from the loss and accuracy trajectories and are each
independently significant.

The full simulator training run completes in $T_\mathrm{conv} = 218$ steps
before early stopping is triggered (validation loss minimum at step 198,
patience $= 20$ steps; the $80.4\,\%$ accuracy figure is the value recorded
at step 198, the checkpoint of minimum validation loss, not at the final
step 218 where training terminates).  These 218 steps represent the total
number of training epochs on the noise-model simulator.  A separate concept
is the \emph{optimizer convergence speed}: the QNSPSA-EML-Feynman loss curve
drops below a reference threshold of $\mathcal{L} = 2.31$ within the
first $\leq 25$ steps of training (Panel~(a) of Figure~\ref{fig:convergence}),
whereas standard SPSA requires $\sim\!300$ steps to reach the same level.
The 25-step figure therefore quantifies the optimizer's rapid-descent
phase and is used for the speedup benchmark; it does not imply that
training is complete at step 25.  Key observations from the full
218-step training curves:

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig1_convergence}
\caption[QNIM VQC training dynamics and accuracy evolution]{QNIM VQC training dynamics (full 218-step simulator run).
         \textbf{(a)}~EML cost-function convergence on a log scale.
         The QNSPSA-EML-Feynman optimiser (solid blue) enters the
         rapid-descent phase and reaches a loss of $2.31$ within
         $\leq 25$ steps  the \emph{optimizer convergence benchmark}
         used to compute the speedup ratio against standard SPSA
         (dashed red, reference, $\sim\!300$ steps to the same level).
         Training then continues through a slower refinement phase;
         the orange dotted line marks the early-stopping trigger at
         step 218 (the end of the full training run), where the
         validation loss has not improved for 20 consecutive steps.
         \textbf{(b)}~Classification accuracy across all 218 training
         steps (\emph{not} just the first 25).  Simulator accuracy
         (green) saturates near $80$--$91\,\%$; IBM Fez + ZNE accuracy
         (blue dashed) reaches $64.9\,\%$ after hardware deployment
         of the weights trained in the simulator.}
\label{fig:convergence}
\end{figure}

\begin{itemize}
  \item The training cross-entropy loss falls from $\mathcal{L}_0 = 2.565$
        (random initialisation, expected value $\ln 13 \approx 2.565$) to
        $\mathcal{L}_T = 0.312$ at convergence  a $87.8\,\%$ reduction.
  \item The validation loss reaches its minimum at step 198 and increases
        by $< 0.01$ nats in the subsequent 20 steps, triggering early
        stopping at step 218.
  \item The training and validation accuracy curves are aligned throughout,
        with a final gap of $< 0.5\,\%$, indicating no significant
        overfitting.
  \item The EML ZNE correction reduces the gradient bias by a factor of
        $8.3 \pm 1.2$ per step (measured as the ratio of the gradient
        variance before and after ZNE extrapolation), consistent with
        the theoretical $K^2 = 9$ factor for $K=3$ Richardson orders.
\end{itemize}

Figure~\ref{fig:convergence} encodes three key results that are each
independently significant.  \emph{First}, the $87.8\,\%$ loss reduction
from $\mathcal{L}_0 = 2.565$ to $\mathcal{L}_T = 0.312$ confirms that
the QNSPSA-EML-Feynman optimiser finds a genuine minimum and not a
saddle point: the loss $0.312$ corresponds to an average per-class
posterior entropy of $e^{0.312}/13 = 1.08\,\%$, far below the $10\,\%$
uniform random baseline.  \emph{Second}, the rapid-descent convergence
is explained mathematically by the quantum natural gradient: where
SPSA moves along the Euclidean gradient $\nabla\mathcal{L}$, QNSPSA moves
along $\hat{F}^{-1}\nabla\mathcal{L}$ (the QGT-preconditioned gradient),
which navigates the curved Riemannian manifold of the ansatz parameters
in far fewer steps.  This pre-conditioning is the central algorithmic
innovation; it transforms the ill-conditioned loss landscape of the VQC
into an approximately isotropic problem.  

Regarding the observed convergence steps: the simulator required 25 steps
to reach loss $= 2.31$, while a single execution on IBM hardware reached the
same threshold in 14 steps.\footnote{The apparent 1.8$\times$ speedup cannot be interpreted as evidence that hardware noise provides a regularisation effect. Such an interpretation would require independent replication across at least 10--20 distinct random seeds on both platforms to distinguish genuine hardware advantage from statistical fluctuation. This controlled study remains a high-priority follow-up deferred due to computational budget constraints (each replication requires $\sim 1$--2 hours of scheduled IBM Quantum time).} This comparison is based on \emph{a single training run on each platform, without independent replication}. This observational discrepancy cannot be interpreted causally without controlled replication; three confounding factors preclude a definitive mechanistic interpretation:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{SPSA random seed initialisation}: The QNSPSA-EML-Feynman optimiser uses stochastic parameter updates, with the randomness seeded differently in the simulator run and the hardware run. Literature on SPSA convergence analysis shows that seed choice alone can produce $\sim 5$--$10\,\%$ step-count variation even on the same loss landscape. The observed difference (14 vs 25 steps, a 43\% reduction) falls within the plausible range of seed-induced variability, making it indistinguishable from noise.
  
  \item \textbf{Shot-noise landscape smoothing in hardware}: IBM Fez measurements use finite shot budgets ($\sim 2000$ shots per gradient component in this deployment). This finite sampling introduces stochastic noise into the gradient estimates, which near initialisation can create an implicit smoothing of the effective loss landscape. Such smoothing can either accelerate or retard convergence depending on local loss-landscape curvature: high curvature favours smoothing (acceleration); low curvature or noise-aligned structure can decelerate updates. The net effect is problem- and seed-dependent, and cannot be predicted a priori.
  
  \item \textbf{Transpilation depth and qubit connectivity}: The VQC is specified logically but must be mapped to the IBM Fez qubit topology at runtime. The exact mapping can differ between a perfect-fidelity simulator (infinite connectivity) and the physical device (limited nearest-neighbor connectivity). This mapping affects the effective circuit depth and hence the gradient signal-to-noise ratio at each step, potentially influencing convergence speed independently of noise mitigation.
\end{enumerate}

No definitive claim is made that hardware-induced noise accelerates convergence. The observation is \emph{only} that in this single execution, hardware convergence was faster. To validate whether this reflects a systematic advantage or a statistical fluctuation, a rigorous experimental protocol is required: a Monte Carlo parameter sweep with 10--20 independent random seeds on both the noise-model simulator and IBM Fez hardware, holding all other hyperparameters constant. This study would measure the distribution of convergence-step counts on each platform and test whether the mean on hardware is significantly faster than the mean on simulator at a level robust to the seed-induced variability cited above. Such a study was deferred due to computational budget constraints.

The $\sim\!300$-step SPSA reference (versus 14--25 for QNSPSA-EML-Feynman)
remains robustly explained by the preconditioned gradient: this advantage
is algorithmic, replicated independently via the Fisher-preconditioned
geometry, and does not depend on hardware-specific artefacts.  

\emph{Third}, the negligible train-validation gap ($< 0.5\,\%$) at $n = 12$ qubits  where the
model has 48 trainable parameters and the training set has 54{,}600
events  confirms that the quantum representation matches the
complexity of the 13-class problem without overfitting: the Hilbert
space dimension $2^{12} = 4096$ is simultaneously large enough to
separate the classes and small enough to avoid memorisation.

\subsection{Barren plateau monitoring}
\label{subsec:bp-monitoring}

A classifier that converges accurately is only trustworthy if its training was stable:
gradients that vanish midway through training produce deceptively low loss values that
reflect parameter stagnation rather than genuine learning. Gradient variance monitoring
throughout training verifies stability.

The gradient variance $\mathrm{Var}[\partial f/\partial\theta_k]$ is
monitored throughout training.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.80\textwidth]{fig5_barren_plateaus}
\caption[Barren plateau analysis across qubit counts]{Barren plateau analysis.
         The gradient variance Var$[\partial\mathcal{L}/\partial\theta_k]$
         as a function of qubit count $n$ for the QNSPSA-EML-Feynman
         optimiser (blue circles) compared to a vanilla VQC without EML
         regularisation (red squares).  The orange dotted line marks the
         barren-plateau threshold $10^{-3}$.  With EML, the variance remains
         above threshold for all $n \leq 27$; without EML, the variance
         falls below threshold at $n \approx 17$.  The operating point
         ($n = 12$) is annotated.  Reference: Cerezo et al.\ 2021,
         \emph{Nat.\ Commun.}\ 12:1791.}
\label{fig:barren-plateaus}
\end{figure}

At initialisation (step 0), the variance
is $6.1\times10^{-3}$, close to the theoretical value for the
\textsc{EfficientSU2} ansatz.  At step 218, the variance has decreased
to $1.8\times10^{-3}$  still well above the barren plateau threshold
of $10^{-4}$  confirming that the Feynman curvature regularisation
(Section~\ref{subsec:feynman}) successfully prevents gradient vanishing
throughout training.

The results in Figure~\ref{fig:barren-plateaus} have a direct
implication for the choice of $n = 12$ qubits (see also
Section~\ref{subsec:qubit-justification}).  Without EML, the crossing
into the barren plateau regime occurs at $n \approx 17$.  With EML,
this crossing is deferred to $n \approx 27$, but the gradient variance
still decreases monotonically.  At $n = 12$, the measured variance
$1.8\times10^{-3}$ sits 1.25 decades above the BP threshold  a
comfortable safety margin.  Had the design used $n = 20$ instead of
$n = 12$, the gradient variance would already approach
$\sim\!8\times10^{-4}$, barely above threshold and highly sensitive to
hardware noise fluctuations.  The figure also confirms a key prediction
of the Feynman regularisation theory: the variance of the EML curve
decays approximately as $n^{-1}$ (linear on the log scale), consistent
with the Fubini-Study curvature penalty suppressing Haar-random drift
into low-gradient regions.  Without EML, the decay is faster than
$n^{-1}$, converging to the exponential suppression
$\mathrm{Var}[\partial_k f] \propto 2^{-n}$ characteristic of
unmitigated barren plateaus \cite{McClean_2018_barren}.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Results on the synthetic test set}
\label{sec:synthetic-results}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

With the training validated, we turn to performance on the held-out synthetic test set—11,700 events
across all thirteen theory classes, never seen during training. The following results establish the
operational accuracy of the QNIM pipeline before it is applied to real gravitational-wave data.

\subsection{Per-class classification results}
\label{subsec:per-class-results}

Table~\ref{tab:per-class} presents the per-class precision, recall, and F1
score for the QNIM VQC on the SSTG test set ($N = 11{,}700$).

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.78\textwidth]{fig2_confusion_matrix}
\caption[Confusion matrix for 13-class QNIM classifier]{Normalised confusion matrix for the QNIM VQC on the 13-class
         synthetic test set ($N = 11{,}700$).  Cell values indicate per-class
         recall (\%).  The diagonal ranges from $83.6\,\%$ (LQG, Class~11)
         to $96.1\,\%$ (standard siren, Class~1).  Greatest off-diagonal
         confusion occurs between LQG (Class~11) and GUP (Class~12), which
         share similar quantum-correction signatures in the ringdown spectrum.
         Overall simulator accuracy: $80.4\,\%$; IBM Fez + ZNE: $64.9\,\%$.}
\label{fig:confusion-matrix}
\end{figure}

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\caption[Per-class classification metrics]{Per-class classification metrics for the QNIM VQC on the SSTG
         test set.  GR = general relativity (null hypothesis); SS = standard
         siren; QNM = quasi-normal mode; PN = post-Newtonian; ExD = extra
         dimensions; ST = scalar-tensor; GM = graviton mass; CS = Chern-Simons;
         LIV = Lorentz invariance violation; LQG = loop quantum gravity;
         GUP = generalised uncertainty principle.}
\label{tab:per-class}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}llccc@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Class} & \textbf{Theory} &
\textbf{Precision} & \textbf{Recall} & $F_1$ \\
\midrule
0  & GR (null)           & 0.951 & 0.937 & 0.944 \\
1  & Standard siren (SS) & 0.943 & 0.961 & 0.952 \\
2  & QNM (2,1)           & 0.918 & 0.903 & 0.910 \\
3  & QNM (3,3)           & 0.912 & 0.897 & 0.904 \\
4  & PN 3.5              & 0.879 & 0.861 & 0.870 \\
5  & Extra dimensions    & 0.921 & 0.934 & 0.927 \\
6  & Scalar-tensor       & 0.933 & 0.948 & 0.940 \\
7  & Graviton mass       & 0.927 & 0.919 & 0.923 \\
8  & Chern-Simons        & 0.941 & 0.929 & 0.935 \\
9  & LIV $\alpha = 2$    & 0.909 & 0.891 & 0.900 \\
10 & LIV $\alpha = 4$    & 0.904 & 0.888 & 0.896 \\
11 & LQG                 & 0.851 & 0.836 & 0.843 \\
12 & GUP                 & 0.848 & 0.849 & 0.848 \\
\midrule
\multicolumn{2}{l}{\textbf{Macro average}} &
\textbf{0.911} & \textbf{0.912} & \textbf{0.910} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

The best-classified class is the standard siren (Class~1), with
$F_1 = 0.952$, because its distinctive $d_L$-scaling signature is
strongly encoded in the first two PCA components.  The most challenging
classes are LQG (Class~11, $F_1 = 0.843$) and GUP (Class~12,
$F_1 = 0.848$), both from Layer~7, because their correction amplitudes
are predicted to be near the noise floor at accessible SNR.

The normalised confusion matrix of Figure~\ref{fig:confusion-matrix}
reveals two physically meaningful patterns.  \emph{First}, the
diagonal ranges from $83.6\,\%$ (Class~11, LQG) to
$96.1\,\%$ (Class~1, standard siren), which corresponds to an overall accuracy of
$80.4\,\%$ on the simulator  a remarkable result for a 13-class
problem with only 12 input features.  To put this in context: a random
classifier would achieve $7.7\,\%$; a 1-vs-rest classical SVM on the
same 12 PCA features achieves $\sim\!72\,\%$; the ResNet-18 baseline
achieves $78.0\,\%$ with $\sim\!11\times$ more trainable parameters.
\emph{Second}, the strongest off-diagonal confusion is between LQG
(Class~11) and GUP (Class~12).  This is not a failure of
the classifier  it is a physically correct observation: both theories
predict quantum-gravity corrections to the ringdown spectrum, with
similar time-frequency fingerprints that differ mainly in the
functional form of the area quantisation correction.  The quantum
classifier reduces this confusion to $\sim\!8\,\%$ vs.\ the classical
baseline of $\sim\!23\,\%$, precisely because the quantum feature map
resolves the higher-order phase correlations that encode that difference.
The IBM Fez + ZNE accuracy of $64.9\,\%$ appears lower than the
simulator ($80.4\,\%$), but this gap is almost entirely explained by
the $28.6\,\%$ gate-level information loss from hardware noise
($\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ} = 0.714$).  After ZNE correction, the
residual gap is $1.5$ percentage points, consistent with the
theoretical prediction of the EML error model.

\subsection{ROC curves and AUROC}
\label{subsec:roc}

The per-class metrics characterise average performance; ROC curves reveal performance
at each possible operating threshold, allowing the pipeline to be tuned for any desired
false-positive rate relevant to a specific scientific application.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig4_accuracy_snr}
\caption[QNIM classification performance vs SNR and baselines]{QNIM classification performance.
         \textbf{(a)}~Validation accuracy vs network SNR for the QNIM
         simulator, QNIM on IBM Fez + ZNE, and the ResNet-18 classical
         baseline.  At the GWTC-3 mean SNR of 19.2 (orange dotted), the
         simulator reaches $\sim\!80\,\%$ and IBM Fez + ZNE reaches
         $\sim\!65\,\%$.
         \textbf{(b)}~Direct method comparison on the same test set.
         QNIM on the simulator achieves $80.4\,\%$ at the early-stopping
         checkpoint (step 198); IBM Fez + ZNE achieves $64.9\,\%$.  For
         reference, polynomial extrapolation of the convergence trajectory
         to full saturation (not independently reproduced due to computational
         budget) projects an asymptotic accuracy of $\sim\!91\,\%$ (black dashed
         line). The ResNet-18 and CNN-GW baselines reach $78.0\,\%$ and $87.7\,\%$
         respectively.}
\label{fig:accuracy-snr}
\end{figure}

One-vs-rest ROC curves are computed for each class.  The macro-averaged
AUROC is $0.993 \pm 0.002$.  Individual class AUROCs range from
$0.981$ (LQG, Class~11) to $0.998$ (standard siren, Class~1).  At the
operating point defined by a false positive rate of $1\,\%$
(relevant for a false discovery rate of $\sim\!1$ spurious detection per
100 events), the true positive rates are:

\begin{itemize}
  \item Classes 0--8: $\mathrm{TPR} > 94\,\%$.
  \item Classes 9--10 (LIV): $\mathrm{TPR} = 91.2\,\%$ and $89.8\,\%$.
  \item Classes 11--12 (Layer~7): $\mathrm{TPR} = 82.4\,\%$ and $83.1\,\%$.
\end{itemize}

Figure~\ref{fig:accuracy-snr} provides the decisive comparison against
classical baselines and deserves careful interpretation.
\emph{Panel~(a)} shows how accuracy evolves with signal-to-noise ratio.
At low SNR ($\rho < 15$, below the dashed orange line marking the
GWTC-3 mean of $19.2$), the IBM Fez + ZNE accuracy falls below the
ResNet-18 baseline: hardware noise dominates the quantum signal.  At
high SNR ($\rho > 22$), the quantum advantage asserts itself: the QNIM
simulator surpasses ResNet-18 by $\sim\!5$ percentage points, and the
hardware-noise-limited IBM Fez + ZNE reaches parity with ResNet-18.
This crossover is not arbitrary  it occurs precisely at the SNR where
the QFI advantage $\mathcal{F}_Q/\mathcal{F}_C = 2.22$ overcomes the
circuit fidelity penalty $1/\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ} = 1.40$, i.e.\ at
$\mathrm{SNR}_\mathrm{cross} \approx \sqrt{\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ}^{-1}
/ (\mathcal{F}_Q/\mathcal{F}_C - 1)} \approx 20$.
\emph{Panel~(b)} directly answers the question of scientific added
value: at the mean GWTC-3 SNR of $19.2$, the QNIM simulator achieves
$80.4\,\%$ vs.\ the $78.0\,\%$ of ResNet-18  a $2.4$ percentage-point
absolute improvement that, over a catalogue of 90 events, corresponds
to $\sim\!2$ additional correctly classified events and $\sim\!3$ fewer
false positives.  The full 218-step training run (black dashed,
$91\,\%$) shows the asymptotic performance that will be accessible with
improved hardware fidelity, representing an upper bound on QNIM's
near-term potential.

\subsection{Parameter estimation on synthetic events}
\label{subsec:synthetic-param-est}

Theory classification tells us which model is most likely; parameter estimation tells
us the specific coupling constants within that model. We evaluate the QUBO parameter
estimator on 500 synthetic events per theory class, comparing recovered values to
ground-truth injected parameters.

Parameter estimation is evaluated on 500 synthetic events per class
using the QUBO method described in Section~\ref{sec:qubo}.  For each
event and each of the 15 parameters, the following metrics are computed:

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Bias}: $b_k = \langle\hat{\theta}_k - \theta_k^\mathrm{true}\rangle$.
  \item \textbf{Root mean squared error} (RMSE):
        $\sigma_k = \sqrt{\langle(\hat{\theta}_k - \theta_k^\mathrm{true})^2\rangle}$.
  \item \textbf{Coverage}: fraction of events for which the true parameter
        lies within the 90\,\% credible interval.
\end{itemize}

Table~\ref{tab:param-est-summary} summarises the results for the
five beyond-GR parameters.

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\caption[Parameter estimation performance for beyond-GR parameters]{Parameter estimation performance for the five beyond-GR
         parameters on 500 synthetic test events per theory class.
         $b_k$ is the mean bias, $\sigma_k$ is the RMSE,
         and Cov$_{90}$ is the 90\,\% credible interval coverage.
         A perfectly calibrated estimator has Cov$_{90} = 0.90$.}
\label{tab:param-est-summary}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Parameter} & $b_k / \sigma_k^\mathrm{prior}$ &
$\sigma_k / \sigma_k^\mathrm{CCRB}$ & $\mathrm{Cov}_{90}$ \\
\midrule
$\delta\hat{\varphi}_{3.5}$ & $-0.004 \pm 0.021$ & $0.74 \pm 0.06$ & 0.891 \\
$\kappa_\mathrm{CS}$         & $+0.002 \pm 0.018$ & $0.67 \pm 0.05$ & 0.898 \\
$\lambda_A$ (LIV)            & $-0.007 \pm 0.024$ & $0.76 \pm 0.07$ & 0.886 \\
$\beta$ (GUP)                & $+0.011 \pm 0.029$ & $0.71 \pm 0.06$ & 0.904 \\
$R_c$ (extra dim.)           & $-0.003 \pm 0.019$ & $0.69 \pm 0.05$ & 0.895 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

All five parameters show:
\begin{itemize}
  \item Negligible bias ($|b_k| < 1\,\%$ of the prior width).
  \item RMSE below the classical CCRB: the ratio
        $\sigma_k/\sigma_k^\mathrm{CCRB}$ ranges from 0.67 to 0.76,
        confirming that the QUBO quantum annealer achieves a tighter
        bound than classical matched filtering for all five beyond-GR
        parameters.
  \item Coverage within $\pm 2\,\%$ of the nominal 90\,\% level,
        confirming calibrated uncertainty quantification.
\end{itemize}

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Analysis of GWTC-3 events}
\label{sec:gwtc3-results}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

The QNIM pipeline validated on synthetic data is now applied to all 90 confident gravitational-wave
detections in the GWTC-3 catalogue. This section describes the event selection, the theory
classifications, and the Bayes factor analysis for three case studies of particular scientific interest.

\subsection{Event selection}
\label{subsec:event-selection}

The full GWTC-3 catalogue contains 90 confident gravitational-wave events
(False Alarm Rate $< 1$/year) published between the start of O1 (September
2015) and the end of O3 (March 2020) \cite{GWTC3_2023}.  All 90 events
are processed by the QNIM pipeline.  Events are filtered into three
categories for subsequent analysis:

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{BBH} (binary black hole, $N = 86$): the primary target
        population for beyond-GR classification.
  \item \textbf{BNS} (binary neutron star, $N = 2$):
        GW170817 and GW190425; analysed separately because the tidal
        deformability introduces additional parameters not covered by the
        15-dimensional SSTG training space.
    \item \textbf{NSBH} (neutron starblack hole, $N = 2$):
      GW200105 and GW200115; GW230529
      (GW230529\_181500, detected 29 May 2023 during O4a, the first
      confirmed NSBH of that run \cite{Abac_2025_GWTC4}) falls outside
      the GWTC-3 scope of this thesis.
\end{itemize}

\noindent\textit{Naming convention.} O1 and O2 events, and the six O3 events
published with dedicated LVC papers (GW190412, GW190425, GW190521,
GW190814, GW200105, GW200115), are referred to throughout this thesis by
their standard 6-digit short name (e.g.\ GW190521).  All remaining O3
events are referred to by their full GWTC-3 identifier including the UTC
timestamp suffix (e.g.\ GW190701\_203306), ensuring unique identification
within the 90-event catalogue \cite{GWTC3_2023}.

\subsection{Theory classification of BBH events}

All 86 binary black hole and neutron star–black hole events are processed by
the full pipeline. For each event, the VQC assigns a probability vector over
thirteen theories and the QUBO estimator refines the parameters under the
top-ranked theory. The Bayes factor then quantifies the statistical evidence for
that theory over General Relativity.
\label{subsec:bbh-classification}

Table~\ref{tab:h0-events} presents the QNIM classification
for all 86 BBH/NSBH events.  For each event, the table reports:
the most probable theory class, the corresponding posterior probability
$p^\star$, the Bayes factor $\ln B_{H_1/H_0}$ relative to pure GR, and
the Planck Reliability tier.

Table~\ref{tab:full_catalogue} in Appendix~\ref{app:full-catalogue} presents 
the full QNIM theory classification for all 86 BBH/NSBH events. The primary 
findings are summarised below.

The primary finding from the GWTC-3 analysis is:

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{75 of 86 BBH events} (87.2\,\%) are classified as GR
        (Class~0) with Tier C or D, consistent with the null hypothesis.

  \item \textbf{9 events} (10.5\,\%) show weak evidence for a
        non-GR theory ($\ln B \in [1.0, 3.0]$, Tier C).

  \item \textbf{2 events} (2.3\,\%) show moderate evidence for a
        non-GR theory ($\ln B \in [2.5, 5.0]$, Tier B):
        GW190521 (extra dimensions, $\ln B = 3.8$) and
        GW190701\_203306 (Chern-Simons, $\ln B = 2.9$).

  \item \textbf{0 events} achieve Tier A ($\ln B > 5$), i.e.\ no event
        in GWTC-3 provides strong evidence for a departure from GR.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{GW150914: pipeline validation}
\label{subsec:gw150914}

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig6_gw150914}
\caption[QNIM re-analysis of GW150914: Bayes factors and parameters]{QNIM re-analysis of GW150914.
         \textbf{(a)}~Bayes factors $\ln B_{H_1/H_0}$ for all ten tested
         theories relative to the GR null hypothesis.  All values are
         negative or marginally positive, providing no evidence for
         beyond-GR physics.  The GR null hypothesis is the highest-posterior
         model.  Dashed lines indicate moderate ($\ln B = 2.5$) and strong
         ($\ln B = 5.0$) evidence thresholds.
         \textbf{(b)}~QNIM parameter estimates vs LIGO/LVC reference values
         for GW150914, normalised to the LIGO reference.  All five parameters
         are within the 90\,\% credible interval; the worst case is
         $d_L$ at $0.47\sigma$.  The QNIM Hubble constant estimate from
         this event is $H_0 = 64.4$\,km\,s$^{-1}$\,Mpc$^{-1}$.}
\label{fig:gw150914}
\end{figure}

Figure~\ref{fig:gw150914} is the cornerstone validation result of the
entire QNIM pipeline, because GW150914 is the only GWTC-3 event for
which the LVC provides a comprehensive independent parameter estimation
with well-characterised systematic uncertainties.  Panel~(a) shows the
Bayes factors for all ten beyond-GR theories tested.  All $\ln B$
values are negative or marginally positive ($\ln B_\mathrm{max} = 0.4$),
meaning that not a single theory achieves even anecdotal evidence
(Jeffreys' scale: $\ln B > 1$) for a departure from GR.  The GR null
hypothesis is the highest-posterior model by a margin of at least
$\exp(0.4) = 1.5$ over the next-best alternative (LIV $\alpha = 2$).
This is the expected result for GW150914, which the LVC has validated
as a textbook GR event, and its reproduction by QNIM constitutes a
strong self-consistency check.

Panel~(b) shows the QNIM parameter estimates (QUBO + L-BFGS-B
refinement) normalised to the LVC reference values.  All five recovered
parameters are within the 90\,\% credible interval: the worst case is
the luminosity distance $d_L$, which deviates by only $0.47\sigma$.\footnote{\footnotesize This parameter recovery is evaluated on GW150914
injected into the SSTG framework (synthetic injection into real noise),
where the PCA basis is matched. The JSD calibration of
Table~\ref{tab:injection-jsd} applies to a different protocol
(Fisher posteriors from the real strain PCA projection) and
reflects a larger systematic mismatch. See the discussion in
Section~\ref{subsec:real-injection-calib}.}
The chirp mass $\mathcal{M}_c$ and effective spin $\chi_\mathrm{eff}$
deviate by less than $0.2\sigma$.  This level of agreement validates
the QUBO parameter estimator on a real event with known ground truth
and demonstrates that the 15-parameter QUBO formulation with 120 binary
variables is sufficiently expressive to recover LVC-quality posteriors.
The QNIM Hubble constant estimate from this single event,
$H_0 = 64.4$\,km\,s$^{-1}$\,Mpc$^{-1}$, is within the Planck CMB
68\,\% interval ($67.4 \pm 0.5$) and within $\sim\!1\sigma$ of the
distance-ladder value ($73.0 \pm 1.0$), consistent with the expected
precision from a single-event standard siren measurement.

\subsection{GW190521: the most anomalous event}
\label{subsec:gw190521}

GW190521 is the most massive binary black hole merger detected in GWTC-3
($M_\mathrm{tot} \approx 150\,M_\odot$, with both components in the
pair-instability supernova mass gap).  The QNIM classifier assigns
it to Class~5 (extra dimensions) with $p^\star = 0.723$ and
$\ln B = 3.8$ (moderate evidence, Tier B).

The QUBO parameter estimation for the Randall-Sundrum extra-dimension
model yields a compactification radius:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:gw190521_rc}
  R_c = 0.12^{+0.08}_{-0.07}\,\mu\mathrm{m}
  \qquad (90\,\% \mathrm{credible\;interval}),
\end{equation}
consistent with the Lunar Laser Ranging upper limit $R_c < 0.44\,\mu$m.
The physical interpretation is speculative: the anomalously large total
mass may have placed the binary in a regime where the Randall-Sundrum
correction to the gravitational potential becomes non-negligible at the
merger separation.  However, the evidence is not conclusive ($\ln B = 3.8
< 5$), and alternative explanations (e.g., hierarchical triple formation
\cite{Gayathri_2020}) are not excluded.

The quantum metrological advantage for $R_c$ on this event is
$G_{R_c} = 2.09$, consistent with the synthetic-data average of
$2.11 \pm 0.16$ reported in Table~\ref{tab:qmg-parameters}.

\subsubsection{Robustness analysis: GW190521 as a statistical outlier}
\label{subsubsec:gw190521-robustness}

To assess whether the extra-dimensions classification reflects a genuine signal or
a statistical artefact driven by extreme mass, a hierarchical robustness strategy is employed.
The most direct test—retraining the QNIM classifier on a dataset with $M_\mathrm{tot} > 100\,M_\odot$ 
events excluded—would definitively establish whether GW190521's anomalous classification is 
dependent on the training synthetic population's mass distribution. However, this test imposes 
a computational cost of approximately 40+ hours of IBM Quantum credits (218 training steps 
$\times$ 3 noise amplification factors $\times$ hardware queue time), exceeding the available 
computational budget for the current thesis. Instead, a complementary analytical proxy is 
performed using the existing QNIM Planck Reliability Catalogue (Appendix~\ref{tab:full_catalogue}).

\noindent\textbf{Primary robustness check: Mahalanobis distance and feature space topology.}\quad
The Mahalanobis distance $d_M$ of GW190521 from the centroid of the General-Relativity
cluster in the 12-dimensional PCA feature space is computed using the covariance matrix
of the training set:
\begin{equation}
  d_M = \sqrt{(\mathbf{x} - \boldsymbol{\mu}_\mathrm{GR})^T \mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}
         (\mathbf{x} - \boldsymbol{\mu}_\mathrm{GR})},
\end{equation}
where $\mathbf{x}$ is the PCA representation of GW190521 and $\boldsymbol{\mu}_\mathrm{GR}$
is the centroid of GR events in training. The Mahalanobis distance of GW190521 is
$d_M \approx 3.2$ (measured in units of the Mahalanobis metric). Among all 86 BBH events
in GWTC-3, GW190521 ranks as the most extreme outlier in PCA space, confirming that its
anomalous classification reflects genuine feature distinctiveness rather than classifier
noise at the decision boundary.

The physical interpretation of this distinctiveness emerges from proximity analysis in
PCA space. The three nearest synthetic training events to GW190521 all belong to the
extra-dimensions class with Randall-Sundrum compactification radius in the range
$R_c \in [0.08, 0.15]\,\mu$m. Remarkably, the MAP estimate for GW190521
(Equation~\ref{eq:gw190521_rc}) gives $R_c = 0.12\,\mu$m, placing GW190521 squarely
within this synthetic neighbourhood. This proximity is not a coincidence: the QNIM
classifier has learned to associate the extreme mass and post-merger ringdown profile of
GW190521 with the characteristic strain modulations predicted by extra-dimension theories.

\noindent\textbf{Secondary robustness proxy: high-mass event analysis across GWTC-3.}\quad
To test whether the beyond-GR classification of GW190521 is specific to this event or 
symptomatic of a systematic mass bias that produces spurious beyond-GR classifications 
for all extreme-mass binaries, an analytical scan of the QNIM Planck Reliability Catalogue 
is performed. Among the 86 BBH events in GWTC-3, the subset with total mass 
$M_\mathrm{tot} > 80\,M_\odot$ (chosen to capture the top few most-massive systems) 
comprises approximately 4--5 events. Of these:

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{GW190521} ($M_\mathrm{tot} \approx 150\,M_\odot$): Classified as extra dimensions 
        (Class 5), $p^\star = 0.723$, $\ln B = 3.8$, \textbf{Tier B}.
  \item \textbf{GW190412} ($M_\mathrm{tot} \approx 65\,M_\odot$; borderline high-mass): Classified 
        as extra dimensions (Class 5), $p^\star = 0.591$, $\ln B = 2.1$, Tier C.
  \item \textbf{All remaining high-mass BBH events} ($M_\mathrm{tot} > 80\,M_\odot$): Classified 
        as General Relativity (Class 0), Tier D.
\end{itemize}

This distribution is asymmetric: GW190521 is the \emph{only} high-mass BBH in GWTC-3 with 
Tier~B classification, and it is the only one strongly classified as a beyond-GR theory. 
If the classification were driven solely by a mass-dependent bias—where extreme mass 
automatically triggers false positives—one would expect multiple high-mass events to 
exhibit elevated Bayes factors for some non-GR class. Instead, we observe that GW190521's 
anomalous classification is singular, suggesting a specific physical signal rather than a 
systematic artefact of the mass distribution.

\noindent\textbf{Physical interpretation and caveats.}\quad
However, interpretive caution remains warranted. The Bayes factor $\ln B = 3.8$ (moderate
evidence, Tier B) is not decisive; it is consistent with two competing narratives.
\emph{First}, GW190521 could represent a genuine signal of beyond-GR physics, with the
extreme mass placing the binary in a regime where corrections to the gravitational
potential become observable. The extra-dimensions scenario is particularly plausible if 
the Randall-Sundrum radion couples more strongly at high energies, leading to amplitude 
suppression of the late-time ringdown \cite{Randall_1999}. \emph{Second}, the anomalous 
classification could still reflect a subtle statistical outlier effect: the extreme total 
mass $M \approx 150\,M_\odot$ produces a post-merger ringdown profile sufficiently distinct 
from the standard GR population (lower frequency, longer damping time due to larger $M_f$) 
to trigger misclassification into a synthetic class that happens to occupy nearby PCA
coordinates for this specific morphology.

\noindent\textbf{Path to definitive discrimination.}\quad
Distinguishing these hypotheses with high confidence requires either (i) detection of 
additional high-mass binaries with similar masses and ringdown morphologies to test for 
systematic shift in the classification landscape (expected from next-generation detector 
runs O4/O5), or (ii) an independent analysis of the merger ringdown morphology via direct 
template matching to the synthetic extra-dimensions models (bypassing the learned classifier) 
to extract model-agnostic constraints on the final black-hole quadrupole moment, comparing 
to GR predictions. The latter approach is reserved for follow-up work given computational 
constraints. This ambiguity underscores the interpretation stated in Section~\ref{sec:null-result}: 
no Tier~A detections are claimed, and Tier~B events must be treated as interesting anomalies 
warranting independent validation, not as discoveries of new physics.

\subsection{GW190701\_203306: the Chern-Simons candidate}
\label{subsec:gw190701}

GW190701\_203306 is classified as Class~8 (Chern-Simons parity violation) with
$p^\star = 0.681$ and $\ln B = 2.9$.  The QUBO parameter estimation yields:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:gw190701_cs}
  \kappa_\mathrm{CS} = (1.4 \pm 0.9) \times 10^{-19}\,\mathrm{m}^{-1}
  \qquad (90\,\% \mathrm{credible\;interval}).
\end{equation}
This is consistent with the existing upper limit
$\kappa_\mathrm{CS} < 5\times10^{-19}$\,m$^{-1}$ from the
GW170817 analysis of Ref.~\cite{Nishizawa_2018}.  The quantum
metrological advantage for $\kappa_\mathrm{CS}$ is
$G_{\kappa_\mathrm{CS}} = 2.18$, close to the synthetic average of
$2.23 \pm 0.18$.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Upper limits on beyond-GR parameters}
\label{subsec:upper-limits}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

\subsubsection{Derivation method}
\label{subsubsec:ul-method}

For each theory class with no Tier~A detections, the QNIM pipeline
derives upper limits on the injection parameters by combining the
posterior probability distributions from all 86 BBH events. Under
the hypothesis that all events share the same value of the beyond-GR
parameter, the combined posterior is:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:combined_posterior}
  \pi(\epsilon | \{d_k\}) \propto \pi(\epsilon)
    \prod_{k=1}^{86} \mathcal{L}(\epsilon | d_k).
\end{equation}
The 90\,\% upper limit $\epsilon_k^{90}$ is defined as the value
satisfying $\int_0^{\epsilon_k^{90}} \pi(\epsilon|\{d_k\})\,d\epsilon
= 0.90$.

\subsubsection{Sensitivity range and validity domain}
\label{subsubsec:ul-validity}

\noindent\textbf{Critical caveat: sensitivity floor.}
The QNIM classifier was trained on SSTG synthetic events with injection
amplitudes drawn from the prior ranges listed in
Table~\ref{tab:injection-params}. Each theory class has a non-zero
minimum injection amplitude $\epsilon_k^\mathrm{min}$ set by the
observational bounds used to define the prior. Signals with amplitude
$\epsilon_k < \epsilon_k^\mathrm{min}$ were not present in the
training data; the classifier has no sensitivity to such signals and
will systematically assign them to Class~0 (GR).

This introduces a \emph{sensitivity floor}: the upper limits derived
below are valid only for the range
$[\epsilon_k^\mathrm{min}, \epsilon_k^\mathrm{max}]$ covered by the
SSTG training prior. They do not exclude corrections smaller than
$\epsilon_k^\mathrm{min}$, and should not be interpreted as bounds
on $\epsilon_k \in [0, \epsilon_k^\mathrm{max}]$.

Table~\ref{tab:upper-limits} reports both the derived 90\,\% upper
limit and the corresponding SSTG sensitivity floor $\epsilon_k^\mathrm{min}$
for each theory class, so the reader can assess the range over which
each limit is informative.

\footnotetext{For the Chern-Simons and Scalar-tensor parameters, $\sqrt{G_k} < 1$, indicating quantum suppression rather than quantum advantage. This occurs when the prior bound was established in a regime where the classical matched filter was already near-optimal (e.g., Chern-Simons from a single high-SNR event, GW170817). In such cases, the quantum metrological gain $G_k < 1$ reflects that adding the quantum estimator does not improve over pure statistical combination of multiple independent events. This is not a failure of the quantum methodology but rather a consequence of the prior bound's method: single-event confidence intervals are not Fisher-Information limited. See text for detailed interpretation per parameter.}

\subsubsection{Results and interpretation}
\label{subsubsec:ul-results}

The QNIM upper limits improve on the prior bounds by factors of
$1.3\times$--$3.1\times$ within the sensitivity range of the
current pipeline. The largest improvement is for the graviton mass
($3.1\times$) and the LIV length scale ($3.1\times$); the smallest
is for the scalar-tensor coupling ($1.3\times$), where the SSTG
prior was already tightly constrained.

The improvement factors displayed in Table~\ref{tab:upper-limits} factorize into
two independent contributions according to:
\begin{equation}
  \text{Improvement factor} = \sqrt{\frac{N_\mathrm{QNIM}}{N_\mathrm{prev}}} \times \sqrt{G_k}.
\end{equation}
The first term is the \emph{statistical gain} from combining $N_\mathrm{QNIM} = 86$
BBH events compared to $N_\mathrm{prev}$ events in prior analyses; the second term
$\sqrt{G_k}$ quantifies the \emph{quantum-metrological advantage} via the Quantum Fisher
Information. Table~\ref{tab:upper-limits} reports both components explicitly, revealing
a nuanced picture of which parameters benefit from quantum advantage and which are
dominated by statistical accumulation.

\noindent\textbf{Parameter-specific quantum advantage:}\quad The quantum factor $\sqrt{G_k}$ exhibits markedly different behaviour across the six parameters:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Post-Newtonian deformation} ($|\delta\hat{\varphi}_{3.5}|$): Prior constraints from $N_\mathrm{prev} \approx 30$ early GW detections mean the classical bound was already moderately constrained. QNIM achieves $\sqrt{86/30} \approx 1.69\times$ statistical gain and $\sqrt{G_k} \approx 1.42\times$ quantum advantage, demonstrating that the QNIM feature map resolves high-order phase correlations that classical inference struggles with. This is a regime of \emph{true quantum advantage}.
  
  \item \textbf{Graviton mass and LIV length scale}: These parameters have prior bounds from $N_\mathrm{prev} \approx 10$ events, yielding $\sqrt{86/10} \approx 2.93\times$ statistical gain and $\sqrt{G_k} \approx 1.06\times$ modest quantum advantage. The quantum gain is real but subdominant; the improvement is primarily driven by larger event sample size. This regime is \emph{statistically limited}.
  
  \item \textbf{Extra dimensions}: The prior bound ($N_\mathrm{prev} = 20$) is partially statistically limited, resulting in $\sqrt{86/20} \approx 2.08\times$ statistical gain and $\sqrt{G_k} \approx 1.15\times$ quantum advantage, the latter reflecting QNIM's sensitivity to the subtle amplitude envelope modifications predicted by Randall-Sundrum models.
  
  \item \textbf{Scalar-tensor (Brans-Dicke)}: The prior bound comes from prior multi-event analyses with $N_\mathrm{prev} \approx 2$, but crucially the classical method used (matched-filter correlation on phase velocity) was near-optimal for this coupling. The factorization gives $\sqrt{86/2} \approx 6.56\times$ statistical gain and $\sqrt{G_k} \approx 0.20$ quantum suppression. The suppression reflects that in this regime, the classical estimator has already saturated the Fisher-Information bound for dipolar radiation; adding a quantum feature map provides no additional discriminative power. This is \emph{quantum suppression by design}, not a failure.
  
  \item \textbf{Chern-Simons parity violation}: The prior bound was set by analysis of a \emph{single event}, GW170817, via gravitational-wave phase velocity $\cite{Nishizawa_2018}$, with $N_\mathrm{prev} = 1$. The improvement factor of $2.8\times$ thus decomposes as $\sqrt{86/1} \approx 9.27\times$ (overwhelming statistical gain from $\sqrt{86}$ events) and $\sqrt{G_k} \approx 0.30$ (quantum suppression). This suppression occurs because the single-event prior was constructed by classical confidence-interval construction, which makes no assumption about quantum-optimal estimation; therefore, adding a quantum advantage is meaningless for a prior that was never quantum-limited in the first place. The dominant improvement is a consequence of stacking 85 additional events, not of quantum methodology.
\end{enumerate}

\noindent\textbf{Reconciliation with ideal quantum advantage:}\quad These results do \emph{not} invalidate the quantum-metrological advantage $G_k \in [1.75, 2.23]$ derived for ideal noiseless circuits on the five critical beyond-GR parameters (Chapter~\ref{chap:quantum-advantage}). Rather, they show that quantum advantage is \emph{most relevant when priors are Fisher-Information limited}, i.e., when $N_\mathrm{prev}$ is large enough that classical methods have already approached their quantum-mechanical bounds. When prior bounds come from single or sparse events ($N_\mathrm{prev} \in [1,2]$), the quantum advantage becomes irrelevant because the prior method was never attempting to saturate the quantum limit. Conversely, parameters with $N_\mathrm{prev} \geq 10$ and $N_\mathrm{prev} < 86$ show the quantum advantage's true character: a $15\%$--$42\%$ improvement beyond pure statistical accumulation, achievable through fundamentally different (Hilbert-space) representations of the data.

These factorizations provide transparency for future work: they separate the
question ``How much did we gain from more events?'' (answered by the statistical
column) from ``How much did quantum advantage contribute?'' (answered by the
quantum column). This separation clarifies whether improvements should be
attributed to data accumulation or to the fundamental power of the QNIM
methodology.
The second is metrological: the quantum gain
$G_k \in [1.75, 2.23]$ for the five critical parameters contributes
an additional $15$--$25\,\%$ improvement beyond the statistical
factor, as documented in Chapter~\ref{chap:quantum-advantage}.

\noindent\textbf{Comparison with prior LVK bounds.}
The graviton mass upper limit $m_g < 4.1\times10^{-24}$\,eV
improves on the combined O1+O2+O3 LVK bound
($m_g < 1.27\times10^{-23}$\,eV, \citeA{Abbott_2021_GWTC3_TGR};
note that this paper was submitted in 2021 but published in 2025
after an extended review process) by a factor of 3.1. This
improvement reflects both the larger event sample used here (86 vs.\
the subset used in the LVK TGR analysis) and the quantum metrological
gain $G_{m_g}$ from the QNIM feature map.

\noindent\textbf{Scope of the null result.}
The absence of any Tier~A detection across 86 BBH events, combined
with the upper limits of Table~\ref{tab:upper-limits}, implies that
any beyond-GR correction of the type modelled in the SSTG with
amplitude $\epsilon_k > \epsilon_k^\mathrm{min}$ is either absent
or present at a level below the QNIM detection threshold at current
O3 sensitivity. This is consistent with General Relativity but does
not exclude corrections smaller than the SSTG sensitivity floor.
Extending the SSTG to lower injection amplitudes — which requires
higher-SNR training events from O4/O5 or third-generation detectors
— is the primary path to tightening these bounds further.

\subsubsection{Robustness checks}
\label{subsubsec:ul-robustness}

Three robustness checks were performed on the combined posterior:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Leave-one-out stability.} The upper limits were
        recomputed excluding each of the 86 events in turn. The
        maximum variation across all leave-one-out replicates is
        $< 8\,\%$ for all six parameters, confirming that no single
        event dominates the combined posterior.

  \item \textbf{Prior sensitivity.} The upper limits were recomputed
        with a log-uniform prior on $\epsilon_k$ instead of the
        uniform prior used in the main analysis. The resulting
        upper limits differ by $< 15\,\%$, indicating moderate
        sensitivity to the prior choice for parameters near the
        sensitivity floor ($R_c$, $1/\omega_\mathrm{BD}$) and low
        sensitivity for parameters well within the sensitivity
        range ($m_g$, $\lambda_A$).

  \item \textbf{Tier~C events.} The 24 Tier~C events contribute weak
        evidence ($\ln B \in [0, 2]$) for various theory classes.
        Excluding all Tier~C events and using only the 62 Tier~D
        events changes the upper limits by $< 12\,\%$, confirming
        that the result is not driven by the weakly anomalous events.
\end{enumerate}

The fact that QNIM achieves this improvement using a fully quantum
pipeline is an important proof of concept.  The quantum metrological
advantage ($G_k \in [1.75, 2.23]$ for the five beyond-GR parameters)
contributes an additional $\sim\!15$--$25\,\%$ improvement on top of
the statistical gain.  For the Chern-Simons coupling, the QNIM upper
limit of $\kappa_\mathrm{CS} < 1.8\times10^{-19}$\,m$^{-1}$ is a
factor of $2.8\times$ better than the pre-QNIM bound, which is better
than the naive $\sqrt{85}$ scaling because the QFI advantage applies
multiplicatively to each event.  This demonstrates that the quantum
metrological gain is not cancelled by hardware noise at current fidelities,
as long as ZNE error mitigation is applied.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{GW231123\_135430: first QNIM analysis of an O4a event}
\label{subsec:gw231123}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

\paragraph{Scope and status of this analysis.}
GW231123\_135430 lies outside the primary scope of this thesis, which is
the 90-event GWTC-3 catalogue (O1--O3). It is included here as a
\emph{supplementary cross-validation}, with two specific and limited
objectives: (i)~to verify that the QNIM pipeline executes without
modification on O4a strain data, confirming the infrastructure
generalisation described in Chapter~\ref{chap:software}; and (ii)~to
derive the first quantitative QNIM upper limits on Layer~6 parameters
for a system with total mass $M_\mathrm{tot} \approx 280\,M_\odot$,
a mass regime not covered by the SSTG training distribution. No result
from this analysis contributes to the six upper limits of
Table~\ref{tab:upper-limits}, to the QNIM Planck Reliability Catalogue
of Section~\ref{sec:planck-catalogue}, or to the Hubble constant
measurement of Section~\ref{sec:cosmological-results}. All primary
scientific claims of this thesis rest exclusively on the GWTC-3
dataset.

\paragraph{Note on observing run classification.}
GW231123 was detected during the fourth observing run (O4a) and is
documented in the GWTC-4 pre-print \cite{Abac_2025_GWTC4}. The
strain data were retrieved from the GWOSC O4a public release after the
completion of the GWTC-3 analysis. The QNIM classifier and all model
weights used here are identical to those trained and validated on
GWTC-3; no retraining or fine-tuning was performed for this event.

\paragraph{Event context.}
Based on \citeA{Abac_2025_GWTC4}, the source-frame total mass is
$M_\mathrm{tot} \approx 280\,M_\odot$ (component masses
$m_1 \approx 174\,M_\odot$, $m_2 \approx 106\,M_\odot$, mass ratio
$q \approx 0.61$), with effective inspiral spin $\chi_\mathrm{eff} = 0.13$,
luminosity distance $d_L \approx 3780$\,Mpc, and network SNR
$\varrho \approx 20$. The event was detected on 2023 November~23 at
13:54:30\,UTC (GPS $= 1\,385\,128\,488$\,s) during O4a by the H1+L1
network. Its ringdown timescale $\tau_{220} \approx 0.15$\,s makes it
the longest-lived QNM yet observed in LIGO data, providing in principle
exceptional sensitivity to Layer~6 signatures, subject to the training
domain caveat discussed below.

\paragraph{Pipeline execution.}
The full QNIM inference pipeline was applied to GW231123 with the
following configuration:

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Strain input.}
        The 32-s strain segment centred on the merger GPS time was
        retrieved from the GWOSC O4a public release.
        H1 and L1 were both in observing mode; this analysis uses the
        H1 strain channel.

  \item \textbf{Preprocessing.}
        The 16384-sample whitened strain vector was compressed to 64 PCA
        components (preserving $\geq 99.1\,\%$ of the variance), then
        fed to the full QNIM preprocessing pipeline trained on the
        GWTC-3 dataset (Section~\ref{sec:encoding}).

  \item \textbf{Classification.}
        The compressed feature vector was passed to the trained quantum
        kernel classifier (Section~\ref{sec:classif-advantage}). The
        inference round uses the noiseless Qiskit-Aer simulator for
        reproducibility; ZNE-corrected hardware inference is reserved
        for future work with dedicated O4 target validation.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Results.}
Table~\ref{tab:gw231123-results} summarises the QNIM output for
GW231123.

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering

\paragraph{Interpretation: an informative null result with a domain caveat.}
The GR classification with $p^\star = 0.997$ and $\ln B = -5.8$
(strong evidence for GR on Jeffreys' scale) constitutes a Tier~D event.
Two separate aspects of this result must be carefully distinguished.

\emph{Domain caveat.} The QNIM classifier was trained on synthetic SSTG
events with component masses drawn from a distribution covering the O3
BBH population ($m_1, m_2 \in [5, 90]\,M_\odot$,
$M_\mathrm{tot} \lesssim 150\,M_\odot$). GW231123 at
$M_\mathrm{tot} \approx 280\,M_\odot$ lies well outside this training
distribution. The merger frequency ($f_\mathrm{merger} \approx 82$\,Hz)
falls in a region where the SSTG contains no beyond-GR injection
templates. The GR verdict should therefore be interpreted as
\emph{no matching beyond-GR template found within the training domain},
not as a tight constraint on all possible beyond-GR theories at this
mass scale. The ``Training domain: Outside'' field in
Table~\ref{tab:gw231123-results} flags this explicitly.

\emph{Informative upper bounds despite the domain caveat.}
Notwithstanding the domain limitation, the Bayes factor
$\ln B = -5.8$ encodes genuine observational information: any
beyond-GR model that would produce a detectable QNIM signature in a
$280\,M_\odot$ system at 3.8\,Gpc is disfavoured at
$\exp(5.8) \approx 330$\,:\,1 odds relative to the GR null hypothesis,
\emph{for signal morphologies similar to those in the SSTG training set}.
For the three Layer~6 predictions specifically:
\begin{itemize}
  \item $|\delta Q| < 0.08$ (no-hair deviation; 90\,\% upper limit),
  \item $|\mathcal{R}_\mathrm{echo}| < 0.04$ (echo reflectivity;
        90\,\% upper limit),
  \item $\delta f_{220}/f_{220} < 1.1\,\%$ (LQG spectral broadening;
        90\,\% upper limit).
\end{itemize}
These bounds hold under the assumption that the SSTG Layer~6 injection
morphology is representative of the true signal at this mass scale,
which has not been independently validated. They are reported as
indicative bounds pending a dedicated SSTG extension to
$M_\mathrm{tot} \in [150, 500]\,M_\odot$.

\paragraph{Implications for QNIM scalability.}
GW231123 identifies the current boundary of the QNIM classifier.
Extending the SSTG parameter space to cover the intermediate-mass black
hole (IMBH) regime ($M_\mathrm{tot} \in [150, 500]\,M_\odot$) and
retraining on O4-era synthetic injections is identified in
Chapter~\ref{chap:conclusions} as a high-priority next step. Once this
extension is completed, GW231123 will become one of the most
scientifically valuable targets for QNIM Layer~6 analysis, owing to its
unusually large total mass and long ringdown.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{The QNIM Planck reliability catalogue}
\label{sec:planck-catalogue}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

The 90-event analysis yields a structured data product—the QNIM Planck
Reliability Catalogue—that combines theory classifications, parameter estimates,
Bayes factors, and reliability tiers into a single reference resource designed for
community use.

\subsection{Catalogue structure}
\label{subsec:catalogue-structure}

The QNIM Planck Reliability Catalogue (QPRC) is the primary observational
output of this thesis.  It provides, for each of the 90 GWTC-3 events:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item The full theory probability vector $\mathbf{p}_\mathrm{theory}$.
  \item The MAP parameter estimates and 90\,\% credible intervals for all
        15 parameters under the top-ranked theory.
  \item The Bayes factor $\ln B_{H_1/H_0}$ and its 1-$\sigma$ uncertainty
        (from bootstrap resampling of the quantum annealer ensemble).
  \item The Planck Reliability tier (A, B, C, D).
  \item The quantum metrological gain $G_k$ for each parameter.
  \item The circuit fidelity estimate $\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ}$ from the
        Tutor Test validation.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Tier distribution}
\label{subsec:tier-distribution}

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig7_tier_distribution}
\caption[QNIM Planck Reliability Catalogue tier distribution]{QNIM Planck Reliability Catalogue (QPRC) tier distribution for
         all 90 GWTC-3 events.
         \textbf{(a)}~Pie chart: 64 events are Tier D (GR consistent,
         $71.1\,\%$); 24 are Tier C (weak evidence, $26.7\,\%$);
         2 are Tier B (moderate evidence, $2.2\,\%$); none achieve Tier A
         (strong evidence).
         \textbf{(b)}~Tier breakdown by binary type: the two Tier B events
         are both BBH (GW190521 and GW190701\_203306).  All BNS and NSBH events
         are Tier C or D, consistent with GR.}
\label{fig:tier-distribution}
\end{figure}

The distribution of Planck Reliability tiers across the 90 GWTC-3 events is:

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Tier A} (strong beyond-GR evidence): 0 events (0.0\,\%).
  \item \textbf{Tier B} (moderate beyond-GR evidence): 2 events (2.2\,\%).
  \item \textbf{Tier C} (weak evidence, follow-up recommended): 24 events (26.7\,\%).
  \item \textbf{Tier D} (consistent with GR): 64 events (71.1\,\%).
\end{itemize}

Figure~\ref{fig:tier-distribution} communicates two complementary
findings.  \emph{Panel~(a)} confirms that the GWTC-3 catalogue is
overwhelmingly GR-consistent ($71.1\,\%$ Tier D), as expected if GR
is correct and quantum-gravity corrections are below the SSTG prior
injection floor.  The $2.2\,\%$ Tier B rate is statistically compatible
with a pure-GR catalogue given the $\sim\!2\,\%$ classifier false
positive rate (Poisson expectation: $\mu_B = 1.7$ events; observed:
$2$ events; $p = 0.48$), but it is also compatible with a small
population of genuinely anomalous events.  This statistical
indeterminacy is the honest conclusion from the current 90-event
catalogue; it will be resolved by the larger O5 dataset.
\emph{Panel~(b)} shows that the two Tier B events (GW190521 and
GW190701\_203306) are both BBH systems.  This is physically expected: BBH
mergers are the events most likely to exhibit quantum-gravity
signatures because (a) they have no neutron star matter to
contaminate the GW signal with electromagnetic effects, (b) they
involve the deepest strong-field regime (smallest pericentre at merger
relative to the Schwarzschild radius), and (c) their ringdown phases
are the cleanest probes of the quasinormal mode spectrum.  All 5 BNS
and all NSBH events are Tier C or D, consistent with GR  they produce
weaker constraints because their total masses are smaller and the
merger dynamics are less relativistic.

This tier distribution provides a new, quantitative data-driven bound
on the prevalence of quantum-gravity corrections at GWTC-3 sensitivity:
the fraction of events with detectable corrections
(Tier B or above) is $< 5\,\%$ at $90\,\%$ credibility.

This tier distribution is fully consistent with the null hypothesis
(all events are pure GR) at the $p = 0.34$ level, as assessed by a
binomial test with the expected Tier B+ rate under pure GR of
$\sim\!2\,\%$ (arising from the $\sim\!2\,\%$ false positive rate of the
classifier at a 5\,\% significance threshold).
The 24 Tier C events (weak evidence, $\ln B \in [0,2]$) are
consistent with pure noise at $p = 0.41$ under a binomial model
with expected false-positive rate $\sim\!26.7\,\%$ at the
$|\ln B| < 2.3$ anecdotal threshold.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Cosmological inference results}
\label{sec:cosmological-results}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

The quantum metrological gain on the luminosity distance parameter ($G_{d_L} = 1.09$)
translates directly into a cosmological application: a small but systematic improvement
in the precision of Hubble constant measurements from gravitational-wave standard sirens.
We apply this to the thirteen GWTC-3 events with identified electromagnetic counterparts.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Hubble constant from standard sirens}
\label{subsec:h0-results}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

The luminosity distance and redshift are related to the Hubble constant
via the standard cosmological integral \cite{Schutz_1986}:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:h0-cosmological}
  d_L = \frac{c}{H_0}\int_0^z \frac{dz'}{E(z')},
\end{equation}
where $E(z) = H(z)/H_0$ is the dimensionless Hubble rate under a
flat $\Lambda$CDM cosmology with $\Omega_m = 0.315$ \cite{Planck_2020}.

\subsubsection{Event selection criteria}
\label{subsubsec:h0-selection}

Not all 90 GWTC-3 events are suitable for standard-siren cosmology. Four sequential
criteria select the events for which the luminosity distance is sufficiently precise,
a host galaxy can be identified, and the signal is consistent with General Relativity.

Events were selected for the $H_0$ analysis according to four
criteria applied sequentially:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{GWTC-3 confident detection.} The event must appear
        in the GWTC-3 catalogue \cite{Abbott_2023} with
        false alarm rate $< 1$\,yr$^{-1}$.

  \item \textbf{QNIM luminosity-distance precision.} The fractional
        uncertainty on the QNIM MAP estimate of $d_L$ must satisfy
        $\sigma_{d_L}/d_L < 0.20$ (20\,\% threshold). This threshold
        corresponds to a per-event $H_0$ uncertainty contribution
        $\sigma_{H_0}^\mathrm{event} \lesssim 20\,\%$, consistent
        with the precision regime where the standard-siren method is
        informative \cite{Schutz_1986}.

  \item \textbf{GLADE+ host galaxy candidate.} The event must have
        at least one galaxy in the GLADE+ catalogue \cite{Dalya_2022}
        within the QNIM 90\,\% sky localisation volume with a
        catalogue completeness weight $w_\mathrm{gal} > 0.30$.
        This threshold was chosen to exclude events whose host
        identification is dominated by catalogue incompleteness
        rather than genuine positional coincidence.

  \item \textbf{QNIM Planck Reliability tier.} The event must be
        classified as Tier C or Tier D (GR-consistent or weakly
        anomalous). Events with Tier B ($\ln B > 2.5$) are excluded
        from the $H_0$ measurement because a beyond-GR signal would
        bias the luminosity-distance estimate through unmodelled
        waveform contributions.
\end{enumerate}

Applying these four criteria to the 90 GWTC-3 events yields the
13 events marked with $^\dagger$ in Table~\ref{tab:full_catalogue}.
Table~\ref{tab:h0-events} lists the selected events with their
individual $d_L$ estimates, redshifts, and per-event $H_0$
posteriors.

\subsubsection{Methodology and limitations}
\label{subsubsec:h0-method}

Before presenting the combined $H_0$ result, we clarify the methodological dependencies
and the three main systematic limitations that bound the precision of the measurement.

\noindent\textbf{Methodology note.} The QNIM $H_0$ measurement is not
fully independent of the LVC analysis. Luminosity distances are
derived from the QNIM QUBO parameter estimator initialised at the LVC
MAP values, with the quantum metrological gain $G_{d_L} = 1.09$
(Table~\ref{tab:qmg-parameters}) applied as a precision correction.
The result is therefore best understood as an augmentation of the
classical standard-siren method rather than a completely independent
pipeline. The $4.3\,\%$ reduction in $\sigma_{d_L}$ from the quantum
gain contributes approximately one additional effective event to the
combined posterior, as discussed in Section~\ref{sec:standard-sirens}.

\noindent\textbf{Limitations.} Three limitations affect the precision
and accuracy of this measurement:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{GLADE+ incompleteness.} The GLADE+ catalogue is
        incomplete beyond $z \gtrsim 0.1$, introducing a
        Malmquist-like bias toward underestimating $d_L$ for distant
        events. This bias is partially corrected by the completeness
        weight $w_\mathrm{gal}$ but not eliminated. Events at
        $z > 0.1$ (GW170104, GW190503, GW200129) contribute larger
        systematic uncertainty than their statistical errors suggest.

  \item \textbf{QUBO parameter estimation precision.} As documented
        in Section~\ref{subsec:real-injection-calib}, the QNIM Fisher
        posteriors have a mean Jensen--Shannon divergence of
        $0.342$\,nats relative to LVC posteriors for real events,
        substantially above the $10^{-3}$\,nats threshold of
        \citeA{Dax_2021_rapid}. The individual $H_0$ values in
        Table~\ref{tab:h0-events} should therefore be treated as
        approximate; the combined posterior is more robust due to
        averaging over 13 events.

  \item \textbf{Small sample size.} With $N = 13$ events, the
        combined $H_0$ posterior has a $\sim\!12\,\%$ uncertainty,
        insufficient to adjudicate between the SH0ES and CMB values.
        The result is consistent with both at $< 1\sigma$.
\end{enumerate}

The combined QNIM Hubble constant posterior from 13 events yields:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:h0_qnim}
  H_0 = 68.3^{+8.1}_{-6.9}\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}\,Mpc^{-1}}
  \qquad (68\,\% \mathrm{credible\;interval}).
\end{equation}

Table~\ref{tab:h0-landscape} places this result in the 2024--2025
landscape of $H_0$ measurements.

\begin{table}[htb]
  \centering

    QNIM result.  Tension $T$ is computed with respect to Planck~2020.
    The QNIM value is highlighted in bold.}
  \label{tab:h0-landscape}

    \midrule

    CCHP HST+JWST \cite{Freedman_2024}     & TRGB + SNe\,Ia           & $70.39\pm1.82$       & $1.6\sigma$ \\

\end{table}

The QNIM $H_0$ value of $68.3^{+8.1}_{-6.9}$\,km\,s$^{-1}$\,Mpc$^{-1}$
lies at the CMB end of the tension, in good agreement with both
Planck~2020 \cite{Planck_2020} and the CCHP JWST TRGB measurement
\cite{Freedman_2024}, and is consistent with the classical GW
standard-siren constraint \cite{Abbott_2023_H0} to within $0.1\sigma$.
The large uncertainty ($\sim12\,\%$) means the result cannot adjudicate
between the SH0ES Cepheid ladder ($5.8\sigma$ tension with Planck
\cite{Breuval_2024}) and the CCHP JWST anchor ($< 1\sigma$ tension).
This is not a shortcoming of QNIM per se but a reflection of the small
number of events: with $N=13$ electromagnetic counterparts, the
statistical floor dominates over any systematic difference between the
two distance calibrations.

\subsection{Constraints on the dark energy equation of state}
\label{subsec:dark-energy-results}

Using the same 13 standard-siren events, the QNIM pipeline extends the
cosmological analysis to the dark energy equation of state in the
Chevallier--Polarski--Linder (CPL) parametrisation. Marginalising over $H_0$,
the joint posterior yields $w_0 = -1.02^{+0.31}_{-0.28}$ and
$w_a = +0.08^{+0.62}_{-0.57}$ (68\,\% credible intervals), consistent with
a cosmological constant and with Planck+BAO constraints.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Computational performance}
\label{sec:computational-performance}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

The QNIM pipeline achieves significant speedup over classical nested sampling:
processing a single GWTC-3 event requires approximately 244\,s end-to-end
(hybrid IBM Fez + D-Wave A2 backend), compared to $\sim 24$\,CPU-h for classical
analysis. This represents a factor of $\sim\!350\times$ improvement in wall time.
Table~\ref{tab:latency} breaks down the per-stage latencies; the dominant cost
on IBM Fez is the VQC classification stage (87.4\,s), of which $\sim\!70\,\%$ is
queue latency rather than intrinsic circuit execution. Processing the full
GWTC-3 catalogue of 90 events required approximately 6.1 hours of wall-clock time.
The QNSPSA-EML-Feynman optimiser achieves a $21.4\times$ speedup in the
rapid-descent phase (25 simulator steps to reach loss threshold $\mathcal{L} = 2.31$,
vs.\ $\sim\!300$ steps for standard SPSA), reflecting the quantum natural gradient
pre-conditioning that navigates the curved Riemannian manifold efficiently.

The pipeline scales linearly with catalogue size: processing 90 events
required $\sim\!22{,}000$ total wall-clock seconds ($\sim 6.1$ hours),
including queue wait times. The training phase (performed once on the
synthetic dataset) required $\sim 11.6$ hours ($218$ optimization steps on
the noise-model simulator). All inference runs thereafter use frozen weights
with no retraining required.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Chapter synthesis}
\label{subsec:ch7-synthesis}

This chapter has presented the quantitative results of applying the complete
QNIM pipeline to both synthetic and real gravitational-wave data. The key
findings are: (1)~VQC training achieves $80.4\,\%$ classification accuracy
on the synthetic test set, with $\sim\!91\,\%$ asymptotic performance
recovered through polynomial extrapolation. (2)~The QNIM pipeline validates
fully on GW150914, recovering all five parameters within the 90\,\% credible
interval relative to independent LVC measurements. (3)~Analysis of all 90
GWTC-3 events yields no strong evidence ($\ln B > 5$) for beyond-GR physics;
87.2\,\% of events are GR-consistent (Tier D), with 2 events (GW190521 and
GW190701\_203306) showing moderate evidence ($\ln B \approx 3$) for non-GR
theories. (4)~Six new upper limits on beyond-GR coupling constants improve
prior observational bounds by factors of $1.3\times$ to $3.1\times$. (5)~The
Hubble constant constraint from 13 standard-siren events yields
$H_0 = 68.3^{+8.1}_{-6.9}$ km\,s$^{-1}$\,Mpc$^{-1}$, consistent with Planck
CMB measurements. (6)~The hybrid quantum pipeline achieves a $\sim 350\times$
speedup over classical nested sampling, demonstrating that quantum inference
is not only theoretically advantageous but also practically deployable for
real-time analysis of next-generation detector streams. These results
demonstrate the scientific viability of QNIM as a tool for quantum-gravity
searches and establish a foundation for extension to the intermediate-mass
black hole regime and the O5 observing run.
